Quantum-classical correspondence and obstruction to dispersion on the Engel group
Lino Benedetto

Abstract
. In this paper, we develop the semiclassical analysis of the lowest dimensional simply connected nilpotent Lie group of step 3, called the Engel group and denoted by 𝔼 {\mathbb{E}} , in the continuity of works for groups of step 2 realized in [ 16 ] and [ 18 ] , while exhibiting new features specific to groups of higher step. We are interested in the propagation of the semiclassical measures associated to solutions of the Schrödinger equation for the canonical subLaplacian Δ 𝔼 \Delta_{\mathbb{E}} at different time-scales τ ∈ ℝ > 0 + \tau\in{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}^{+} . In particular, for τ = 1 \tau=1 we recover a quantum-classical correspondence where we observe the fundamental role of abnormal extremal lifts of the Engel group and are able to discuss the speed of the propagation of the singularities. Furthermore, in order to understand the dispersive nature of the subLaplacian, we are led to develop a second-microlocal analysis on particular cones in our phase space. This is done by relating the quasi-contact structure of the group 𝔼 {\mathbb{E}} to its semiclassical analysis via harmonic analysis. As a consequence, we are able to prove obstruction to local smoothness-type estimates and Strichartz estimates for the Engel subLaplacian.



1.
Introduction

1.1.
The Engel group and its subLaplacian

We consider the lowest dimensional connected and simply connected nilpotent Lie group of step 3, called the Engel group and denoted by
{\mathbb{E}}
.
Its Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}_{{\mathbb{E}}}
is the four-dimensional Lie algebra linearly spanned by the vectors
X_{1},X_{2},X_{3}
and
X_{4}

satisfying the following bracket relations:

(1.1)

[X_{1},X_{2}]=X_{3},\ [X_{1},X_{3}]=X_{4}.

The Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{E}}
is stratitified when one considers the following decomposition:

\mathfrak{g}_{{\mathbb{E}}}=\mathfrak{g}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{2}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{3},

where
\mathfrak{g}_{1}=\rm Vect(X_{1},X_{2})
,
\mathfrak{g}_{2}
is spanned by
X_{3}
and
\mathfrak{g}_{3}
is the center of the Lie algebra, spanned by
X_{4}
.
We also introduce the notation

\mathfrak{r}_{2}=\rm Vect(X_{2}).

The Engel group is, up to isomorphism, the only connected and simply connected Lie group admitting
\mathfrak{g}_{{\mathbb{E}}}
as its Lie algebra.
In what follows we endow the vector space
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{E}}
with the scalar product for which the basis
(X_{1},X_{2},X_{3},X_{4})
is orthonormal.
We thus give the Engel group
{\mathbb{E}}
the structure of a Carnot group, see
[
32
]
.

As a connected and simply connected nilpotent Lie group, the exponential map denoted by

{\rm Exp}_{{\mathbb{E}}}:\mathfrak{g}_{{\mathbb{E}}}\longrightarrow{\mathbb{E}},

defines a diffeomorphism between the Lie algebra and
{\mathbb{E}}
. The pushforward of the Lebesgue measure on
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{E}}
via
{\rm Exp}_{\mathbb{E}}
defines a bi-invariant Haar measure on
{\mathbb{E}}
that we
will simply denote by
dx
when a variable
x\in{\mathbb{E}}
is considered.

Homogeneous structure

The stratification of
\mathfrak{g}_{{\mathbb{E}}}
leads us to consider the family of dilations defined by
r\cdot X=r^{i}X
for every
X\in\mathfrak{g}_{i}
, with
i\in\{1,2,3\}
and
r>0
(see
[
21
]
,
[
22
]
).
The integers
i\in\{1,2,3\}
are called the weights and will sometimes be denoted by
\upsilon_{i}
.

The associated group dilations are defined by

r\cdot x:={\rm Exp}_{\mathbb{E}}(r\cdot X),\quad x={\rm Exp}_{\mathbb{E}}(X)\in{\mathbb{E}},\ r>0.

These families of dilations on
\mathfrak{g}_{{\mathbb{E}}}
and
{\mathbb{E}}
form one-parameter groups of automorphisms of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}_{{\mathbb{E}}}
and of the group
{\mathbb{E}}
.
The Jacobian of the dilation associated to
r>0
is
r^{Q}
where

Q:={\rm dim}\,\mathfrak{g}_{1}+2{\rm dim}\,\mathfrak{g}_{2}+3{\rm dim}\,\mathfrak{g}_{3}=7

is called the
homogeneous dimension
of
{\mathbb{E}}
.

SubLaplacian and Schrödinger equation

We are interested in the study of the following differential operator

\Delta_{{\mathbb{E}}}=X_{1}^{2}+X_{2}^{2},

called the
Engel subLaplacian
, and more precisely in the associated semiclassical Schrödinger equations

(1.2)

\begin{cases}&i\hbar^{\tau}\partial_{t}\psi^{\hbar}=-\hbar^{2}\Delta_{{\mathbb{E}}}\psi^{\hbar},\\
&\psi_{|t=0}^{\hbar}=\psi_{0}^{\hbar}\in L^{2}({\mathbb{E}}),\end{cases}

where we have introduced a small semiclassical parameter
\hbar>0
and a time-scale
\tau\in{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
. As
\Delta_{{\mathbb{E}}}
is essentially self-adjoint on
C_{0}^{\infty}({\mathbb{E}})
(see
[
21
]
),
using Stone’s theorem we have existence and unicity of the solution to (
1.2
) for any initial datum in
L^{2}({\mathbb{E}})
. We will denote by
e^{it\Delta_{{\mathbb{E}}}}
,
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, the
associated propagator.

The use of a semiclassical parameter
\hbar
can be understood as a way of measuring the scale of the oscillations of our initial data: indeed, we will often assume that the family of initial data
(\psi_{0}^{\hbar})_{\hbar>0}

is
\hbar
-oscillating
, i.e satisfies the following property:

(1.3)

\limsup_{\hbar\rightarrow 0}\|{\bm{1}}_{-\hbar^{2}\Delta_{\mathbb{E}}>R}\,\psi_{0}^{\hbar}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{E}})}\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits_{R\rightarrow+\infty}0,

meaning the oscillations are of the scale
1/\hbar
when measured with respect to the subLaplacian
\Delta_{\mathbb{E}}
. We highlight the fact that measuring oscillations of a family
(\psi_{0}^{\hbar})_{\hbar>0}

with respect to the subLaplacian is compatible with the homogeneous structure of
{\mathbb{E}}
and is thus anisotropic: we allow for higher scales of oscillations
in directions transverse to the first stratum.

Time-scales

As said before, the parameter
\tau
corresponds to a choice of time-scale. The first natural one is
\tau=1
, called the
semiclassical time-scale
, through which we recover
a quantum-classical correspondence. More precisely, we wish to find the relevant dynamic underlying the quantum evolution as
\hbar
goes to 0. For example, in the Riemannian setting and considering the Laplace-Beltrami operator,
this dynamic is the one of the geodesic flow associated to the metric. On the Engel group, we expect to observe flows related to its subRiemannian structure, about which more will be discussed below.

The other natural choice is
\tau=2
, where Equation (
1.2
) is then equivalent to the standard Schrödinger equation.
We expect this time-scale, along with assumption
1.3
on the family of initial data, to give us insights on the dispersive nature of the subLaplacian.

We will study the Schrödinger equation for both of these time-scales by the introduction of an adapted phase space different from the cotangent bundle
T^{*}G
and of operator-valued measures capturing the concentration loci of our solutions in the asymptotics

\hbar\rightarrow 0
.

1.2.
Phase space analysis on nilpotent Lie groups

Our approach relies on the introduction of a particular phase space on the Engel group based on its harmonic analysis
and on the use of an associated pseudodifferential calculus.
Such a strategy has already been implemented on several nilpotent Lie groups: one can refer to
[
16
]
,
[
17
]
for the study of the quantum evolution of the subLaplacian on H-type groups, to
[
19
]
for the case of Heisenberg nilmanifolds with a view towards observability
and to
[
18
]
for the study of quantum limits on general 2-step nilmanifolds.
For a systematic development of this approach for general graded nilpotent Lie groups, we refer to the monograph
[
21
]
and to the articles
[
15
]
,
[
6
]
. However, this article is
the first one implementing this approach for a group of step 3.

Unitary dual set of a nilpotent Lie group

We will consider the unitary dual of a nilpotent Lie group
G
, denoted by
\widehat{G}
, as the natural replacement for impulsion space.

First, recall that a
unitary representation

(\pi,\mathcal{H}_{\pi})
of a group
G
is a pair consisting in a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{\pi}
and a group morphism
\pi
from
G
to the set of unitary operators on
\mathcal{H}_{\pi}
.
In this paper, the representations will always be assumed strongly continuous, and their associated Hilbert spaces separable.
A representation is said to be
irreducible
if the only closed subspaces of
\mathcal{H}_{\pi}
that are stable under
\pi
are
\{0\}
and
\mathcal{H}_{\pi}
itself.
Two representations
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}
are equivalent if there exists a unitary transform
\mathcal{I}
called an
intertwining map
that sends
\mathcal{H}_{\pi_{1}}
on
\mathcal{H}_{\pi_{2}}
with

\pi_{1}=\mathcal{I}^{-1}\circ\pi_{2}\circ\mathcal{I}.

The
dual set

\widehat{G}
is obtained by taking the quotient of the set of irreducible representations by this equivalence relation.

Definition 1.1

(
[
12
]
)
.

The set of all irreducible unitary representations, up to equivalence, is called the unitary dual and denoted by
\widehat{G}
:

\widehat{G}:=\{\left[\pi\right]\,:\,\pi:G\rightarrow\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\pi})~\text{irreducible s.c. unitary representation of}~G\},

where
\left[\pi\right]
denotes the equivalence class of the representation
\pi
. This set is naturally endowed with a topology, called the Fell topology, and a standard Borel space structure.
Furthermore, there exists a unique (up to a scalar) canonical measure
\mu_{\widehat{G}}
on
\widehat{G}
, called the Plancherel measure.

We can then define the Fourier transform
\mathcal{F}_{G}
on the group
G
, sending integrable functions on
G
to fields of operators on the dual
\widehat{G}
acting on the representations’ spaces
\mathcal{H}_{\pi}
, see Section
2
.

Semiclassical quantization and semiclassical measures

We will work with the measured space
G\times\widehat{G}
as our natural phase space. The observables associated to this phase space are the measurable fields of operators

\sigma=\{\sigma(x,\pi)\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\pi})\,:\,(x,\pi)\in G\times\widehat{G}\}.

For a certain class of observables regular enough that we will denote by
\mathcal{A}_{0}
, we define a quantization procedure based on the Fourier transform on the group
G
turning these observables into operators on
L^{2}(G)
:

{\rm Op}_{\hbar,G}(\sigma):L^{2}(G)\rightarrow L^{2}(G),

(see Section
3
).

For a bounded family
(\psi_{0}^{\hbar})_{\hbar>0}
of functions in
L^{2}(G)
, we can consider the following quadratic quantities

\sigma\in\mathcal{A}_{0}\mapsto\left({\rm Op}_{\hbar,G}(\sigma)\psi_{0}^{\hbar},\psi_{0}^{\hbar}\right)_{L^{2}(G)}\in{\mathbb{C}},

and more precisely, the limit of such quantities as
\hbar\rightarrow 0
. Such a limit allows us to understand the concentration loci of
the family
(\psi_{0}^{\hbar})_{\hbar>0}
in phase space, for the scale of oscillations
\hbar^{-1}
.
In a similar way as in the Euclidean setting, such limits can be described by
positive measures on the phase space
G\times\widehat{G}
, or more precisely, in order to take into account the operator nature of our phase space, by operator-valued measures
\Gamma d\gamma
(see Section
3
):
such an object is the data of a positive finite measure
\gamma
on
G\times\widehat{G}
and of a field of trace-class operators
(x,\pi)\in G\times\widehat{G}\mapsto\Gamma(x,\pi)\in\mathcal{L}^{1}(\mathcal{H}_{\pi})
integrable with respect to
d\gamma
.
Indeed, up to an extraction
\hbar_{k}\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits_{k\rightarrow+\infty}0
, we can write

\left({\rm Op}_{\hbar_{k},G}(\sigma)\psi_{0}^{\hbar_{k}},\psi_{0}^{\hbar_{k}}\right)_{L^{2}(G)}\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits_{\hbar_{k}\rightarrow 0}\int_{G\times\widehat{G}}{\rm Tr}_{\mathcal{H}_{\pi}}\left(\sigma(x,\pi)\Gamma(x,\pi)\right)d\gamma(x,\pi).

1.3.
Generic representations of the Engel group and Montgomery operators

The unitary dual set
\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}
of the Engel group can be explicity computed, similarly as in any other nilpotent Lie group. We refer to Section
2
for a complete description.
We describe here only the support of the Plancherel measure, called the set of generic representations and denoted by
\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}_{\rm gen}
. It can be parametrized by two parameters

(\delta,\beta)
in
\mathfrak{g}_{3}^{*}\setminus\{0\}\times\mathfrak{r}_{2}^{*}
, and is homeomorphic to
{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\{0\}\times{\mathbb{R}}
. In the equivalence class given by
(\delta,\beta)
, we can exhibit a particular representation that we denote by
\pi^{\delta,\beta}
(see Proposition
2.3
).

As the subLaplacian
\Delta_{\mathbb{E}}
is invariant by translations of the group
{\mathbb{E}}
, it can be seen as a Fourier multiplier for the Fourier transform of the group
{\mathbb{E}}
.
For a generic representation
\pi^{\delta,\beta}\in\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}_{\rm gen}
, we introduce the following notation for the Fourier transform of
-\Delta_{{\mathbb{E}}}
.

H(\pi^{\delta,\beta})=\mathcal{F}_{{\mathbb{E}}}(-\Delta_{\mathbb{E}})(\pi^{\delta,\beta})=-\partial_{\xi}^{2}+\left(\beta+\frac{\delta}{2}\xi^{2}\right)^{2}.

Following
[
23
]
, the resolvents of these differential operators are compact and as such their spectrums are discrete:
we will denote by
\mu_{n}(\delta,\beta)
,
n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{>0}
, their eigenvalues in increasing order. We also introduce the following notation:
for
n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{>0}
, we write
\Pi_{n}
the field of eigenprojectors on
\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}_{\rm gen}
satisfying

\forall\pi^{\delta,\beta}\in\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}_{\rm gen},\ H(\pi^{\delta,\beta})\Pi_{n}(\pi^{\delta,\beta})=\mu_{n}(\delta,\beta)\Pi_{n}(\pi^{\delta,\beta}).

This family of operators can actually be rescaled to a one-parameter family of differential operators: for
\nu\in{\mathbb{R}}
, we introduce the operator

\tilde{H}(\nu):=-\partial_{\tilde{\xi}}^{2}+\left(\nu+\frac{1}{2}\tilde{\xi}^{2}\right)^{2}.

This family of differential operators, called the
Montgomery operators
, has been studied by numerous authors (see
[
23
]
,
[
24
]
,
[
30
]
) and
is related to the Fourier transform of
-\Delta_{\mathbb{E}}
in the following way: for
(\delta,\beta)\in\mathfrak{g}_{3}^{*}\setminus\{0\}\times\mathfrak{r}_{2}^{*}
, through the rescaling
\xi=\delta^{-1/3}\tilde{\xi}
,
H(\pi^{\delta,\beta})
is unitarily equivalent to the operator

\delta^{2/3}\tilde{H}(\beta\delta^{-1/3})=\delta^{2/3}\left(\partial_{\tilde{\xi}}^{2}+\left(\beta\delta^{-1/3}+\frac{1}{2}\tilde{\xi}^{2}\right)^{2}\right).

Similarly as above, we can consider the ordered discrete spectrum
\{\tilde{\mu}_{n}(\nu)\,:\,n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{>0}\}
of
\tilde{H}(\nu)
, and we deduce from the previous rescaling
the following relation between the eigenvalues of these operators: for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{>0}
,

(1.4)

\mu_{n}(\delta,\beta)=\delta^{2/3}\tilde{\mu}_{n}(\beta\delta^{-1/3}).

We observe that the family
(\tilde{H}(\nu))_{\nu\in{\mathbb{R}}}
is an analytic family of type B (see
[
26
]
). We deduce that for each
n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{>0}
,
\nu\in{\mathbb{R}}\mapsto\tilde{\mu}_{n}(\nu)
is analytic and thus
the maps
\mu_{n}
are smooth with respect to the parameters
(\delta,\beta)\in\mathfrak{g}_{3}^{*}\setminus\{0\}\times\mathfrak{r}_{2}^{*}
.

1.4.
Semiclassical time-scale and quantum-classical correspondence

Our first result concerns the propagation properties of the semiclassical measures associated to a solution

(\psi^{\hbar}(t))_{\hbar>0}
of the Schrödinger equation (
1.2
) for the choice
\tau=1
and for a bounded family of initial data
(\psi_{0}^{\hbar})_{\hbar>0}\in L^{2}({\mathbb{E}})
.
We are then able to relate the semiclassical measures of the solution at time
t
with the one of the initial condition through simple propagation law.

Theorem 1.2
.

Let
(\psi_{0}^{\hbar})_{\hbar>0}
be a bounded family in
L^{2}({\mathbb{E}})
and
\psi^{\hbar}(t)={\rm e}^{i\hbar t\Delta_{{\mathbb{E}}}}\psi^{\hbar}_{0}
the solution to (
1.2
) for
\tau=1
.
Then any time-averaged semiclassical measure

t\mapsto\Gamma_{t}d\gamma_{t}\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}_{t},{\mathcal{M}}_{ov}^{+}({\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}))
for the family
(\psi^{\hbar})_{\hbar>0}
satisfies the following additional properties:

(i)

For
d\gamma_{t}
-almost all generic representations
\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}_{\rm gen}=\{\pi^{\delta,\beta}\,:\,(\delta,\beta)\in\mathfrak{g}_{3}^{*}\setminus\{0\}\times\mathfrak{r}_{2}^{*}\}
, we have the following decomposition

\Gamma_{t}(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta})=\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{>0}}\Gamma_{n,t}(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta})\;\;{with}\;\;\Gamma_{n,t}(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta}):=\Pi_{n}(\pi^{\delta,\beta})\Gamma_{t}(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta})\Pi_{n}(\pi^{\delta,\beta})\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\pi^{\delta,\beta}}),

where the
\Pi_{n}
are the measurable symbols given by the spectral projections of
H
for the eigenvalues
\mu_{n}
,
n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{>0}
.

(ii)

For
n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{>0}
, the distribution
d\gamma_{n,t}(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta})dt={\rm Tr}\left(\Gamma_{n,t}(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta})\right)d\gamma_{t}(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta})dt
on
{\mathbb{R}}_{t}\times{\mathbb{E}}\times(\mathfrak{g}_{3}^{*}\setminus\{0\}\times\mathfrak{r}_{2}^{*})
is a weak solution of the following transport equation:

\left(\partial_{t}-\partial_{\beta}\mu_{n}(\delta,\beta)X_{2}\right)\left(d\gamma_{n,t}(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta})\right)=0.

Remark 1.3
.

The last transport equation allows us to determine the time-averaged semiclassical measure at any time
t\in{\mathbb{R}}_{t}
as one can prove
the continuity of each map
t\mapsto\gamma_{n,t}
,
n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{>0}
, valued in the space of distributions (see Proposition
3.14
in Section
3
).
The initial condition at time
t=0
is then given by any semiclassical measure of the family
(\psi_{0}^{\hbar_{k}})_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}
, where
(\hbar_{k})_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}
is the sequence
converging towards 0 associated to the time-averaged semiclassical measure
t\mapsto\Gamma_{t}d\gamma_{t}
.

1.5.
Engel manifolds, quasi-contact structure and singular curves

We introduce here the relevant material to understand the geometrical meaning of the objects we have met so far.
Let
M
denote a manifold. By a
k
-distribution we mean a smooth subbundle
\mathcal{D}\subset TM
of rank
k
. We use the notation

[\mathcal{D},\mathcal{D}]
for the sheaf generated by all Lie brackets
[X,Y]
of sections
X,Y
of the distribution
\mathcal{D}
. This way,
setting
\mathcal{D}^{1}:=\mathcal{D}
, for
j\geq 1
we set

\mathcal{D}^{j+1}:=\mathcal{D}^{j}+[\mathcal{D},\mathcal{D}^{j}].

Definition 1.4
.

A four-dimensional manifold
M
is an
Engel manifold
when it is endowed with an
Engel distribution
, meaning a
2-distribution
\mathcal{D}
with the property that
\mathcal{D}^{2}
is a distribution of rank 3 and
\mathcal{D}^{3}
is the whole tangent bundle
TM
.

The Engel group is then trivially an Engel manifold when endowed with the distribution
\mathcal{D}_{\mathbb{E}}
given by the first stratum
\mathfrak{g}_{1}
when seen as the subbundle obtained by pushforwards of translations.

The interest for Engel manifolds comes from the following result.

Proposition 1.5

(
[
32
]
)
.

Let
k
and
n
be the rank of a distribution and the dimension of the manifold on which it lives. Then the only stable distributions (in the sense of singularity theory)
occur for
(k,n)=(1,n),(n-1,n)
or
(2,4)
. Any stable regular 2-distribution on a manifold of dimension 4 defines an Engel distribution.

As this proposition shows, Engel manifolds are a particularity of dimension 4. The other stable manifolds correspond to line fields, contact manifolds and their
even-dimensional counterpart, quasi-contact manifolds. In this article, we will refer to a four dimensional manifold
M
endowed with a
non-integrable distribution of rank 3 as a quasi-contact structure.

Another interesting feature of Engel manifolds is the existence of a Darboux-like theorem.

Theorem 1.6

(Engel normal form,
[
31
]
)
.

Any two Engel manifolds are locally diffeomorphic in the following sense: for any point of an Engel manifold
(M,\mathcal{D})
, there exist a neighborhood
U\subset M
,
an open set
V\subset{\mathbb{E}}
and a diffeomorphism
\Phi:U\rightarrow V
such that

\Phi^{*}(\mathcal{D})=\mathcal{D}_{\mathbb{E}}.

Charateristic line field

A central object in the study of an Engel manifold
(M,\mathcal{D})
is its characteristic line field
L
, that one can define by the property

[L,\mathcal{D}^{2}]\equiv 0,\mod\mathcal{D}^{2}.

Any Engel manifold is then endowed with a canonical flag

L\subset\mathcal{D}\subset\mathcal{D}^{2}\subset TM.

Observe that the line field
L
is a feature of the quasi-contact manifold
(M,\mathcal{D}^{2})
rather than the Engel structure directly.
It is not difficult to check that on the Engel group
{\mathbb{E}}
, the characteristic line field corresponds to the subbundle
\mathfrak{r}_{2}
, i.e spanned by the vector field
X_{2}
.
We write

{\mathbb{R}}_{2}={\rm Exp}_{\mathbb{E}}(\mathfrak{r}_{2}),

for the associated one-dimensional subgroup.

Singular curves and comments on Theorem
1.2

One striking property of the characteristic line field is the following: any sufficiently short integral curve
c
of
L
is
a minimizing subRiemannian geodesic between its endpoints, and this holds independently of the choice of the metric put on the 2-distribution
\mathcal{D}
. Moreover, for a generic
metric,
c
is not the projection of any solution to Hamilton’s equations for the subRiemannian Hamiltonian (see
[
32
]
). The curve
c
is called
a
singular curve
on
(M,\mathcal{D})
.

Singular curves are a particularity of subRiemannian geometry. These curves admit multiple characterizations but the following microlocal one we give is
straightforward: we use the notation
\mathcal{D}^{\perp}
for the annihilator of
\mathcal{D}
(thus a subbundle of the cotangent bundle
T^{*}M
) and
\bar{\omega}
for the restriction
to
\mathcal{D}^{\perp}
of the canonical symplectic form
\omega
on
T^{*}M
.

Definition 1.7
.

A characteristic curve for
\mathcal{D}^{\perp}
is an absolutely continuous curve
\lambda(\cdot)\in\mathcal{D}^{\perp}
that never intersects the zero section and that satisfies

\dot{\lambda}(t)\in\ker(\bar{\omega}(\lambda(t)))
at every point
t
for which the derivative
\dot{\lambda}
exists.

Theorem 1.8

(
[
32
]
)
.

Let
(M,\mathcal{D})
be a subRiemannian manifold. A curve in
M
is singular if and only if it is the projection of a characteristic
\lambda
for
\mathcal{D}^{\perp}
with square-integrable derivative;
\lambda
is then called an
abnormal extremal lift of the singular curve.

In the setting of rank 2 distribution, one can show that any abnormal extremal lift is in fact a section of
\mathcal{D}^{2,\perp}\setminus\{0\}
(see
[
32
]
). On the Engel group, this would correspond to a characteristic
valued in the subbundle
\mathcal{D}_{{\mathbb{E}}}^{2,\perp}\setminus\{0\}=\mathfrak{g}_{3}^{*}\setminus\{0\}
.

We observe that the propagation of the semiclassical measures as described in Theorem
1.2
takes place along
the abnormal extremal lifts of the characteristic line field
\mathfrak{r}_{2}
that are constant-valued in
\mathfrak{g}_{3}^{*}\setminus\{0\}
.
Moreover, the speed of propagation is entirely determined and depends also of the impulsion variable
\beta\in\mathfrak{r}_{2}^{*}
. This description
contrasts with the results obtained so far concerning propagation of singularities for subelliptic operators: in
[
29
]
, it is indeed shown that for the wave
equation associated to subLaplacians on a subRiemannian manifolds, singularities (in the usual sense of wave front set) can propagate along abnormal extremal lifts but
nothing has been said in a general setting about their speed of propagation. In
[
10
]
, it has been shown that for the special case of flat Martinet distribution, which
is closely related to the Engel group, there exists a continuum of such speeds. It is remarkable that our analysis, taking advantage of the homogeneous structure of the Engel group and
by consequence fundamentally anisotropic, is able to isolate the different speeds of propagation with the natural addition of the variable
\beta\in\mathfrak{r}_{2}^{*}
.

1.6.
Time evolution of microlocally concentrated wave packets

Theorem
1.2
has shown the importance of particular abnormal extremal lifts of the singular curves in
({\mathbb{E}},\mathcal{D}_{\mathbb{E}})
for the quantum evolution. We present here particular solutions to
Equation (
1.2
) that are microlocally concentrated on such abnormal extremal lifts up to finite time. This construction is based on the notion of wave packets for Lie groups and is to be
compared with the particular solutions to the Martinet subelliptic wave equation exhibited in
[
10
]
.

Let us first briefly recall the definition of classical Euclidean wave packets. Given
(x_{0},\xi_{0})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
and
a\in\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
, we consider the family (indexed by
\hbar
) of functions

\psi_{\rm eucl}^{\hbar}(x)=\hbar^{-d/4}a\left(\frac{x-x_{0}}{\sqrt{\hbar}}\right){\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\hbar}\xi_{0}\cdot(x-x_{0})},\;\;x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}.

Such a family is called a Euclidean wave packet.

The oscillation along
\xi_{0}
is forced by the term
{\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\hbar}\xi_{0}\cdot(x-x_{0})}
and the concentration on
x_{0}
is performed at the scale
\sqrt{\hbar}
for symmetry reasons : the
\hbar
-Fourier transform of
\psi^{\hbar}_{\rm eucl}
,

\hbar^{-d/2}\widehat{\psi}^{\hbar}_{\rm eucl}(\xi/\hbar)
presents a concentration on
\xi_{0}
at the scale
\sqrt{\hbar}
.

The following definition takes inspiration from the Euclidean setting, and has already been introduced in previous articles (see
[
16
]
,
[
19
]
and
[
6
]
where a Wick-calculus on general compact
and graded nilpotent Lie groups is developed thanks to these adapted wave packets).

Definition 1.9
.

Let
a_{0}\in C^{\infty}({\mathbb{E}})
,
(x_{0},\pi^{\delta_{0},\beta_{0}})\in{\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}_{\rm gen}
and
\Phi_{1}
,
\Phi_{2}\in\mathcal{H}_{\pi^{\delta_{0},\beta_{0}}}^{\infty}
, i.e the space of smooth vectors in
\mathcal{H}_{\pi^{\delta_{0},\beta_{0}}}
.
We call a wave packet the family of maps
\left(WP_{x_{0},\pi^{\beta_{0},\delta_{0}}}^{\hbar}(a,\Phi_{1},\Phi_{2})\right)_{\hbar>0}
, defined by

\forall x\in{\mathbb{E}},\ WP_{x_{0},\pi^{\delta_{0},\beta_{0}}}^{\hbar}(a_{0},\Phi_{1},\Phi_{2})(x)=\hbar^{-Q/4}a_{0}(\hbar^{-1/2}\cdot(x_{0}^{-1}x))\left(\pi^{\delta_{0},\beta_{0}}(\hbar^{-1}\cdot(x_{0}^{-1}x))\Phi_{1},\Phi_{2}\right).

In order for a wave packet to be in
L^{2}({\mathbb{E}})
, following
[
15
]
,
[
33
]
, it is enough for the profile
a
to be square-integrable only with respect to the variables that do not belong
to the jump set
e\subset\{1,2,3,4\}
associated to the representation
\pi^{\delta_{0},\beta_{0}}
and the basis
(X_{1},X_{2},X_{3},X_{4})
. In our case,
e
is equal to the subset of indices
\{1,3\}
and for this reason, we introduce
\mathfrak{g}_{e^{c}}
the Lie algebra
spanned by
X_{2}
and
X_{4}
, and
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}_{e^{c}}={\rm Exp}_{\mathbb{E}}(\mathfrak{g}_{e^{c}})
the associated Lie subgroup. It is thus enough to take a profile
a
in
\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{R}}^{2}_{e^{c}})
for the associated wave packet to define a bounded family
in
L^{2}({\mathbb{E}})
.

Theorem 1.10
.

Let
\psi^{\hbar}(t)
be the solution to the Schrödinger equation (
1.2
) for
\tau=1
with initial data being a wave packet:

\psi_{0}^{\hbar}=WP_{x_{0},\pi^{\delta_{0},\beta_{0}}}^{\hbar}(a_{0},\Phi_{1},\Phi_{2}),

where
(x_{0},\pi^{\delta_{0},\beta_{0}})
,
\Phi_{1}
,
\Phi_{2}
as introduced in Definition
1.9
and
a_{0}\in\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{R}}^{2}_{e^{c}})
.
Suppose that
\Phi_{1}
is an eigenvector of
H(\pi^{\delta_{0},\beta_{0}})
for the
n
-th eigenvalue (
n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{>0}
):

H(\pi^{\delta_{0},\beta_{0}})\Phi_{1}=\mu_{n}(\delta_{0},\beta_{0})\Phi_{1}.

Then there exists a time-varying profile
(t,y)\mapsto a(t,y)
in
C^{1}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{R}}^{2}_{e^{c}}))
satisfying
a(0,\cdot)=a_{0}(\cdot)
and such that

\psi^{\hbar}(t)=\hbar^{-Q/4}e^{-\frac{i}{\hbar}\mu_{n}(\delta_{0},\beta_{0})t}a(\hbar^{-1/2}\cdot(x(t)^{-1}x))\left(\pi^{\delta_{0},\beta_{0}}(\hbar^{-1}\cdot(x_{0}^{-1}x))\Phi_{1},\Phi_{2}\right)+O_{L^{2}({\mathbb{E}})}(\hbar^{1/2}),

where

x(t)=x_{0}{\rm Exp}_{{\mathbb{E}}}(\partial_{\beta}\mu_{n}(\delta_{0},\beta_{0})tX_{2}),\ t\in{\mathbb{R}}.

Remark 1.11
.

This is of course coherent with Theorem
1.2
as the semiclassical measure of
(\psi_{0}^{\hbar})_{\hbar>0}
is given by the Dirac measure on
(x_{0},\pi^{\delta_{0},\beta_{0}})
and by the projector
\lvert\Phi_{1}\rangle\langle\Phi_{1}\rvert
, see

[
16
]
,
[
15
]
.

1.7.
Second microlocalization on the Engel group

We now investigate the Schrödinger equation for the time-scale
\tau=2
, once again through the use of time-averaged semiclassical measures.

Support condition

With a similar analysis as for the previous results, we obtain the following proposition.

Proposition 1.12
.

Let
(\psi^{\hbar}_{0})_{\hbar>0}
be a bounded family in
L^{2}({\mathbb{E}})
and
\psi^{\hbar}(t)=e^{it\Delta_{\mathbb{E}}}\psi_{0}^{\hbar}
the solution to the Schrödinger equation (
3.7
) for
\tau=2
.
Then any time-averaged semiclassical measure
t\mapsto\Gamma_{t}d\gamma_{t}\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathcal{M}}_{ov}^{+}({\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}))
for the family
(\psi^{\hbar}(t))_{\hbar>0}
and the sequence
(\hbar_{k})_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}
satisfies the
following additional properties:

(i)

Similarly as in Theorem
1.2
, for
d\gamma_{t}
-almost all generic representations
\pi^{\delta,\beta}
, the field of operators
\Gamma
decomposes along the spectral projectors of
H
:

\Gamma_{t}(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta})=\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{>0}}\Gamma_{n,t}(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta})\;\;{with}\;\;\Gamma_{n,t}(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta}):=\Pi_{n}(\pi^{\delta,\beta})\Gamma_{t}(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta})\Pi_{n}(\pi^{\delta,\beta})\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\pi^{\delta,\beta}}).

(ii)

For
n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{>0}
and almost all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}_{t}
, the distribution
d\gamma_{n,t}(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta})={\rm Tr}\left(\Gamma_{n,t}(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta})\right)d\gamma_{t}(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta})
on
{\mathbb{E}}\times(\mathfrak{g}_{3}^{*}\setminus\{0\}\times\mathfrak{r}_{2}^{*})
, we now have the following support condition:

(1.5)

{\rm supp}(\gamma_{n,t})\subset{\mathbb{E}}\times\{\pi^{\delta,\beta}\in\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}_{\rm gen}\,:\,\partial_{\beta}\mu_{n}(\delta,\beta)=0\}.

Using the relation given by Equation (
1.4
) between the eigenvalues of the symbol
H
and the Montgomery operators, we can write

\partial_{\beta}\mu_{n}(\delta,\beta)=\delta^{1/3}\partial_{\nu}\tilde{\mu}_{n}(\beta\delta^{-1/3}).

Thus for any critical point
\nu_{0}
of
\tilde{\mu}_{n}
, the set

C_{\nu_{0}}={\mathbb{E}}\times\{\pi^{\delta,\beta}\in\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}_{\rm gen}\,:\,\beta=\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}\},

is contained in the right-hand side’s set of Equation (
1.5
), and more precisely any point in
{\rm supp}(\gamma_{n,t})
is contained into
some set
C_{\nu_{c}}
for
\nu_{c}
a critical point of
\tilde{\mu}_{n}
. Remark that these sets are invariant by the dilations on
\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}
, and we call such subsets
cones
.

The question of the existence of non-degenerate critical points of the curves
(\nu\mapsto\tilde{\mu}_{n}(\nu))_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{>0}}
has already been studied and
we recall here the most recent results concerning such critical points.

Theorem 1.13

(
[
23
]
, Theorem 1.5)
.

There exists
n_{0}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{>0}
such that for
n=1
or
n\geq n_{0}
,
\tilde{\mu}_{n}
admits a unique critical point
\nu_{n,c}
. Moreover, it corresponds to a global minimum, i.e
\tilde{\mu}_{n}(\nu_{n,c})=\min_{\nu\in{\mathbb{R}}}\tilde{\mu}_{n}(\nu)
,
and this minimum is non-degenerate.

This result is conjectured to hold for all the eigenvalues of the Montgomery operators. Note also that, for any
n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{>0}
,
\nu\mapsto\tilde{\mu}_{n}(\nu)
admits at least one critical point
as it diverges to
+\infty
as
\nu
goes to
\pm\infty
.

Second-microlocal semiclassical measures

Contrary to the results obtained for the time-scale
\tau=1
, we cannot here relate the semiclassical measures at time
t\neq 0
with the one at
t=0
through propagation laws as the tools introduced so far do not
allow us to go past the previous support condition. For this reason, we develop a second microlocalization analysis on the cones
C_{\nu_{0}}
by extending our phase space and our space of symbols (see Section
4
).
The next result follows from the existence and the propagation of second-microlocal semiclassical measures on the cone
C_{\nu_{0}}
.

Theorem 1.14
.

Using the same notations as in Proposition
1.12
, let
n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{>0}
and
\nu_{0}
be a non-degenerate critical point of the map
\nu\in{\mathbb{R}}\mapsto\tilde{\mu}_{n}(\nu)
. Then up to a further extraction
of the sequence
(\hbar_{k})_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}
, there exist a positive measure

\gamma_{n}^{2}
on
{\mathbb{H}}\times\{\pi^{\delta,\beta}\in\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}_{\rm gen}\,:\,\beta=\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}\}
and a field of trace-class operators

(h,\pi)\in{\mathbb{H}}\times\{\pi^{\delta,\beta}\in\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}_{\rm gen}\,:\,\beta=\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}\}\mapsto\Gamma_{n,0}^{2}(h,\pi)\in\mathcal{L}^{1}(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}_{2})),

both depending solely on the subsequence
(\psi^{\hbar_{k}}_{0})_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}
and
such that the semiclassical measure
t\mapsto\gamma_{n,t}
of Proposition
1.12
satisfies for almost all time
t\in{\mathbb{R}}_{t}
:

\gamma_{n,t}(h,x_{2},\pi^{\delta,\beta})\geq{\mathbb{1}}_{C_{\nu_{0}}}k_{n,t}^{2}(h,\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}},x_{2},x_{2})dx_{2}\otimes\gamma_{n}^{2}(h,\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}}),

where
k_{n,t}^{2}(h,\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}},\cdot,\cdot)
is the kernel of the trace-class operator

\Gamma_{n,t}^{2}(h,\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}})=e^{-it\frac{\tilde{\mu}_{n}^{\prime\prime}(\nu_{0})}{2}\partial_{x_{2}}^{2}}\Gamma_{n,0}^{2}(h,\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}})e^{it\frac{\tilde{\mu}_{n}^{\prime\prime}(\nu_{0})}{2}\partial_{x_{2}}^{2}}.

We have used in the precedent theorem the writing of an element
x\in{\mathbb{E}}
as a couple
(h,x_{2})
in
{\mathbb{H}}\times{\mathbb{R}}_{2}
. For further details,
we refer to Section
2
.

Remark 1.15
.

One can reformulate Theorem
1.14
saying the map
t\mapsto\Gamma_{n,t}^{2}
is continuous and is determined by the following weak Heisenberg equation:

\begin{cases}&i\partial_{t}\Gamma_{n,t}^{2}=\left[-\frac{\tilde{\mu}_{n}^{\prime\prime}(\nu_{0})}{2}\partial_{x_{2}}^{2},\Gamma_{n,t}^{2}\right],\\
&\Gamma_{n,t}^{2}{}_{|t=0}=\Gamma_{n,0}^{2}.\end{cases}

When there is exactly one critical point to the map
\nu\in{\mathbb{R}}\mapsto\tilde{\mu}_{n}(\nu)
, which is true for all but a finite number of
n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{>0}
, the previous inequality on
\gamma_{n,t}
is actually an equality.

1.8.
Obstruction to dispersion

We present here the consequences of Theorem
1.14
for the study of the Schrödinger equation (
1.2
) for
\tau=2
, i.e the non-semiclassical regime.
Let
(\psi^{\hbar}(t)=e^{it\Delta_{{\mathbb{E}}}}\psi_{0}^{\hbar})_{\hbar>0}
be a solution to this equation. As dispersion often translates into a better regularity
of the densities
(|\psi^{\hbar}(t,x)|^{2}dxdt)_{\hbar>0}
than expected at first, we wish to undertand the regularity
of the weak limits of this family. More precisely, we are interested in the behaviour of quantities

\int_{0}^{T}\int_{{\mathbb{E}}}\chi(x)|e^{it\Delta_{{\mathbb{E}}}}\psi_{0}^{\hbar}(x)|^{2}\,dxdt,

in the asymptotic
\hbar\rightarrow 0
, for
\chi\in C_{0}^{\infty}({\mathbb{E}})
,
T\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
. Our understanding of such weak limits comes from the fact
that they can be obtained as marginals of the previously studied semiclassical measures.

Concerning the existence of Strichartz estimates, first note that their study in the setting of nilpotent Lie groups has already been the topic of multiple articles (see
[
1
]
,
[
3
]
,
[
4
]
,
[
13
]
),
but has been limited to nilpotent groups of step 2. The following result restricts the range of exponents for which one can hope to prove Strichartz estimates on the Engel group.
First observe that the only couples of exponents
(q,p)\in[2,\infty]^{2}
for which there exists
C_{p,q}>0
such that

(1.6)

\forall\psi_{0}\in L^{2}({\mathbb{E}}),\ \|e^{it\Delta_{\mathbb{E}}}\psi_{0}\|_{L^{q}({\mathbb{R}}_{t},L^{p}({\mathbb{E}}))}\leq C_{p,q}\|\psi_{0}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{E}})},

must satisfy the following scaling admissibility condition

\frac{2}{q}+\frac{7}{p}=\frac{7}{2}.

It is interesting to see that only one couple of exponents can actually been obtained aside from the trivial one
(\infty,2)
.

Proposition 1.16
.

The only exponents
(q,p)
for which the previous Strichartz estimates are possible are

(q,p)=(\infty,2)\ {\rm or}\ (2,14/5).

Our second result concerns the study of the local smoothing effect of the Schrödinger equation on the Engel group.
Thanks to the contribution of many authors (see for example
[
5
]
,
[
11
]
,
[
27
]
,
[
28
]
,
[
35
]
,
[
37
]
), it is now well-known that dispersive equations give rise to smoothing-type estimates.
In the simple case of the free Laplacian on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, given any
\epsilon>0
and any ball
B\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, it is possible to find
C>0
such that the following inequality

(1.7)

\int_{0}^{\epsilon}\||D_{x}|^{1/2}\left(e^{it\Delta}\psi_{0}\right)\|_{L^{2}(B)}^{2}\,dt\leq C\|\psi_{0}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})}^{2},

holds uniformly for every
\psi_{0}\in C_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})
.

One can wonder if a similar phenomenon can be observed on the Engel group: given
\epsilon>0
,
s>0
and a bounded open set
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{E}}
, is it possible to find a constant
C>0
such that
the following inequality

(1.8)

\int_{0}^{\epsilon}\||\Delta_{{\mathbb{E}}}|^{s/2}\left(e^{it\Delta_{\mathbb{E}}}\psi_{0}\right)\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}\,dt\leq C\|\psi_{0}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{E}})}^{2},

holds uniformly for every
\psi_{0}\in C_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{E}})
?

Theorem 1.17
.

Given any
\epsilon>0
,
s>0
and any bounded open set
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{E}}
, it is not possible to find a constant
C>0
such that the estimate (
1.8
)
holds uniformly for every solution of Schrödinger equation with initial datum
\psi_{0}\in C_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{E}})
.

Actually, one can prove a finer result as the previous theorem does not exclude that smoothness could be gained in some particular directions.
The only direction where one could expect to gain regularity is along the characteristic leaves but, in the spirit of
[
8
]
, the following result shows that this is still not attainable.

Theorem 1.18
.

Given any
\epsilon>0
,
s>0
and any bounded open set
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{E}}
, it is not possible to find a constant
C>0
such that the estimate

\int_{0}^{\epsilon}\||X_{2}|^{s}\left(e^{it\Delta_{\mathbb{E}}}\psi_{0}\right)\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}\,dt\leq C\|\psi_{0}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{E}})}^{2},

holds uniformly for every solution of Schrödinger equation with initial datum
\psi_{0}\in C_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{E}})
.

Remark 1.19
.

The previous results still hold for a Schrödinger operator
-\Delta_{\mathbb{E}}+V
where
V\in C^{\infty}({\mathbb{E}})
is bounded as well as all its derivatives, as such an addition would only modify the propagation laws
of the time-averaged second-microlocal semiclassical measures. Indeed, the operator
-\frac{\tilde{\mu}_{n}^{\prime\prime}(\nu_{0})}{2}\partial_{x_{2}}^{2}
is then replaced by the Schrödinger operators
-\frac{\tilde{\mu}_{n}^{\prime\prime}(\nu_{0})}{2}\partial_{x_{2}}^{2}+V(h,\cdot)

on the real line. For the sake of clarity, we will focus on the case
V=0
.

1.9.
Organization of the paper

In the next section, we present the fundamental results concerning the Engel group. The following
two sections develop the semiclassical analysis of
{\mathbb{E}}
and present the proofs of Theorems
1.2
and
1.10
and Proposition
1.12
.
In Section
4
, we develop a second-microlocal analysis on the cones
C_{\nu_{0}}
of the phase space and apply these tools in Section
5
to the study
of solutions to Schrödinger equation (
1.2
) for the time-scale
\tau=2
. At the end of this section, we give the proofs of the corollaries of this analysis to the study of dispersion.

Aknowledgement

The author is deeply grateful to Clotilde Fermanian Kammerer for her numerous advices during the realization of this project. Many thanks also
go to Véronique Fischer for very interesting conversations.

2.
Preliminaries on the Engel group

2.1.
Semidirect decomposition

As seen in the introduction, the exponential map defines a diffeomorphism between the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{E}}
and the Engel group. With the particular choice of basis
(X_{i})_{1\leq i\leq 4}
, one can then realize the Engel group
in its isomorphism class as
{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
equipped with the polynomial group law induced by the Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff formula and
the relations (
1.1
).
However, other diffeomorphisms exist between
\mathfrak{g}_{{\mathbb{E}}}
and the group
{\mathbb{E}}
and as a matter of fact, different parametrisations of the Engel group have been used in the litterature, see
[
2
]
,
[
7
]
.

The one we present here is well suited for our analysis as it results from an underlying algebraic decomposition of the Engel group and allows for a clear presentation of its irreducible representations.

Proposition 2.1
.

The following map

(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{4}\mapsto{\rm Exp}(x_{1}X_{1}+x_{3}X_{3}+x_{4}X_{4}){\rm Exp}(x_{2}X_{2})\in{\mathbb{E}},

defines a diffeomorphism.

This parametrisation allows us to single out a realization in its isomorphism class.

Definition 2.2
.

In the rest of the article, we realize the Engel group
{\mathbb{E}}
as the manifold
{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
endowed with the following group product: for
x=(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4}),\,y=(y_{1},y_{2},y_{3},y_{4})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
, we have

\displaystyle xy~=

\displaystyle\left(x_{1}+y_{1},x_{2}+y_{2},x_{3}+y_{3}-x_{2}y_{1},x_{4}+y_{4}+\frac{1}{2}(x_{1}y_{3}-x_{3}y_{1})-\frac{1}{2}x_{1}x_{2}y_{1}\right).

The inverse of an element
x
is given by

x^{-1}=(-x_{1},-x_{2},-x_{3}-x_{2}x_{1},-x_{4}).

The parametrisation given by Proposition
2.1
leads us to consider the subalgebras

\mathfrak{h}={\mathbb{R}}X_{1}\oplus{\mathbb{R}}X_{3}\oplus{\mathbb{R}}X_{4},

and
\mathfrak{r}_{2}={\mathbb{R}}X_{2}
, already introduced before.
As
\mathfrak{h}
satisfies the commutation relations of the Heisenberg Lie algebra, we denote by
{\mathbb{H}}={\rm Exp}(\mathfrak{h})
this injection of the Heisenberg group into
{\mathbb{E}}
.
We will also denote by
{\mathbb{R}}_{2}={\rm Exp}(\mathfrak{r}_{2})
the one parameter subgroup associated to
X_{2}
.

Any element of the Engel group can then be decomposed
as the product of an element of
{\mathbb{H}}
and an element of
{\mathbb{R}}_{2}
. Furthermore, for
x_{2}\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
(y_{1},y_{3},y_{4})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
, thanks to the Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff
formula, we compute that

(2.1)

{\rm Exp}(x_{2}X_{2}){\rm Exp}(y_{1}X_{1}+y_{3}X_{3}+y_{4}X_{4}){\rm Exp}(-x_{2}X_{2})={\rm Exp}(y_{1}X_{1}+(y_{3}-x_{2}y_{1})X_{3}+y_{4}X_{4}).

In other words, we have proved that the Engel group
{\mathbb{E}}
can be written as a semidirect product:

(2.2)

{\mathbb{E}}={\mathbb{H}}\rtimes{\mathbb{R}}_{2},

where the action of
{\mathbb{R}}_{2}
on
{\mathbb{H}}
is given by (
2.1
). For this reason, the coordinates introduced in Proposition
2.1
will be called the
semidirect coordinates
.
It is straightforward to check that the pushforward of the Lebesgue measure on
{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
by the semidirect coordinates corresponds to the same Haar measure already introduced.

We can compute the expressions of the vector fields
X_{i}
,
1\leq i\leq 4
in semidirect coordinates.
Recall that we may identify
\mathfrak{g}_{{\mathbb{E}}}
with the space of left-invariant vector fields on
{\mathbb{E}}
via

(2.3)

Xf(x)=\frac{d}{dt}f(x{\rm Exp}(tX))_{|t=0},\quad f\in C^{\infty}({\mathbb{E}}),\ x\in{\mathbb{E}}.

The basis
(X_{1},X_{2},X_{3},X_{4})
thus corresponds to the following vector fields:

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{split}X_{1}&=\partial_{1}-x_{2}\partial_{3}-\frac{1}{2}\left(x_{3}+x_{1}x_{2}\right)\partial_{4},\\
X_{2}&=\partial_{2},\\
X_{3}&=\partial_{3}+\frac{1}{2}x_{1}\partial_{4},\\
X_{4}&=\partial_{4}.\end{split}\right.

2.2.
The unitary dual set and the Fourier transform

We present here the harmonic analysis of the Engel group that we will use in the following sections.

2.2.1.
Irreducible unitary representations and dual set

The first step in understanding the harmonic analysis of the Engel group is to compute its irreducible representations.

For any nilpotent Lie group
G
, Kirillov’s theory and the orbit method allow for an explicit description of its irreducible representations. Furthermore, it gives
an homeomorphism between the dual set
\widehat{G}
and the set of coadjoint orbits
\mathfrak{g}^{*}/{\rm Ad}^{*}(G)
. For a more extensive discussion of the orbit method, see
[
12
]
,
[
14
]
,
[
21
]
, and in
the particular case of the Engel group see
[
2
, Appendix A]
.

In the case of the Engel group, this gives the following proposition.

Proposition 2.3
.

The dual of
{\mathbb{E}}
is the disjoint union of three classes of representations

\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}=\{\pi^{\delta,\beta}\,:\,(\delta,\beta)\in\mathfrak{g}_{3}^{*}\setminus\{0\}\times\mathfrak{r}_{2}^{*}\}\sqcup\{\pi^{\lambda}\,:\,\lambda\in\mathfrak{g}_{2}^{*}\setminus\{0\}\}\sqcup\{\pi^{(0,\alpha)}\,:\,\alpha\in\mathfrak{g}_{1}^{*}\}.

Let
x=(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4})\in{\mathbb{E}}
. For
(\delta,\beta)\in\mathfrak{g}_{3}^{*}\setminus\{0\}\times\mathfrak{r}_{2}^{*}
, the irreducible unitary representation
\pi^{\delta,\beta}
is defined by: for
\varphi\in\mathcal{H}_{\pi^{\delta,\beta}}:=L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}_{\xi})
,

\pi^{\delta,\beta}(x)\varphi(\xi)={\rm exp}\left[i\delta\left(x_{4}+\xi x_{3}+\frac{1}{2}x_{1}x_{3}\right)\right]{\rm exp}\left[i\left(\beta+\frac{\delta}{2}(\xi+x_{1})^{2}\right)x_{2}\right]\varphi(\xi+x_{1}).

For
\lambda\in\mathfrak{g}_{2}^{*}\setminus\{0\}
, the unitary representation
\pi^{\lambda}
acts on
\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}:=L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}_{\xi})
by

\pi^{\lambda}(x)\varphi(\xi)={\rm exp}\left[i\lambda\left(x_{3}+\xi x_{2}+\frac{1}{2}x_{1}x_{2}\right)\right]\varphi(\xi+x_{1}).

And finally, the last unitary irreducible representations are given by the characters of the first stratum: for every
\alpha=(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})\in\mathfrak{g}_{1}^{*}
, we set

\pi^{(0,\alpha)}(x)={\rm exp}\left[i(\alpha_{1}x_{1}+\alpha_{2}x_{2})\right]\in{\mathbb{C}}.

Note that the trivial representation
1_{{\mathbb{E}}}
corresponds to the class of
\pi^{(0,\alpha)}
with
\alpha=0
.

As already pointed out in the introduction, the family of irreducible representations
\{\pi^{\delta,\beta}\,:\,(\delta,\beta)\in\mathfrak{g}_{3}^{*}\setminus\{0\}\times\mathfrak{r}_{2}^{*}\}
are called the generic representations and is denoted by
\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}_{\rm gen}
. This set
is open and dense in
\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}
, and homeomorphic to
{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\{0\}\times{\mathbb{R}}
.

Remark 2.4
.

The non-generic representations can be understood in the following way. Consider the realization of the Heisenberg group as the manifold
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
with the following group product:
for
(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}),(y_{1},y_{2},y_{3})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
, we put

(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})\star_{\bar{{\mathbb{H}}}}(y_{1},y_{2},y_{3})=(x_{1}+y_{1},x_{2}+y_{2},x_{3}+y_{3}-x_{2}y_{1}),

and we denote by
\bar{{\mathbb{H}}}
this realization. We easily ckeck that the map
p_{\bar{{\mathbb{H}}}}:{\mathbb{E}}\rightarrow\bar{{\mathbb{H}}}
defined by

p_{\bar{{\mathbb{H}}}}:(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4})\in{\mathbb{E}}\mapsto(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})\in\bar{{\mathbb{H}}},

is a surjective group morphism.
The non-generic irreducible representations of
{\mathbb{E}}
, i.e the families of representations

\{\pi^{\lambda}\,:\,\lambda\in\mathfrak{g}_{2}^{*}\setminus\{0\}\}
and
\{\pi^{(0,\alpha)}\,:\,\alpha\in\mathfrak{g}_{1}^{*}\}
, are obtained as the irreducible representations of
\bar{{\mathbb{H}}}

composed with the morphism
p_{\bar{{\mathbb{H}}}}
. As such we will write

\widehat{\bar{{\mathbb{H}}}}=\{\pi^{\lambda}\,:\,\lambda\in\mathfrak{g}_{2}^{*}\setminus\{0\}\}\sqcup\{\pi^{(0,\alpha)}\,:\,\alpha\in\mathfrak{g}_{1}^{*}\}.

2.2.2.
Fourier transform

For
f
a function in
L^{1}({\mathbb{E}})
and
\pi
an irreducible representation, the Fourier transform of
f
at
\pi
is defined by

\hat{f}(\pi)=\mathcal{F}f(\pi):=\int_{{\mathbb{E}}}f(x)\pi(x)^{*}\,dx.

Note that, as each representation
\pi
is a group morphism from
{\mathbb{E}}
to the group of unitary operators
U(\mathcal{H}_{\pi})
, we have that
\mathcal{F}f(\pi)
is a bounded operator on
\mathcal{H}_{\pi}
with a norm satifisfying

\|\mathcal{F}f(\pi)\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\pi})}\leq\int_{{\mathbb{E}}}|f(x)|\|\pi(x)^{*}\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\pi})}\,dx=\|f\|_{L^{1}({\mathbb{E}})}.

Observe that if
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}
are two representations intertwined by an operator
\mathcal{I}
, we have

\mathcal{F}f(\pi_{1})=\mathcal{I}^{-1}\circ\mathcal{F}f(\pi_{2})\circ\mathcal{I},

and we thus intepret the Fourier transform as a field of operators on
\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}
up to equivalence through intertwining operators.

We also introduce here the semiclassical Fourier transform
\mathcal{F}_{{\mathbb{E}}}^{\hbar}
. First, we extend the dilations on the group
{\mathbb{E}}
to its unitary dual
\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}
: for
\pi\in\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}
and
r\in{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
,
we define the representation
r\cdot\pi
by

\forall x\in{\mathbb{E}},\ (r\cdot\pi)(x):=\pi(r\cdot x).

For each kind of representations given by Proposition
2.3
, we have

[r\cdot\pi^{\delta,\beta}]=[\pi^{r^{3}\delta,r\beta}],\ [r\cdot\pi^{\lambda}]=[\pi^{r^{2}\lambda}],\ [r\cdot\pi^{(0,\alpha)}]=[\pi^{(0,r\alpha)}].

For
f
a function in
L^{1}({\mathbb{E}})
and
\pi
an irreducible representation, the semiclassical Fourier transform of
f
at
\pi
is defined by

(2.4)

\mathcal{F}_{{\mathbb{E}}}^{\hbar}f(\pi):=\int_{{\mathbb{E}}}f(x)(\hbar^{-1}\cdot\pi(x))^{*}\,dx=\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{E}}f(\hbar^{-1}\cdot\pi).

2.2.3.
Plancherel formula

Following
[
14
]
, the Plancherel theorem states there exists a unique positive
\sigma
-finite measure
\mu_{\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}}
, called the
Plancherel measure
of
{\mathbb{E}}
, such that for any

f\in\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{E}})
, we have

(2.5)

\int_{{\mathbb{E}}}|f(x)|^{2}\,dx=\int_{\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}}\|\mathcal{F}f(\pi)\|_{{\rm HS}(\mathcal{H}_{\pi})}^{2}\,d\mu_{\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}}(\pi).

The orbit method allows us to compute explicitly this measure with respect to the parametrisation of
\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}
given by Proposition
2.3
.

Proposition 2.5
.

The Plancherel measure of the Engel group is supported in
\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}_{\rm gen}
. Moreover, there exists
c_{{\mathbb{E}}}>0
such that

d\mu_{\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}}(\pi^{\delta,\beta})=c_{{\mathbb{E}}}|\delta|d\delta d\beta.

Equation (
2.5
) may be reformulated in the following way:
the group Fourier transform can be extended to a surjective isometry from
L^{2}({\mathbb{E}})
onto the Hilbert
space

L^{2}(\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}):=\int_{\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}}^{\oplus}{\rm HS}(\mathcal{H}_{\pi})\,d\mu_{\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}}(\pi),

i.e the space of
\mu_{\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}}
-measurable fields of Hilbert-Schmidt operators
\{\sigma(\pi)\in{\rm HS}(\mathcal{H}_{\pi})\,:\,\pi\in\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}\}
which are square-integrable in the
sense that

\|\sigma\|_{L^{2}(\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}})}:=\int_{\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}}\|\sigma(\pi)\|_{{\rm HS}(\mathcal{H}_{\pi})}^{2}\,d\mu_{\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}}(\pi)<\infty.

The Plancherel formula also yields an inversion formula: for any
f\in{\mathcal{S}}({\mathbb{E}})
and
x\in{\mathbb{E}}
, we have

(2.6)

f(x)=\int_{\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}}{\rm Tr}_{\mathcal{H}_{\pi}}\left(\pi(x){\mathcal{F}}f(\pi)\right)\,d\mu(\pi)\,,

where
{\rm Tr}_{\mathcal{H}_{\pi}}
denotes the trace of trace-clas operators on
\mathcal{H}_{\pi}
.
This formula makes sense since for
f\in{\mathcal{S}}({\mathbb{E}})
, the operators
{\mathcal{F}}f(\pi)
for
\pi\in\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}
, are trace-class and the right-side term is finite, see
[
12
]
.

2.2.4.
Convolution and the von Neumann algebra of the group

Let
f,g\in L^{1}({\mathbb{E}})
be two integrable functions on
{\mathbb{E}}
, the convolution
f*g
is defined by

\forall x\in{\mathbb{E}},\ (f*g)(x):=\int_{{\mathbb{E}}}f(y)g(y^{-1}x)\,dy.

The Young inequality is still valid if one takes the homogeneous dimension
Q
instead of the dimension of the manifold
n=4
.

As expected, the Fourier transform sends the convolution to composition in the following way:

(2.7)

\forall\pi\in\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}},\ {\mathcal{F}}(f*g)(\pi)={\mathcal{F}}g(\pi)\circ{\mathcal{F}}f(\pi)\,.

We recall that a convolution operator
T
with integrable convolution kernel
\kappa\in L^{1}({\mathbb{E}})
is defined by
Tf=f*\kappa
and we have
\mathcal{F}(Tf)=\mathcal{F}\kappa\circ\mathcal{F}f
by (
2.7
).
Hence,
T
appears as a Fourier multiplier with Fourier symbol
\mathcal{F}\kappa
acting on the left of
\mathcal{F}f
.
Consequently,
T
is invariant under left-translation and bounded on
L^{2}({\mathbb{E}})
with operator norm

\|T\|_{{\mathcal{L}}(L^{2}({\mathbb{E}}))}\leq\sup_{\pi\in\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}}\|\mathcal{F}\kappa(\pi)\|_{{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{H}_{\pi})}.

In other words,
T
is in the space
{\mathcal{L}}(L^{2}({\mathbb{E}}))^{\mathbb{E}}
of the left-invariant bounded operators on
L^{2}({\mathbb{E}})
.

Let us denote by
L^{\infty}(\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}})
the space of measurable fields of operators
\sigma=\{\sigma(\pi):\pi\in\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}\}
which are bounded in the sense that the essential supremum
with respect to the Plancherel measure
\mu_{\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}}
,

\|\sigma\|_{L^{\infty}(\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}})}:={\rm supess}_{\pi\in\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}}\|\sigma\|_{{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{H}_{\pi})},

is finite.
The space
L^{\infty}(\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}})
with the norm
\|\cdot\|_{L^{\infty}(\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}})}
is a von Neummann algebra, and is called the
von Neumann algebra of the group
.
As explained above, we already know we have
\mathcal{F}\left(L^{1}({\mathbb{E}})\right)\subset L^{\infty}(\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}})
, but this inclusion is strict.

The full Plancherel theorem (see
[
14
]
) implies that the von
Neumann algebras
L^{\infty}(\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}})
and the space
{\mathcal{L}}(L^{2}({\mathbb{E}}))^{\mathbb{E}}
of bounded operators on
L^{2}({\mathbb{E}})
invariant by left-translations introduced above are isomorphic via the mapping

\sigma\mapsto\mathfrak{M}(\sigma)
where
\mathfrak{M}(\sigma)
is the Fourier multiplier defined by

(2.8)

\mathcal{F}\left(\mathfrak{M}(\sigma)f\right)=\sigma\circ\mathcal{F}f,\quad f\in L^{2}({\mathbb{E}}).

The isomorphism between
L^{\infty}(\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}})
and
{\mathcal{L}}(L^{2}({\mathbb{E}}))^{\mathbb{E}}
allows us to naturally extend the group Fourier transform to distributions
\kappa\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}({\mathbb{E}})
such that the convolution operator
f\mapsto f*\kappa
is bounded on
L^{2}({\mathbb{E}})

by setting that
\mathcal{F}\kappa
is the symbol of the corresponding operator in
{\mathcal{L}}(L^{2}({\mathbb{E}}))^{\mathbb{E}}
. Following
[
21
]
, the space of such distributions
\kappa

will be denoted by
\mathcal{K}({\mathbb{E}})
and constitutes another realization of the von Neumann algebra of
{\mathbb{E}}
.

Another functional space that will be of use in our study is the space of the Fourier transform of Schwartz functions.

Definition 2.6
.

We denote by
\mathcal{S}(\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}})\subset L^{2}(\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}})
the image by the Fourier transform of the Schwartz space
\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{E}})
, i.e

\mathcal{S}(\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}})=\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{E}})).

Moreover, we put on
\mathcal{S}(\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}})
the topology such that the Fourier transform
\mathcal{F}:\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{E}})\rightarrow\mathcal{S}(\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}})
is a homeomorphism.

2.2.5.
Infinitesimal representations and Fourier transforms of left-invariant differential vector fields

The group Fourier transform can also be extended to certain classes of distributions whose convolution operators yield left-invariant operators, but not necessarily bounded on
L^{2}({\mathbb{E}})
as previously.
Indeed, denoting by
\pi(X)
the infinitesimal representation of
\pi
at
X\in\mathfrak{g}
, i.e.
\pi(X)=\frac{d}{dt}\pi({\rm Exp}(tX))|_{t=0}
, we have

\mathcal{F}(Xf)(\pi)=\pi(X)\circ\mathcal{F}f(\pi).

For instance, we have for
(\delta,\beta)\in\mathfrak{g}_{3}^{*}\setminus\{0\}\times\mathfrak{r}_{2}
, we have

(2.9)

\left\{\begin{split}\pi^{\delta,\beta}(X_{1})&=\partial_{\xi},\\
\pi^{\delta,\beta}(X_{2})&=i\left(\beta+\frac{\delta}{2}\xi^{2}\right),\\
\pi^{\delta,\beta}(X_{3})&=i\delta\xi,\\
\pi^{\delta,\beta}(X_{4})&=i\delta.\end{split}\right.

These operators need to be seen as acting on the space of smooth vectors
\mathcal{H}_{\pi}^{\infty}\subset\mathcal{H}_{\pi}
for
\pi=\pi^{\delta,\beta}
, which can be identified with the Schwartz space
\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{R}}_{\xi})\subset L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}_{\xi})
, see
[
21
]
.
We also compute for any
\lambda\in\mathfrak{g}_{2}^{*}\setminus\{0\}
that

(2.10)

\pi^{\lambda}(X_{1})=\partial_{\xi},\ \pi^{\lambda}(X_{2})=i\lambda{\xi},\ \pi^{\lambda}(X_{3})=i\lambda\ \mbox{and}\ \pi^{\lambda}(X_{4})=0,

and for
\alpha=(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})\in\mathfrak{g}_{1}^{*}
,

(2.11)

\pi^{(0,\alpha)}(X_{1})=i\alpha_{1},\ \pi^{(0,\alpha)}(X_{2})=i\alpha_{2}\ \mbox{and}\ \pi^{(0,\alpha)}(X_{3})=\pi^{(0,\alpha)}(X_{4})=0.

The infinitesimal representation of
\pi
extends to the universal enveloping Lie algebra
\mathfrak{U}(\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{E}})
of
\mathfrak{g}_{{\mathbb{E}}}
that we identify with the left-invariant differential operators on
{\mathbb{E}}
.
Then for such a differential operator
T
, we have
\mathcal{F}(Tf)(\pi)=\pi(T)\mathcal{F}f(\pi)

and we may write
\pi(T)={\mathcal{F}}(T)
.
For instance, if we denote by
{X}^{\aleph}
the product of
|\aleph|
left-invariant vector fields (
\aleph\in{\mathbb{N}}^{4}
), then

(2.12)

\mathcal{F}({X}^{\aleph}f)(\pi)=\pi({X})^{\aleph}\mathcal{F}f(\pi)\;\;{\rm and}\;\;\mathcal{F}({X}^{\aleph})=\pi({X})^{\aleph}.

Note that
\pi({X})^{\aleph}
may be considered as a field of unbounded operators on
\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}
defined on the smooth vectors of the representations, (see
[
21
]
).

For the particular case of the subLaplacian
\Delta_{\mathbb{E}}
, we compute its infinitesimal representation
H=\mathcal{F}(-\Delta_{\mathbb{E}})
thanks to the equalities in (
2.9
), (
2.10
) and (
2.11
):
at
\pi^{(0,\alpha,\beta)}
,
(\alpha,\beta)\in\mathfrak{g}_{1}^{*}
, we find the scalar

(2.13)

H(\pi^{(0,\alpha)})=\mathcal{F}(-\Delta_{{\mathbb{E}}})(\pi^{(0,\alpha)})=|\alpha|^{2}.

For a representation of the form
\pi^{\lambda}
,
\lambda\in\mathfrak{g}_{2}^{*}
, we obtain

(2.14)

H(\pi^{\lambda})=\mathcal{F}(-\Delta_{\mathbb{E}})(\pi^{\lambda})=-\partial_{\xi}^{2}+\lambda^{2}\xi^{2},

and finally for
\pi^{\delta,\beta}\in\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}_{\rm gen}
, we get

(2.15)

H(\pi^{\delta,\beta})=\mathcal{F}(-\Delta_{\mathbb{E}})(\pi^{\delta,\beta})=-\partial_{\xi}^{2}+\left(\beta+\frac{\delta}{2}\xi^{2}\right)^{2}.

2.2.6.
Difference operators

For a general nilpotent Lie group
G
with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
, we introduce the following operators acting on fields of operators on
\widehat{G}
.

Definition 2.7
.

For any
q\in C^{\infty}(G)
with atmost polynomial growth, we set

\forall\pi\in\widehat{G},\ \Delta_{q}\widehat{\kappa}(\pi):=\widehat{q\kappa}(\pi),

for any Schwartz function
\kappa\in\mathcal{S}(G)
.

Note that for two such functions
q_{1},q_{2}
, the corresponding difference operators commute:

\Delta_{q_{1}}\Delta_{q_{2}}=\Delta_{q_{2}}\Delta_{q_{1}}=\Delta_{q_{1}q_{2}}.

Remark 2.8
.

The difference operators generalise the Euclidean derivatives with respect to the Fourier variable on
({\mathbb{R}}^{n},+)
and will serve as a mean
of measuring regularity with respect to the dual variable
\pi\in\widehat{G}
.

Proposition 2.9
.

Let
G
a nilpotent Lie group with Plancherel measure
d\mu_{\widehat{G}}
: for
f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(G)
and
g\in\mathcal{S}(G)
, we have

\int_{\widehat{G}}{\rm Tr}(\Delta_{i}\widehat{f}(\pi)\widehat{g}(\pi))\,d\mu_{\widehat{G}}(\pi)=-\int_{\widehat{G}}{\rm Tr}(\widehat{f}(\pi)\Delta_{i}\widehat{g}(\pi))\,d\mu_{\widehat{G}}(\pi),

where
\Delta_{i}
is the difference operator associated to the exponential coordinate
x_{i}
and
\hat{f}
is in the domain of
\Delta_{i}
.

Proof.

With the same notations, we can write

\displaystyle\int_{\widehat{G}}{\rm Tr}(\Delta_{i}\widehat{f}(\pi)\widehat{g}(\pi))\,d\mu_{\widehat{G}}(\pi)

\displaystyle=\left((-x_{i}f)*g\right)(e_{G})

\displaystyle=\langle f,y_{i}g(\cdot^{-1})\rangle_{\mathcal{S}^{\prime},\mathcal{S}}

\displaystyle=-\left(f*(x_{i}g)\right)(e_{G})

\displaystyle=-\int_{\widehat{G}}{\rm Tr}(\widehat{f}(\pi)\Delta_{i}\widehat{g}(\pi))\,d\mu_{\widehat{G}}(\pi).

∎

In the case of the Engel group, we will more simply note as
\Delta_{i}
the difference operators associated to the coordinate function

q_{i}(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4})=-x_{i}
and write, for
\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}}^{4}
,
\Delta^{\alpha}
the product of
|\alpha|
such operators.

As one might expect, the difference operators enjoy an avatar of Leibniz formula.

Proposition 2.10
.

[
21
, Chapter 5]

For any
\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}}_{>0}^{4}
, there exist constants
c_{\alpha,\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{>0}^{4}
, with
[\alpha_{1}]+[\alpha_{2}]=[\alpha]
, such that for any
\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}\in\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{E}})
, we have

\Delta^{\alpha}\left(\hat{\kappa}_{1}\hat{\kappa}_{2}\right)=\sum_{[\alpha_{1}]+[\alpha_{2}]=[\alpha]}c_{\alpha,\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}\Delta^{\alpha_{1}}\hat{\kappa}_{1}\Delta^{\alpha_{2}}\hat{\kappa}_{2},

with

c_{\alpha,\alpha_{1},0}=\left\{\begin{split}1&\ if\ \alpha_{1}=\alpha\\
0&\ otherwise\ \end{split}\right.,\ c_{\alpha,0,\alpha_{2}}=\left\{\begin{split}1&\ if\ \alpha_{2}=\alpha\\
0&\ otherwise\ \end{split}\right..

Finally, we can explicitly compute the action of difference operators on elements of
\mathcal{S}(\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}})
.

Proposition 2.11
.

Using the parametrisation of the dual
\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}
given by Proposition
2.3
, for
\kappa\in\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{E}})
a Schwartz function, the restriction of the Fourier transform
\widehat{\kappa}
to the generic representations
\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}_{\rm gen}

(\delta,\beta)\in\mathfrak{g}_{3}^{*}\setminus\{0\}\times\mathfrak{r}_{2}^{*}\mapsto\widehat{\kappa}(\pi^{\delta,\beta})\in L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}_{\xi}),

identifies with a smooth map in
C^{\infty}(\mathfrak{g}_{3}^{*}\setminus\{0\}\times\mathfrak{r}_{2}^{*},L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}_{\xi})
. Moreover, for
\pi^{\delta,\beta}\in\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}_{\rm gen}
we have:

\displaystyle\Delta_{1}\widehat{\kappa}(\pi^{\delta,\beta})

\displaystyle=\frac{i}{\delta}\left[\pi^{\delta,\beta}(X_{3}),\widehat{\kappa}(\pi^{\delta,\beta})\right],

\displaystyle\Delta_{2}\widehat{\kappa}(\pi^{\delta,\beta})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{i}\partial_{\beta}\widehat{\kappa}(\pi^{\delta,\beta}),

\displaystyle\Delta_{3}\widehat{\kappa}(\pi^{\delta,\beta})

\displaystyle=\frac{i}{\delta}\pi^{\delta,\beta}(X_{3})\Delta_{2}\widehat{\kappa}(\pi^{\delta,\beta})-\frac{i}{\delta}\left[\pi^{\delta,\beta}(X_{1}),\widehat{\kappa}(\pi^{\delta,\beta})\right],

\displaystyle\Delta_{4}\widehat{\kappa}(\pi^{\delta,\beta})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{i}\partial_{\delta}\widehat{\kappa}(\pi^{\delta,\beta})+\frac{1}{2}\Delta_{1}\Delta_{3}\widehat{\kappa}(\pi^{\delta,\beta})+\frac{i}{\delta}\pi^{\delta,\beta}(X_{3})\Delta_{3}\widehat{\kappa}(\pi^{\delta,\beta})

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\xi^{2}\Delta_{2}\widehat{\kappa}(\pi^{\delta,\beta}).

Proof.

Let
\varphi\in\mathcal{H}_{\pi^{\delta,\beta}}^{\infty}
a smooth vector. By definition we have

\Delta_{1}\widehat{\kappa}(\pi^{\delta,\beta})\varphi=-\int_{{\mathbb{E}}}x_{1}\kappa(x)\pi^{\delta,\beta}(x)^{*}\varphi\,dx.

It follows from direct computation that we have

\left[\frac{i}{\delta}\pi^{\delta,\beta}(X_{3}),\pi^{\delta,\beta}(x)^{*}\right]=-x_{1}\pi^{\delta,\beta}(x)^{*},

and we deduce the corresponding formula for
\Delta_{1}
. The formula for
\Delta_{2}
follows from the equation

\partial_{\beta}\pi^{\delta,\beta}(x)^{*}=-x_{2}\pi^{\delta,\beta}(x)^{*},

and we argue similarly for
\Delta_{3}
. Finally, to establish the formula concerning
\Delta_{4}
, observe that we have

\frac{1}{i}\partial_{\delta}\widehat{\kappa}(\pi^{\delta,\beta})\varphi(\xi)=\int_{{\mathbb{E}}}\kappa(y)\left(-y_{4}+\frac{1}{2}y_{1}y_{3}-\xi y_{3}-\frac{1}{2}\xi^{2}y_{2}\right)\pi^{\delta,\beta}(y)^{*}\varphi(\xi)\,dy

We recognize in the first two terms the operator
\Delta_{4}-\frac{1}{2}\Delta_{1}\Delta_{3}
, while we can also write the third one as follows

\int_{{\mathbb{E}}}\kappa(y)(-\xi y_{3})\pi^{\delta,\beta}(y)^{*}\,dy=-\frac{i}{\delta}\pi^{\delta,\beta}(X_{3})\Delta_{3}\widehat{\kappa}(\pi^{\delta,\beta}),

and we obtain the desired formula.
∎

Remark 2.12
.

These formulae depend on the choice of parametrisation of
\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}
given by Proposition
2.3
. Changing our choice of representation in its
equivalence by an intertwining operator would lead to a change in the expressions of the difference operators.

3.
Semiclassical analysis on the Engel group

In this section, we recall the elements of semiclassical pseudodifferential calculus necessary for our analysis on the Engel group, but the presentation
would hold for any graded nilpotent Lie group. We present also the results on the Schrödinger equation for the semiclassical time-scale
\tau=1
.

3.1.
Semiclassical pseudodifferential operators

3.1.1.
The space
{\mathcal{A}}_{0}
of semiclassical symbols

We denote by
\mathcal{A}_{0}({\mathbb{E}})
the space of symbols

\sigma=\{\sigma(x,\pi):(x,\pi)\in{\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}\}
of the form

\sigma(x,\pi)=\mathcal{F}\kappa_{x}(\pi)=\int_{\mathbb{E}}\kappa_{x}(z)(\pi(z))^{*}\,dz,

where
x\mapsto\kappa_{x}(\cdot)
is a smooth and compactly supported function from
{\mathbb{E}}
to
\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{E}})
.

As the Fourier transform is injective, it yields a one-to-one correspondence between the symbol
\sigma
and the function
\kappa
:
we have
\sigma(x,\pi)=\mathcal{F}\kappa_{x}(\pi)
and conversely the Fourier inversion formula (
2.6
)
yields

\forall x,z\in G,\ \kappa_{x}(z)=\int_{\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}}{\rm Tr}\left(\pi(z)\sigma(x,\pi)\right)d\mu_{\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}}(\pi).

The set
\mathcal{A}_{0}
is an algebra for the composition of symbols since if
\sigma_{1}(x,\pi)=\mathcal{F}\kappa_{1,x}(\pi)

and
\sigma_{2}(x,\pi)=\mathcal{F}\kappa_{2,x}(\pi)

are in
\mathcal{A}_{0}
, then so is
\sigma_{1}(x,\pi)\sigma_{2}(x,\pi)=\mathcal{F}(\kappa_{2,x}*\kappa_{1,x})(\pi)

by (
2.7
). It is also stable under the action of the difference operators and by differentiation in the first variable.

3.1.2.
General classes of symbols

It is possible to define classes of symbols
à la Hörmander
in the context of nilpotent Lie group. Indeed, following
[
21
, Section 5.2]
,
for
\rho,\delta\in[0,1]
and
m\in{\mathbb{R}}
fixed, the class
S_{\rho,\delta}^{m}({\mathbb{E}})
of
symbols of order
m
is the space of the fields of operators

\sigma(x,\pi)
such that for every
a,b,c\in{\mathbb{N}}_{>0}
, we have

(3.1)

\|\sigma\|_{S_{\rho,\delta}^{m},a,b,c}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\gamma|\leq c,\\
[\alpha]\leq a,[\beta]\leq b\end{subarray}}\sup_{x\in{\mathbb{E}},\pi\in\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}}\|({\rm Id}+H(\pi))^{\frac{\rho[\alpha]-\delta[\beta]-m+\gamma}{2}}X_{x}^{\beta}\Delta^{\alpha}\sigma(x,\pi)({\rm Id}+H(\pi))^{-\frac{\gamma}{2}}\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\pi})}<\infty.

These spaces are naturally Fréchet spaces when equipped with the seminorms
\|\cdot\|_{S_{\rho,\delta}^{m},a,b,c}
.

Remark 3.1
.

It has been shown in
[
21
]
that the topology on
S_{\rho,\delta}^{m}({\mathbb{E}})
is also generated by the seminorms
\|\cdot\|_{S_{\rho,\delta}^{m},a,b}:=\|\cdot\|_{S_{\rho,\delta}^{m},a,b,0}
, meaning we
can restrict ourselves to
c=0
.

The class of
smoothing symbols

S^{-\infty}({\mathbb{E}})
is then defined as the intersection of all theses classes
\bigcap_{m\in{\mathbb{R}}}S^{m}
with the topology of the projective limit.
We can then check that our symbols
\mathcal{A}_{0}
corresponds to smoothing symbols with compact support on the first variable.

3.1.3.
Quantization and semiclassical pseudodifferential operators

Given
\hbar>0
, the
semiclassical parameter
, we quantify a symbol
\sigma\in S^{m}({\mathbb{E}})
,
m\in{\mathbb{R}}
, by setting for
f\in{\mathcal{S}}({\mathbb{E}})
,

(3.2)

{\rm Op}_{\hbar}(\sigma)f(x)=\int_{\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}}{\rm Tr}\left(\pi(x)\sigma(x,\hbar\cdot\pi){\mathcal{F}}f(\pi)\right)d\mu_{\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}}(\pi),

see
[
21
, Section 5.1]
for the well-posedness of this formula. Such an operator is called a
semiclassical pseudodifferential operator
.

The integral kernel of the operator
{\rm Op}_{\hbar}(\sigma)
is the distribution

{\mathbb{E}}\times{\mathbb{E}}\ni(x,y)\mapsto\kappa^{\hbar}_{x}(y^{-1}x),

where
\kappa^{\hbar}_{x}(z)=\hbar^{-Q}\kappa_{x}\left(\hbar^{-1}\cdot z\right)

and
\kappa_{x}\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}({\mathbb{E}})
is such that
\mathcal{F}\kappa_{x}(\pi)=\sigma(x,\pi)
. The distribution
\kappa
is called the
convolution kernel
associated to
\sigma
.
For
f\in\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{E}})
, we can write the action of
{\rm Op}_{\hbar}(\sigma)
as follow

{\rm Op}_{\hbar}(\sigma)f(x)=f*\kappa_{x}^{\hbar}(x).

As an example, the subLaplacian
-\hbar^{2}\Delta_{\mathbb{E}}
belongs to this pseudodifferential calculus and its symbol is given by the field
H
.

As already observed in
[
15
]
, the action of symbols in
\mathcal{A}_{0}
on
L^{2}({\mathbb{E}})
is easily seen to be bounded:

\exists C>0,\forall\sigma\in{\mathcal{A}}_{0},\forall\hbar>0,\ \|{\rm Op}_{\hbar}(\sigma)\|_{{\mathcal{L}}(L^{2}({\mathbb{E}}))}\leq C\,\int_{{\mathbb{E}}}\sup_{x\in{\mathbb{E}}}|\kappa_{x}(z)|\,dz.

For a symbol in a general class
S_{\rho,\delta}^{m}({\mathbb{E}})
,
m\in{\mathbb{R}}
, we have the following result.

Theorem 3.2

(Calderón-Vaillancourt,
[
21
]
)
.

Let
\sigma\in S_{0,0}^{0}({\mathbb{E}})
. Then
{\rm Op}_{1}(\sigma)
extends to a bounded operator on
L^{2}({\mathbb{E}})
. Moreover, there exist a constant
C>0
and
integers
a,b\in{\mathbb{N}}_{>0}
, both independent of
\sigma
, such that for the seminorm
N(\cdot):=\|\cdot\|_{S_{0,0}^{0},a,b}
we have

\forall\phi\in\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{E}}),\ \|{\rm Op}_{1}(\sigma)\phi\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{E}})}\leq CN(\sigma)\|\phi\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{E}})}.

3.1.4.
Symbolic calculus

Composition of two semiclassical pseudodifferential operators is again a semiclassical pseudodifferential operator. Moreover, there exists a symbolic calculus giving the symbol of the composition.
In this paper, we will mainly use the description of the commutator between the subLaplacian and a smoothing semiclassical pseudodifferential operator, which comes from an explicit computation and writes:
for all
\sigma\in{\mathcal{A}}_{0}
,

(3.3)

[{\rm Op}_{\hbar}(\sigma),-\hbar^{2}\Delta_{\mathbb{E}}]={\rm Op}_{\hbar}\left([\sigma,H]\right)\ +\ 2\hbar\,{\rm Op}_{\hbar}\left((\pi(V)\cdot V)\sigma\right)\ +\ \hbar^{2}{\rm Op}_{\hbar}\left(\Delta_{\mathbb{E}}\sigma\right),

where have introduced the notation
(\pi(V)\cdot V)\sigma=\pi(X_{1})X_{1}\sigma+\pi(X_{2})X_{2}\sigma
.

3.2.
Semiclassical measures

To understand the concentration properties of a bounded family
(\psi_{0}^{\hbar})_{\hbar>0}
in
L^{2}({\mathbb{E}})
, we are interested in quantities of the form

\left({\rm Op}_{\hbar}(\sigma)\psi_{0}^{\hbar},\psi_{0}^{\hbar}\right),\quad\sigma\in\mathcal{A}_{0},

in the limit
\hbar
goes to 0.
We follow the presentation in
[
16
, Section 4]
and introduce the following vocabulary for operator-valued measures:

Definition 3.3
.

Let
Z
be a complete separable metric space,
and let
\xi\in Z\mapsto{\mathcal{H}}_{\xi}
a measurable field of complex Hilbert spaces on
Z
.

•

The set
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{ov}(Z,({\mathcal{H}}_{\xi})_{\xi\in Z})

is the set of pairs
(\gamma,\Gamma)
where
\gamma
is a positive Radon measure on
Z

and
\Gamma=\{\Gamma(\xi)\in{\mathcal{L}}^{1}({\mathcal{H}}_{\xi}):\xi\in Z\}
is a measurable field of trace-class operators
such that

\|\Gamma d\gamma\|_{\mathcal{M}}:=\int_{Z}{\rm Tr}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{\xi}}|\Gamma(\xi)|d\gamma(\xi)<\infty.

•

Two pairs
(\gamma,\Gamma)
and
(\gamma^{\prime},\Gamma^{\prime})

in
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{ov}(Z,({\mathcal{H}}_{\xi})_{\xi\in Z})

are equivalent when there exists a measurable function
f:Z\to\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
such that

d\gamma^{\prime}(\xi)=f(\xi)d\gamma(\xi)\;\;{\rm and}\;\;\Gamma^{\prime}(\xi)=\frac{1}{f(\xi)}\Gamma(\xi)

for
\gamma
-almost every
\xi\in Z
.
The equivalence class of
(\gamma,\Gamma)
is denoted by
\Gamma d\gamma
,
and the resulting quotient set is denoted by
{\mathcal{M}}_{ov}(Z,({\mathcal{H}}_{\xi})_{\xi\in Z})
.

•

A pair
(\gamma,\Gamma)

in
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{ov}(Z,({\mathcal{H}}_{\xi})_{\xi\in Z})

is positive when

\Gamma(\xi)\geq 0
for
\gamma
-almost all
\xi\in Z
.
In this case, we may write
(\gamma,\Gamma)\in\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{ov}^{+}(Z,({\mathcal{H}}_{\xi})_{\xi\in Z})
,
and
\Gamma d\gamma\geq 0
for
\Gamma d\gamma\in{\mathcal{M}}_{ov}^{+}(Z,({\mathcal{H}}_{\xi})_{\xi\in Z})
.

By convention and if not otherwise specified, a representative of the class
\Gamma d\gamma
is chosen such that
{\rm Tr}_{{\mathcal{H}}_{\xi}}\Gamma=1
on the support of
\gamma
.
In particular, if
{\mathcal{H}}_{\xi}
is
1
-dimensional,
\Gamma=1
and
\Gamma d\gamma
reduces to the measure
d\gamma
.
One checks readily that
\mathcal{M}_{ov}(Z,({\mathcal{H}}_{\xi})_{\xi\in Z})
equipped with the norm
\|\cdot\|_{{\mathcal{M}}}
is a Banach space.

When the field of Hilbert spaces is clear from the setting,
we may write

\mathcal{M}_{ov}(Z)=\mathcal{M}_{ov}(Z,({\mathcal{H}}_{\xi})_{\xi\in Z}),\quad\mbox{and}\quad\mathcal{M}_{ov}^{+}(Z)=\mathcal{M}_{ov}^{+}(Z,({\mathcal{H}}_{\xi})_{\xi\in Z}),

for short.
For instance, in the context of a nilpotent Lie group
G
, we will consider the space
Z=G\times\widehat{G}
with the field of Hilbert spaces
(\mathcal{H}_{\pi})_{(x,\pi)\in G\times\widehat{G}}
.

3.2.1.
The
C^{*}
-algebras
\mathcal{A}
and its topological dual

A measurable symbol
\sigma=\{\sigma(x,\pi)\,:\,(x,\pi)\in{\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}\}
is said to be bounded when there exists a constant
C>0
such that for
dxd\mu_{\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}}(\pi)
-almost
all
(x,\pi)\in{\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}
, we have
\|\sigma(x,\pi)\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\pi}}\leq C
. We denote by
\|\sigma\|_{L^{\infty}({\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}})}
the smallest such constant
C>0
and by

L^{\infty}({\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}})
the space of bounded measurable symbols. This vector space endowed with this norm is a von Neumann algebra.

Clearly,
\mathcal{A}_{0}
is a subalgebra of
L^{\infty}({\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}})
and we consider its closure for the norm
\|\cdot\|_{L^{\infty}({\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}})}
, denoted
by
\mathcal{A}
. The following property clarifies the introduction of operator-valued measures.

Proposition 3.4

(
[
16
]
)
.

The topological dual
\mathcal{A}^{*}
of the sub-
C^{*}
-algebra
\mathcal{A}
is isomorphic to the Banach space of operator-valued measures
\mathcal{M}_{ov}({\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}})
via

\Gamma d\gamma\in\mathcal{M}_{ov}({\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}})\mapsto\ell_{\Gamma d\gamma},\quad\ell_{\Gamma d\gamma}(\sigma):=\int_{{\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}}{\rm Tr}\left(\sigma(x,\pi)\Gamma(x,\pi)\right)d\gamma(x,\pi).

Moreover, the isomorphism is isometric:

\|\ell_{\Gamma d\gamma}\|_{\mathcal{A}^{*}}=\|\Gamma d\gamma\|_{\mathcal{M}_{ov}({\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}})},

and the positive linear functionals on
\mathcal{A}
are the
\ell_{\Gamma d\gamma}
with
\Gamma d\gamma\in\mathcal{M}_{ov}^{+}({\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}})
.

3.2.2.
Semiclassical measures and
\hbar
-oscillations

We are now able to characterize the limits the following linear forms

\sigma\in\mathcal{A}_{0}\mapsto\left({\rm Op}_{\hbar}(\sigma)\psi_{0}^{\hbar},\psi_{0}^{\hbar}\right)_{L^{2}({\mathbb{E}})}\in{\mathbb{C}}.

Theorem 3.5

(
[
16
]
)
.

Let
(\psi_{0}^{\hbar})_{\hbar>0}
be a bounded family of
L^{2}({\mathbb{E}})
. There exist a sequence
(\hbar_{k})_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}
in
(0,+\infty)

with
\hbar_{k}\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits_{k\rightarrow+\infty}0
and a unique element
\Gamma d\gamma\in\mathcal{M}_{ov}^{+}({\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}})
such that we have

\forall\sigma\in\mathcal{A}_{0},\ \left({\rm Op}_{\hbar^{k}}(\sigma)\psi_{0}^{\hbar_{k}},\psi_{0}^{\hbar_{k}}\right)_{L^{2}({\mathbb{E}})}\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits_{k\rightarrow+\infty}\int_{{\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}}{\rm Tr}\left(\sigma(x,\pi)\Gamma(x,\pi)\right)d\gamma(x,\pi).

Moreover,
\Gamma d\gamma
satisfies the following inequality

\int_{{\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}}{\rm Tr}\left(\Gamma(x,\pi)\right)d\gamma(x,\pi)\leq\limsup_{\hbar>0}\|\psi_{0}^{\hbar}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{E}})}^{2}.

Given the sequence
(\hbar_{k})_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}
,
\Gamma d\gamma
is the only operator-valued measure for which the above convergence holds.

Any equivalence class
\Gamma d\gamma
satisfying Theorem
3.5
is called a
semiclassical measure
of the family
(\psi_{0}^{\hbar})_{\hbar>0}
.

As the last inequality in Theorem
3.5
shows, a loss of mass is possible. It is in this context that the assumptions (
1.3
) of
\hbar
-oscillations on
a family
(\psi_{0}^{\hbar})_{\hbar>0}
becomes particularly relevant as it legitimates the use of our semiclassical pseudodifferential calculus to investigate the weak limits of
the probabilty densities
|\psi_{0}^{\hbar}(x)|^{2}dx
.

Proposition 3.6
.

Let
(\psi_{0}^{\hbar})_{\hbar>0}
be a
\hbar
-oscillating bounded family of
L^{2}({\mathbb{E}})
admitting a semiclassical measure
\Gamma d\gamma
for the sequence
(\hbar_{k})_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}
, then for
all
\phi\in C_{0}^{\infty}(G)

\lim_{k\rightarrow+\infty}\int_{{\mathbb{E}}}\phi(x)|\psi_{0}^{\hbar}(x)|^{2}dx=\int_{{\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}}\phi(x){\rm Tr}\left(\Gamma(x,\pi)\right)d\gamma(x,\pi).

3.2.3.
The sub-
C^{*}
-algebra
\mathcal{B}
and its topological dual

We introduce the subspace
\mathcal{B}_{0}({\mathbb{E}})
of
\mathcal{A}_{0}({\mathbb{E}})
of symbols commuting with
H
. This subspace is not trivial as it contains all symbols of the form

a(x)\chi(H)
,
a\in C_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{E}})
,
\chi\in\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{R}})
, by Hulanicki’s theorem (see
[
25
]
):

Theorem 3.7

(Hulanicki)
.

The convolution kernel of a spectral multiplier
\chi(\mathcal{R})
of a Rockland operator
\mathcal{R}
on nilpotent Lie group
G
for a Schwartz function
\chi\in\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{R}})
is Schwart on
G
.

We denote by
\mathcal{B}
the closure of
\mathcal{B}_{0}
for the norm
\|\cdot\|_{L^{\infty}({\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}})}
. We check readily that
\mathcal{B}
is a sub-
C^{*}
-alegbra of
\mathcal{A}
and that
\mathcal{B}_{0}=\mathcal{A}_{0}\cap\mathcal{B}
.
The next statement identifies the dual of the topological dual of
\mathcal{B}
.

Proposition 3.8

(
[
18
]
)
.

Via
\Gamma d\gamma\mapsto\mathchoice{{\ell_{\Gamma d\gamma}\,\smash{\vrule height=5.55557pt,depth=2.43195pt}}_{\,\mathcal{B}}}{{\ell_{\Gamma d\gamma}\,\smash{\vrule height=5.55557pt,depth=2.43195pt}}_{\,\mathcal{B}}}{{\ell_{\Gamma d\gamma}\,\smash{\vrule height=3.88889pt,depth=1.72832pt}}_{\,\mathcal{B}}}{{\ell_{\Gamma d\gamma}\,\smash{\vrule height=2.77777pt,depth=2.31387pt}}_{\,\mathcal{B}}}
, the topological dual
\mathcal{B}^{*}
of
\mathcal{B}
is isomorphic to the closed subspace
\mathcal{M}_{ov}({\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}})^{(H)}
of operator-valued measures

\Gamma d\gamma\in\mathcal{M}_{ov}({\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}})
such that the operator
\Gamma(x,\pi)
