commutes with the spectral projector
\Pi_{n}(\pi)
for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{>0}
and for
d\gamma
-almost every
(x,\pi)\in{\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}
.

Such a proposition allows us to use symbols only in
\mathcal{B}_{0}
to study our semiclassical measure once we have proved it belongs to the subspace
\mathcal{M}_{ov}({\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}})^{(H)}
.

3.2.4.
Time-averaged semiclassical measures

When investigating semiclassical measures associated to solutions of the Schrödinger equation (
1.2
), time-averaging allows one to make their study more tractable.
For this reason, for a bounded family
(\psi^{\hbar}(t))_{\hbar>0}
in
L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}_{t},L^{2}({\mathbb{E}}))
, we associate the quantities

(3.4)

\ell_{\hbar}(\theta,\sigma)=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}\theta(t)\left({\rm Op}_{\hbar}(\sigma)\psi^{\hbar}(t),\psi^{\hbar}(t)\right)_{L^{2}({\mathbb{E}})}dt,\;\;\sigma\in\mathcal{A}_{0},\;\;\theta\in L^{1}({\mathbb{R}}_{t}),

the limits of which are characterized by a map in
L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}_{t},{\mathcal{M}}_{ov}^{+}({\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}})
.

Theorem 3.9
.

Let
(\psi^{\hbar}(t))_{\hbar>0}
be a bounded family in
L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}_{t},L^{2}({\mathbb{E}}))
. There exists a sequence
(\hbar_{k})_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}
in
(0,+\infty)
with
\hbar_{k}\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits_{k\rightarrow+\infty}0

and a map
t\mapsto\Gamma_{t}d\gamma_{t}
in

L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}_{t},{\mathcal{M}}_{ov}^{+}({\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}))
such that we have for all
\theta\in L^{1}({\mathbb{R}}_{t})
and
\sigma\in{\mathcal{A}}
,

(3.5)

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}\theta(t)\left({\rm Op}_{\hbar_{k}}(\sigma)\psi^{\hbar_{k}}(t),\psi^{\hbar_{k}}(t)\right)_{L^{2}({\mathbb{E}})}dt\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits_{k\rightarrow+\infty}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}}\theta(t){\rm Tr}\left(\sigma(x,\pi)\Gamma_{t}(x,\pi)\right)d\gamma_{t}(x,\pi)dt.

Given the sequence
(\hbar_{k})_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}
,
the map
t\mapsto\Gamma_{t}d\gamma_{t}

is unique up to equivalence. Besides,

(3.6)

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}\int_{{\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}}{\rm Tr}\left(\Gamma_{t}(x,\pi)\right)d\gamma_{t}(x,\pi)\,dt\leq\limsup_{\hbar\rightarrow 0}\|\psi^{\hbar}\|_{L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},L^{2}({\mathbb{E}}))}.

Given the sequence
(\hbar_{k})_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}
,
t\mapsto\Gamma_{t}d\gamma_{t}
is the only operator-valued measure for which the above convergence holds.

We call the map
t\mapsto\Gamma_{t}d\gamma_{t}
satisfying Theorem
3.9
(for some subsequence
\hbar_{k}
) a
time-averaged semiclassical measure
of the family
(\psi^{\hbar}(t))
.
When this family is solution of a Schrödinger equation, we expect the time-averaged semiclassical measures to enjoy additionnal properties.

3.2.5.
Spectral projectors
\Pi_{n}
and approximation by smoothing symbols

In this section, we analyse the fields of the spectral projectors
\Pi_{n}
of
H
in the light of the elements of harmonic analysis and pseudodifferential calculus introduced above.
In contrast with the spectral projectors associated to the symbol of the subLaplacian on a H-type group, where we can prove their appartenance
in homogeneous regular symbol classes (see
[
16
]
,
[
17
]
), the fields
\Pi_{n}
do not enjoy the same property.

\Pi_{n}
is a measurable symbol, and even a 0-homogeneous element of
L^{\infty}(\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}})
, but the behaviour of the eigenvalues
(\mu_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{>0}}

at infinity is an obstruction to the boundedness of their derivatives (i.e with respect to the difference operators).

We can however approximate these spectral projectors by smoothing symbols in a simple way.

Proposition 3.10
.

Let
n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{>0}
and consider the spectral projector
\Pi_{n}\in L^{\infty}(\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}})
. Let
(\chi_{\epsilon})_{\epsilon\in(0,1)}
a family of functions in
\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{R}})
null in a neighborhood of 0 and converging uniformly on
any compact of
{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\{0\}
towards 1.
Then
(\chi_{\epsilon}(H)\Pi_{n})_{\epsilon\in(0,1)}
is a family of smoothing symbols bounded in
L^{\infty}(\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}})
, and the following convergence holds punctually almost everywhere on
\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}
:

\chi_{\epsilon}(H)\Pi_{n}\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\Pi_{n}.

Proof.

For
\epsilon>0
fixed, observe that

\chi_{\epsilon}(H)\Pi_{n}=\chi_{\epsilon}(\mu_{n})\Pi_{n},

and is thus supported inside a compact
K\subset\mathfrak{g}_{3}^{*}\setminus\{0\}\times\mathfrak{r}_{2}^{*}
: indeed, writing
\mu_{n}(\delta,\beta)=\delta^{2/3}\tilde{\mu}_{n}(\beta\delta^{-1/3})
and knowing that
\nu\in{\mathbb{R}}\mapsto\tilde{\mu}_{n}

diverges to
+\infty
when
\nu\rightarrow\pm\infty
, we conclude easily.

We would like to write
\Pi_{n}
on
K
as a Riesz projector of the symbol
H
.
As the graphs of the eigenvalues
(\mu_{m})_{m\in{\mathbb{N}}_{>0}}
are not flat, we decompose
\chi_{\epsilon}=\sum_{j=1}^{N_{\epsilon}}\chi_{\epsilon,j}^{2}
, with the supports of the
\chi_{\epsilon,j}
having a finite number of intersection
with one another and such that the
n
-th eigenvalue
\mu_{n}(\delta,\beta)
is at a distance
d>0
from the rest of the spectrum of
H(\pi^{\delta,\beta})
, uniformly on
K
.
We can then find a closed curve
\mathcal{C}_{n}^{j}
in
{\mathbb{C}}
for which we can write

\chi_{\epsilon,j}^{2}(H)\Pi_{n}=\chi_{\epsilon,j}(H)\int_{\mathcal{C}_{n}^{j}}\chi_{\epsilon,j}(\mu_{n})\left(H-z{\rm Id}\right)^{-1}dz.

Using this representation, we are going to estimate its seminorms introduced in Section
3.1.2
.
First notice that
\chi_{\epsilon,j}(H)
is a smoothing operator by Hulanicki’s theorem, and that the symbol
H
is in
\dot{S}^{2}({\mathbb{E}})
, i.e a regular 2-homogeneous symbol
in the sense of
[
15
, Definition 4.1]
. By Leibniz formula
2.10
, we can concentrate on the symbol given by the contour integral on
\mathcal{C}_{n}^{j}
.
First, for
\alpha=\beta=0
and for all
z\in\mathcal{C}_{n}^{j}
, we have

\sup_{\pi^{\delta,\beta}\in\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}_{\rm gen}}\left\|\chi_{\epsilon,j}(\mu_{n}(\delta,\beta))\left(H(\pi^{\delta,\beta})-z{\rm Id}\right)^{-1}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{H}_{\pi^{\delta,\beta}}\right)}\leq\frac{1}{d}<\infty,

so
\chi_{\epsilon,j}(\mu_{n})\left(H-z{\rm Id}\right)^{-1}
is in
L^{\infty}(\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}})
.

Now for
i\in\{1,2\}
, since
\Delta_{i}{\rm Id}=0
, the Leibniz formula
2.10
implies

\Delta_{i}\left(H(\pi^{\delta,\beta})-z{\rm Id}\right)^{-1}=-\left(H(\pi^{\delta,\beta})-z{\rm Id}\right)^{-1}\Delta_{i}H(\pi^{\delta,\beta})\left(H(\pi^{\delta,\beta})-z{\rm Id}\right)^{-1}.

So we have,

\Delta_{i}\left(\chi_{\epsilon,j}(\mu_{n}(\delta,\beta))\left(H(\pi^{\delta,\beta})-z{\rm Id}\right)^{-1}\right)=\Delta_{i}\left(\chi_{\epsilon,j}(\mu_{n}){\rm Id}\right)(\pi^{\delta,\beta})\left(H(\pi^{\delta,\beta})-z{\rm Id}\right)^{-1}\\
-\chi_{\epsilon,j}(\mu_{n}(\delta,\beta))\left(H(\pi^{\delta,\beta})-z{\rm Id}\right)^{-1}\Delta_{i}H(\pi^{\delta,\beta})\left(H(\pi^{\delta,\beta})-z{\rm Id}\right)^{-1}.

The first term in the right-hand side is treated in the same way as the seminorm estimate for
\alpha=\beta=0
, since the difference operators act on
\chi_{\epsilon,j}(\mu_{n})
only by differentiation, and we conclude by the regularity of the eigenvalue and the support condition. For the second term,
observe that the symbol
\Delta_{i}H
is in
\dot{S}^{1}({\mathbb{E}})
and by
[
15
, Proposition 4.2]
, its composition with
\chi_{\epsilon,j}(H)
gives a smoothing operator.
We estimate the norms of the resolvents as previously thanks to the support condition of
\chi_{\epsilon,j}(\mu_{n})
, and we obtain boundedness in
L^{\infty}(\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}})
.

For
i\in\{3,4\}
, the Leibniz formula is more involved in the sense that terms involving difference operators of lower order terms appear, but are estimated thanks to the previous computation.
We prove recursively this way that
\chi_{\epsilon,i}^{2}(H)\Pi_{n}
is a symbol in
S^{0}({\mathbb{E}})
.

To prove that this symbol is in
S^{-\infty}({\mathbb{E}})
, we just observe that it would be equivalent to prove that, for
m\in{\mathbb{R}}
, the symbol

\chi_{\epsilon,j}(\mu_{n})\chi_{\epsilon,j}(H)({\rm Id+H})^{m}\left(H-z{\rm Id}\right)^{-1}
is in
S^{0}
: the proof is the same as
\chi_{\epsilon,j}(H)({\rm Id}+H)^{m}=\chi_{\epsilon,j}^{m}(H)
with
\chi_{\epsilon,j}^{m}(\lambda)=\chi_{\epsilon,j}(\lambda)(1+\lambda)^{m}
and our analysis holds
by similar support considerations.
∎

The previous proposition allows us to localize on the eigenspaces of
H
through smoothing operators. More precisely, for
\sigma\in\mathcal{A}_{0}
a smoothing symbol, we can
then define the symbol
\sigma^{(n,n^{\prime})}:=\Pi_{n}\sigma\Pi_{n^{\prime}}
for each
n,n^{\prime}
.
We see that for
n=n^{\prime}
,
\sigma^{(n,n^{\prime})}
commutes with
H
, while for general pairs of integers
(n,n^{\prime})
, it satisfies

H\circ\sigma^{(n,n^{\prime})}=\mu_{n}\sigma^{(n,n^{\prime})}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\sigma^{(n,n^{\prime})}\circ H=\mu_{n^{\prime}}\sigma^{(n,n^{\prime})}.

However,
\sigma^{(n,n^{\prime})}
is usually not smoothing, but can by approximated nicely by symbols of
\mathcal{A}_{0}
.

Proposition 3.11
.

Let
\sigma\in\mathcal{A}_{0}({\mathbb{E}})
. With the same notations as in Proposition
3.10
, for
n,n^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{>0}
, the sequence of symbols
(\sigma_{\epsilon}^{(n,n^{\prime})})_{\epsilon>0}
defined by

\sigma_{\epsilon}^{(n,n^{\prime})}=\chi_{\epsilon}(H)\Pi_{n}\sigma\Pi_{n}^{\prime}\chi_{\epsilon}(H),

consists of symbols in
\mathcal{A}_{0}
uniformly bounded in
L^{\infty}(\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}})
and converging towards
\sigma^{(n,n^{\prime})}
almost everywhere on
{\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}
.

As proofs will make it clear, we sometimes need to work with symbols vanishing uniformly in a neighborhood of
\{\delta=0\}
. The following proposition allows us to do so.

Proposition 3.12
.

Let
\sigma\in\mathcal{A}_{0}({\mathbb{E}})
.

(1)

For any
g\in C_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}\setminus\{0\})
, the symbol given by
(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta})\in{\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}{\rm gen}\mapsto g(\delta)\sigma(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta})
is in
\mathcal{A}_{0}
.

(2)

Let
(g_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\subset C_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}\setminus\{0\})
be a sequence of smooth functions bounded by 1 and converging to the constant 1 uniformly on any compact of
{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\{0\}
. Then the
sequence of smooth symbols
(g_{n}\sigma(x,\cdot))_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
converges to
\sigma(x,\cdot)
in
L^{\infty}(\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}})
uniformly in
x\in{\mathbb{E}}
.

Proof.

Let
\kappa_{\sigma}
be the kernels associated to the symbols which may be identified with a map in
C_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{E}},\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{E}}))
. Part (1) follows from the kernel
\kappa_{g}
associated to the symbol
g(\delta
) being the central
distribution
\delta_{(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})=(0,0,0)}\otimes\mathcal{F}_{{\mathbb{R}}}^{-1}g
and the kernel associated to
g(\delta)\sigma
being
\kappa_{\sigma}*\kappa_{g}
. Part (2) follows from the Lebesgue dominated convergence theorem and the Plancherel formula.
∎

3.3.
Propagation of semiclassical measures at the semiclassical time-scale

We investigate here the propagation properties of the time-averaged semiclassical measures associated to a family
(\psi^{\hbar}(t))_{\hbar>0}
of solutions to the Schrödinger equation for the semiclassical time-scale:

(3.7)

\begin{cases}&i\hbar\partial_{t}\psi^{\hbar}=-\hbar^{2}\Delta_{{\mathbb{E}}}\psi^{\hbar},\\
&\psi_{|t=0}^{\hbar}=\psi_{0}^{\hbar}.\end{cases}

Our main result is the following one:

Theorem 3.13
.

Let
(\psi_{0}^{\hbar})_{\hbar>0}
be a bounded family in
L^{2}({\mathbb{E}})
and
\psi^{\hbar}(t)={\rm e}^{i\hbar t\Delta_{{\mathbb{E}}}}\psi^{\hbar}_{0}
be the solution to (
3.7
).
Then any time-averaged semiclassical measure

t\mapsto\Gamma_{t}d\gamma_{t}\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}_{t},{\mathcal{M}}_{ov}^{+}({\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}))
for the family
(\psi^{\hbar}(t))_{\hbar>0}
satisfies the following additional properties:

(i)

For almost every
(t,x,\pi)\in{\mathbb{R}}_{t}\times{\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}
, the operator
\Gamma_{t}(x,\pi)
commutes with
\mathcal{F}(-\Delta_{{\mathbb{E}}})(\pi)=H(\pi)
.

(ii)

For a generic representation
\pi^{\delta,\beta}
,
(\delta,\beta)\in\mathfrak{g}_{3}^{*}\setminus\{0\}\times\mathfrak{r}_{2}^{*}
, we have the following decomposition

(3.8)

\Gamma_{t}(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta})=\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\Gamma_{n,t}(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta})\;\;{with}\;\;\Gamma_{n,t}(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta}):=\Pi_{n}(\pi^{\delta,\beta})\Gamma_{t}(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta})\Pi_{n}(\pi^{\delta,\beta}).

Moreover for each
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, the operator-valued measure
d\gamma_{n,t}(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta})=\Gamma_{n,t}(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta})d\gamma_{t}(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta})
on
{\mathbb{R}}_{t}\times{\mathbb{E}}\times(\mathfrak{g}_{3}^{*}\setminus\{0\}\times\mathfrak{r}_{2}^{*})
is a weak solution of the following transport equation:

\left(\partial_{t}-\partial_{\beta}\mu_{n}(\delta,\beta)X_{2}\right)\left(d\gamma_{n,t}(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta})\right)=0.

(iii)

On the non-generic part of the dual, i.e above
\delta=0
, the semiclassical measure splits into two parts. The first one corresponds to the one supported in the Schrödinger representations

\{\pi^{\lambda}\,:\,\lambda\in\mathfrak{g}_{2}^{*}\setminus\{0\}\}
, that we denote by
\bm{1}_{\delta=0,\gamma\neq 0}\Gamma_{t}d\gamma_{t}
and is invariant with respect to time:

\partial_{t}\left(\bm{1}_{\delta=0,\gamma\neq 0}\Gamma_{t}(x,\pi^{\lambda})d\gamma_{t}(x,\pi^{\lambda})\right)=0.

The second part is supported in the finite dimensional ones
\{\pi^{(0,\alpha)}\,:\,\alpha\in\mathfrak{g}_{1}^{*}\}
, that we denote by
\bm{1}_{\delta=0,\gamma=0}\Gamma_{t}d\gamma_{t}
, and satisfies the following
transport equation:

(\partial_{t}-\alpha\cdot(X_{1},X_{2}))\left(\bm{1}_{\delta=0,\gamma=0}\Gamma_{t}(x,\pi^{(0,\alpha)})d\gamma_{t}(x,\pi^{(0,\alpha)})\right)=0.

This result extends Theorem
1.2
as it also covers the propagation of the part of the time-averaged semiclassical measure supported in the non-generic representations. Unsurprisingly, we find the same
propagation laws that have been found in
[
17
]
for the time-averaged semiclassical measures on the Heisenberg group: we find that for the finite dimensional representations, the propagation is given by
the Hamiltonian flow with impulsion in the dual of the first stratum
\mathfrak{g}_{1}^{*}
, while for the Schrödinger representations
\{\pi^{\lambda}\,:\,\lambda\in\mathfrak{g}_{2}^{*}\setminus\{0\}\}
the
semiclassical measures are invariant in time.

In order to be able to determine fully the time-averaged semiclassical measures of solutions to (
3.7
), we need the following result concerning their continuity with respect to the time variable.

Proposition 3.14
.

Consider as in Theorem
3.13
the time-averaged semiclassical measure
t\mapsto\Gamma_{t}d\gamma_{t}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{ov}^{+}({\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}})

corresponding to the family of solutions to the Schrödinger equation (
3.7
). Then for any symbol
\sigma\in\mathcal{B}_{0}({\mathbb{E}})
, i.e commuting
with the field of
H
, the map

t\in{\mathbb{R}}\mapsto\int_{{\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}}{\rm Tr}\left(\sigma(x,\pi)\Gamma_{t}(x,\pi)\right)d\gamma_{t}(x,\pi),

is locally Lipshitz on
{\mathbb{R}}
.

3.4.
Proof of Theorem
3.13
and Proposition
1.12

Let
(\psi_{0}^{\hbar})_{\hbar>0}
a bounded family in
L^{2}({\mathbb{E}})
and write
\psi^{\hbar}(t)=e^{it\hbar\Delta_{\mathbb{E}}}\psi_{0}^{\hbar}
for
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
. We suppose given a time-averaged semiclassical measure

t\mapsto\Gamma_{t}d\gamma_{t}
of the family
(\psi^{\hbar}(t))_{\hbar>0}
as in Theorem
3.9
. In what follows we will make the abuse of writing
\hbar\rightarrow 0
instead of taking the limit along the subsequence
defining
\Gamma_{t}d\gamma_{t}
. We also introduce the following notation:

\forall\theta\in C_{c}({\mathbb{R}}_{t}),\,\forall\sigma\in\mathcal{A}_{0},\ \ell_{\infty}(\theta,\sigma)=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}\theta(t)\int_{{\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}}{\rm Tr}\left(\sigma(x,\pi)\Gamma_{t}(x,\pi)\right)d\gamma_{t}(x,\pi)dt.

3.4.1.
Proof of Theorem
3.13
(i)

We take
\sigma\in{\mathcal{A}}_{0}
.
The Schrödinger equation (
3.7
) yields

i\hbar\frac{d}{dt}\left({\rm Op}_{\hbar}(\sigma)\psi^{\hbar}(t),\psi^{\hbar}(t)\right)_{L^{2}({\mathbb{E}})}=\left(\left[{\rm Op}_{\hbar}(\sigma),-\hbar^{2}\Delta_{{\mathbb{E}}}\right]\psi^{\hbar}(t),\psi^{\hbar}(t)\right)_{L^{2}({\mathbb{E}})}.

By use of the symbolic calculus given by (
3.3
), we obtain

\displaystyle\left({\rm Op}_{\hbar}\left(\left[\sigma,H\right]\right)\psi^{\hbar}(t),\psi^{\hbar}(t)\right)_{L^{2}({\mathbb{E}})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\left[{\rm Op}_{\hbar}(\sigma),-\hbar^{2}\Delta_{{\mathbb{E}}}\right]\psi^{\hbar}(t),\psi^{\hbar}(t)\right)_{L^{2}({\mathbb{E}})}+\mathcal{O}(\hbar)+\mathcal{O}(\hbar)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle i\hbar\frac{d}{dt}\left({\rm Op}_{\hbar}(\sigma)\psi^{\hbar}(t),\psi^{\hbar}(t)\right)_{L^{2}({\mathbb{E}})}+\mathcal{O}(\hbar).

Therefore, for
\theta\in\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}_{t})
, by integration by part, we find

(3.9)

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}\theta(t)\left({\rm Op}_{\hbar}\left([\sigma,H]\right)\psi^{\hbar}(t),\psi^{\hbar}(t)\right)_{L^{2}({\mathbb{E}})}dt=\mathcal{O}(\hbar).

Taking the limit as
\hbar\to 0
in (
3.9
), we obtain

(3.10)

\ell_{\infty}(\theta,[\sigma,H])=0.

Using Proposition
3.11
, we can apply this to the smoothing symbol
\sigma_{\epsilon}^{(n,n^{\prime})}
for some
\epsilon\in(0,1)
and
n,n^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{>0}
:
by properties of the trace, we see that for
\pi^{\delta,\beta}\in\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}_{\rm gen}
,

{\rm Tr}\left([\sigma_{\epsilon}^{(n,n^{\prime})}(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta}),H(\pi^{\delta,\beta})]\,\Gamma_{t}(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta})\right)={\rm Tr}\Bigl(\sigma(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta})\mu_{n^{\prime}}(\delta,\beta)\chi_{\epsilon}(H)\Pi_{n^{\prime}}(\pi^{\delta,\beta})\Gamma_{t}(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta})\\
-\mu_{n}(\delta,\beta)\chi_{\epsilon}(H)\Pi_{n}(\pi^{\delta,\beta})\sigma(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta})\Gamma_{t}(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta})\Bigr)\\
={\rm Tr}\left(\sigma(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta})[H,\chi_{\epsilon}(H)\Pi_{n^{\prime}}(\pi^{\delta,\beta})\Gamma_{t}(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta})\chi_{\epsilon}(H)\Pi_{n}(\pi^{\delta,\beta})]\right).

and we deduce that for any
\sigma\in\mathcal{A}_{0}
,

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}\theta(t)\int_{{\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}}{\rm Tr}\left(\sigma(x,\pi)[H(\pi),\chi_{\epsilon}(H)\Pi_{n^{\prime}}(\pi)\Gamma_{t}(x,\pi)\chi_{\epsilon}(H)\Pi_{n}(\pi)]\right)d\gamma_{t}(x,\pi)dt=0.

This implies that
the element in
\mathcal{M}_{ov}({\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}})
given by

[H(\pi)\,,\,\chi_{\epsilon}(H)\Pi_{n^{\prime}}(\pi)\Gamma_{t}(x,\pi)\chi_{\epsilon}(H)\Pi_{n}(\pi)]\,d\gamma_{t}(x,\pi)dt

is zero. For any pair of integers with
n\not=n^{\prime}
,
taking
\epsilon\to 0
, this implies that

\Pi_{n^{\prime}}\Gamma_{t}\Pi_{n}=0

for
d\gamma_{t}dt
-almost every
(t,x,\pi)\in{\mathbb{R}}_{t}\times{\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}
. This shows Point (i) of Theorem
3.13
.

For the first claim of Point (ii), as
\Gamma_{t}
is a positive compact operator, the spectral decomposition (
3.8
) follows easily.

3.4.2.
A more precise computation

We write down more precisely the equation that (
3.3
) allows us to get:

\displaystyle i\hbar\frac{d}{dt}\left({\rm Op}_{\hbar}(\sigma)\psi^{\hbar}(t),\psi^{\hbar}(t)\right)

\displaystyle=\left({\rm Op}_{\hbar}\left(\left[\sigma,H\right]\right)\psi^{\hbar}(t),\psi^{\hbar}(t)\right)+2\hbar\left({\rm Op}_{\hbar}\left((\pi(V)\cdot V)\sigma\right)\psi^{\hbar}(t),\psi^{\hbar}(t)\right)

\displaystyle+\hbar^{2}\left({\rm Op}_{\hbar}\left(\Delta_{{\mathbb{E}}}\sigma\right)\psi^{\hbar}(t),\psi^{\hbar}(t)\right).

Using the notation
\ell_{\hbar}(\theta,\sigma)
introduced in (
3.4
), we can rewrite the previous equation more concisely after integrating against
\theta
:

(3.11)

-i\hbar\ell_{\hbar}(\theta^{\prime},\sigma)=\ell_{\hbar}(\theta,\left[\sigma,H\right])+2\hbar\,\ell_{\hbar}(\theta,(\pi(V)\cdot V)\sigma)+\hbar^{2}\ell_{\hbar}(\theta,\Delta_{{\mathbb{E}}}\sigma).

To be able to see the propagation, we now use a symbol
\sigma\in\mathcal{B}_{0}
: the previous equation then becomes

(3.12)

-\ell_{\hbar}(\theta^{\prime},\sigma)=-2i\ell_{\hbar}(\theta,(\pi(V)\cdot V)\sigma)+\mathcal{O}(\hbar).

We have already observed that for any symbol
\tilde{\sigma}\in\mathcal{A}_{0}
, we have

\ell_{\hbar}(\theta,[\tilde{\sigma},H])=\mathcal{O}(\hbar).

This property leads us to decompose the symbol
(\pi(V)\cdot V)\sigma
as the sum of its
H
-diagonal part and its
H
-off-diagonal one:
the off-diagonal part will write itself as a commutator with
H
and thus will not be preserved in the limit
\hbar\rightarrow 0
,
while the diagonal one will give us the infinitesimal generator of the flow along which each measure
\gamma_{n,t}=\Gamma_{n,t}d\gamma_{t}
,
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, propagates.

As the test symbol
\sigma
has been in chosen in
\mathcal{B}_{0}
, we are left with decomposing the operators
\pi^{\delta,\beta}(X_{1})

and
\pi^{\delta,\beta}(X_{2})
. But first recall that by analyticity of the family of operators
(H(\pi^{\delta,\beta}))_{(\delta,\beta)\in\mathfrak{g}_{3}^{*}\setminus\{0\}\times\mathfrak{r}_{2}^{*}}

and by differentiating with respect to the variable
\beta
, we have the following so-called Feynmann-Helmann formula:

Lemma 3.15
.

For
n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{>0}
, we have

(3.13)

(H-\mu_{n}\,{\rm Id})\partial_{\beta}\Pi_{n}=(\partial_{\beta}\mu_{n}\,{\rm Id}-\partial_{\beta}H)\Pi_{n}.

Remark 3.16
.

We observe easily that, as operators on the real line
{\mathbb{R}}_{\xi}
, we have

\partial_{\beta}H(\pi^{\delta,\beta})=2\left(\beta+\frac{\delta}{2}\xi^{2}\right)=-2i\pi^{\delta,\beta}(X_{2}).

Lemma 3.17
.

Let
(\delta,\beta)\in\mathfrak{g}_{3}^{*}\setminus\{0\}\times\mathfrak{r}_{2}^{*}
. The operator
\pi^{\delta,\beta}(X_{1})
is entirely off-diagonal with respect to
H(\pi^{\delta,\beta})
:

(3.14)

\pi^{\delta,\beta}(X_{1})=\left[\frac{1}{2i\delta}\pi^{\delta,\beta}(X_{3}),H(\pi^{\delta,\beta})\right],

while
\pi^{\delta,\beta}(X_{2})
decomposes as follows:

(3.15)

\pi^{\delta,\beta}(X_{2})=\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{>0}}\frac{i}{2}\partial_{\beta}\mu_{n}(\delta,\beta)\Pi_{n}(\pi^{\delta,\beta})+\left[\frac{i}{2}\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{>0}}\partial_{\beta}\Pi_{n}(\pi^{\delta,\beta})\Pi_{n}(\pi^{\delta,\beta}),H(\pi^{\delta,\beta})\right].

Proof.

Relation (
3.14
) is a direct consequence of the following computation in the enveloping algebra of
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{E}}
:

[X_{3},X_{1}^{2}+X_{2}^{2}]=2X_{1}X_{4}.

To prove (
3.15
), observe that Lemma
3.15
allows us to write for
n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{>0}
and
\pi^{\delta,\beta}\in\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}
,

-2i\pi^{\delta,\beta}(X_{2})\Pi_{n}(\pi^{\delta,\beta})=\partial_{\beta}\mu_{n}(\delta,\beta)\Pi_{n}(\pi^{\delta,\beta})+(\mu_{n}(\delta,\beta)\,{\rm Id}-H(\pi^{\delta,\beta}))\partial_{\beta}\Pi_{n}(\pi^{\delta,\beta}).

Composing to the left by
\Pi_{n}(\pi^{\delta,\beta})
, we obtain

\Pi_{n}(\pi^{\delta,\beta})\pi^{\delta,\beta}(X_{2})\Pi_{n}(\pi^{\delta,\beta})=\frac{i}{2}\partial_{\beta}\mu_{n}(\delta,\beta)\Pi_{n}(\pi^{\delta,\beta}),

giving us the expected
H
-diagonal part of
\pi^{\delta,\beta}(X_{2})
.

For the
H
-off-diagonal one, observe that for
n^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{>0}
with
n^{\prime}\neq n
, composing to the left by
\Pi_{n^{\prime}}(\pi^{\delta,\beta})
, we obtain this time

(3.16)

\Pi_{n^{\prime}}(\pi^{\delta,\beta})\pi^{\delta,\beta}(X_{2})\Pi_{n}(\pi^{\delta,\beta})=(\mu_{n}(\delta,\beta)-\mu_{n^{\prime}}(\delta,\beta))\Pi_{n^{\prime}}(\pi^{\delta,\beta})\partial_{\beta}\Pi_{n}(\pi^{\delta,\beta}).

We are left with finding a field of operator
C
such that
\sum_{n^{\prime}\neq n}\Pi_{n^{\prime}}\pi(X_{2})\Pi_{n}=[C,H]
.
This field
C
must then necessarily satisfy

\Pi_{n^{\prime}}(\pi^{\delta,\beta})\pi^{\delta,\beta}(X_{2})\Pi_{n}(\pi^{\delta,\beta})=(\mu_{n}(\delta,\beta)-\mu^{n^{\prime}}(\delta,\beta))\Pi_{n^{\prime}}(\pi^{\delta,\beta})C(\pi^{\delta,\beta})\Pi_{n}(\pi^{\delta,\beta}),

but confronting this equation with (
3.16
), we deduce that we can take the field
C
as follows

C=\frac{i}{2}\sum_{n^{\prime}\neq n}\Pi_{n^{\prime}}\partial_{\beta}\Pi_{n}\Pi_{n}=\frac{i}{2}\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{>0}}\partial_{\beta}\Pi_{n}\Pi_{n},

last equality following from the fact that the
(\Pi_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{>0}}
are fields of projectors, and as such satisfy
\Pi_{n^{\prime}}\partial_{\beta}\Pi_{n}\Pi_{n}=\partial_{\beta}\Pi_{n}\Pi_{n}

for
n^{\prime}\neq n
.
∎

Accordingly, if we choose
\sigma
to be concentrated on a particular eigenspace of
H
, meaning that for a certain
n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{>0}
we have
\sigma=\Pi_{n}\sigma\Pi_{n}
, and null in a neighborhood of
\{\delta=0\}
,
we obtain

-i(\pi(V)\cdot V)\sigma(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta})=\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\beta}\mu_{n}(\delta,\beta)X_{2}\sigma(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta})+\frac{1}{2}\left[\sigma_{1}(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta}),H(\pi^{\delta,\beta})\right],

with

(3.17)

\sigma_{1}=-\frac{1}{\delta}\pi^{\delta,\beta}(X_{3})X_{1}\sigma+\partial_{\beta}\Pi_{n}(\pi^{\delta,\beta})X_{2}\sigma\in\mathcal{A}_{0}.

Equation (
3.12
) then can be rewritten as

-\ell_{\hbar}(\theta^{\prime},\sigma)=\ell_{\hbar}\left(\theta,\partial_{\beta}\mu_{n}X_{2}\sigma\right)+\mathcal{O}(\hbar),

which gives, when
\hbar
goes to 0,

-\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}\theta^{\prime}(t)\int_{{\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}_{\rm gen}}{\rm Tr}\left(\sigma(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta})\Gamma_{n,t}(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta})\right)d\gamma_{t}(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta})dt=\\
\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}\theta(t)\int_{{\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}_{\rm gen}}{\rm Tr}\left(\partial_{\beta}\mu_{n}(\delta,\beta)X_{2}\sigma(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta})\Gamma_{n,t}(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta})\right)d\gamma_{t}(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta})dt.

We conclude by Proposition
3.8
and Point (ii) of Theorem
3.13
.

3.4.3.
Proof of Proposition
3.14

The following argument can already be found in
[
17
]
. For any symbol
\sigma\in\mathcal{B}_{0}({\mathbb{E}})
, we write

\ell_{\hbar,t}(\sigma)=\left({\rm Op}_{\hbar}(\sigma)\psi^{\hbar}(t),\psi^{\hbar}(t)\right)_{L^{2}({\mathbb{E}})}.

Observe that by Equation (
3.12
), we have

\frac{d}{dt}\ell_{\hbar,t}(\sigma)=\mathcal{O}_{\sigma}(1),

in the sense that the map
t\mapsto\frac{d}{dt}\ell_{\hbar,t}(\sigma)
is uniformly bounded in a given bounded interval of
{\mathbb{R}}
and this uniformly for
\hbar\in(0,1)
.
Thus the family of maps
(\ell_{\hbar,\cdot}(\sigma))_{\hbar>0}
is bounded and equicontinuous in
C_{b}({\mathbb{R}})
, so that by Arzelà-Ascoli theorem we can extract
a subsequence
(\hbar_{j_{k}})_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}
such that, as
k\rightarrow+\infty
,
\hbar_{j_{k}}\rightarrow 0
and
(\ell_{\hbar_{j_{k}},\cdot}(\sigma))_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}
converges uniformly
on any bounded interval to a locally Lipshitz function
t\mapsto\ell_{t}(\sigma)
for all
\sigma\in\mathcal{B}_{0}({\mathbb{E}})
(this last assertion follows from considering a dense
subset of such symbols and a diagonal extraction procedure).

It is straightforward to see that for
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
\ell_{t}
defines a state on the
C^{*}
-algebra
\mathcal{B}
. By Proposition
3.8
, it is thus given by
an operator-valued measure
\Gamma^{\ell_{t}}d\gamma^{\ell_{t}}
in
\mathcal{M}_{ov}^{+}({\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}})^{(H)}
. By Theorem
3.9
and by the property of uniform convergence on any bounded interval,
we conclude that the map
t\in{\mathbb{R}}\mapsto\Gamma^{\ell_{t}}d\gamma^{\ell_{t}}
necessarily coincides with the time-averaged semiclassical measure
t\in{\mathbb{R}}\mapsto\Gamma_{t}d\gamma_{t}
and the proposition is proved.

3.4.4.
Proof of Point (iii)

We are interested here with the part of the semiclassical measure supported in the non-generic representations
\widehat{\bar{{\mathbb{H}}}}
. We will denote by
{\bm{1}}_{\widehat{\bar{{\mathbb{H}}}}}\Gamma_{t}d\gamma_{t}
the restrictions
of the operator-valued measures which can be seen as elements of
\mathcal{M}_{ov}^{+}({\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{\bar{{\mathbb{H}}}})
. More precisely, as Point (i) of Theorem
3.13
has been proved, the restriction of the
time-averaged semiclassical measure to
{\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{\bar{{\mathbb{H}}}}
is in
L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}_{t},\mathcal{M}_{ov}^{+}({\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{\bar{{\mathbb{H}}}})^{(H)})
, where the we restrict the symbol
H
to the non-generic representations.
As such we can only deal with symbols commuting with
H
.

To be able to study this restricted measure, we need to enlarge our set of test symbols. To this end we introduce the closures
vN\mathcal{A}
and
vN\mathcal{B}
of the
C^{*}
-algebras
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
respectively for the strong operator topology in
L^{\infty}({\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}})
. They correspond to the von Neumann algebras
generated by
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
respectively and are contained in
L^{\infty}({\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}})
. The semiclassical measures
\Gamma_{t}d\gamma_{t}\in\mathcal{A}^{*}
each have a unique linear continous extension to
vN\mathcal{A}
, meaning it makes sense to
integrate these measures on elements of this von Neumann algebra.

Lemma 3.18
.

If
\sigma\in\mathcal{A}_{0}
, then
{\bm{1}}_{\widehat{\bar{{\mathbb{H}}}}}\sigma
is in
vN\mathcal{A}
. Similarly, if
\sigma\in\mathcal{B}_{0}
, then
{\bm{1}}_{\widehat{\bar{{\mathbb{H}}}}}\sigma
is in
vN\mathcal{B}
. Moreover, for
\sigma\in\mathcal{B}_{0}
, there exists a family
(\sigma_{\epsilon})_{\epsilon\in(0,1)}
of commuting symbols in
\mathcal{B}_{0}
such that

\sigma_{\epsilon}
and
(\pi(V)\cdot V)\sigma_{\epsilon}
converge to
{\bm{1}}_{\widehat{\bar{{\mathbb{H}}}}}\sigma
and
{\bm{1}}_{\widehat{\bar{{\mathbb{H}}}}}(\pi(V)\cdot V)\sigma
respectively in
vN\mathcal{B}
and
vN\mathcal{A}
, as
\epsilon
goes to 0.

Proof.

Let
\sigma\in\mathcal{B}_{0}
and
\eta\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathfrak{g}_{3}^{*})
satisfying
\eta(0)=1
. Writing
\kappa
the convolution kernel of
\sigma
, one easily check that
the symbol given by

\forall(x,\pi)\in{\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}_{\rm gen},\ (\sigma\eta)(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta})=\sigma(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta})\eta(\delta),

is in
\mathcal{B}_{0}
as its convolution kernel is given by
(x,y)\in{\mathbb{E}}^{2}\mapsto(\kappa_{x}(y_{1},y_{2},y_{3},\cdot)*_{\mathfrak{g}_{3}}\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{g}_{3}}^{-1}\eta)(y_{4})
.
Introducing for
\epsilon\in(0,1)
the function
\eta_{\epsilon}(\cdot)=\eta\left(\frac{\cdot}{\epsilon}\right)
, we consider the family of symbols given by

\sigma_{\epsilon}=\sigma\eta_{\epsilon}
as above. We check readily that the limit of
\sigma_{\epsilon}
for the strong operator topology is given by
{\rm 1}_{\widehat{\bar{{\mathbb{H}}}}}\sigma
.
The same result holds for
(\pi(V)\cdot V)\sigma
.
∎

Thus, for
\sigma\in\mathcal{A}_{0}
, the following equation makes sense:

\ell_{\infty}(\theta,{\bm{1}}_{\widehat{\bar{{\mathbb{H}}}}}\sigma)=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}\theta(t)\int_{{\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{\bar{{\mathbb{H}}}}}{\rm Tr}\left(\sigma(x,\pi)\Gamma_{t}(x,\pi)\right)d\gamma_{t}(x,\pi)dt.

However, if the previous lemma allow us to consider more test symbols than those given by the
C^{*}
-algebras
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
, we need to check that there are enough
of them to fully characterize the restricted measures
{\bm{1}}_{\widehat{\bar{{\mathbb{H}}}}}\Gamma_{t}d\gamma_{t}\in\mathcal{M}_{ov}^{+}({\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{\bar{{\mathbb{H}}}})^{(H)}
.
We will note
\mathcal{A}_{0}({\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{\bar{{\mathbb{H}}}})
the space of fields of operators
\sigma=\{\sigma(x,\pi)\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\pi})\,:\,(x,\pi)\in{\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{\bar{{\mathbb{H}}}}\}

given by the Fourier transform of kernels
\kappa
in
C_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{E}},\mathcal{S}(\bar{{\mathbb{H}}}))
:

\sigma(x,\pi)=\mathcal{F}_{\bar{{\mathbb{H}}}}\kappa_{x}(\pi).

We will denote by
\mathcal{A}({\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{\bar{{\mathbb{H}}}})
the closure of
\mathcal{A}_{0}({\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{\bar{{\mathbb{H}}}})
for the norm
\|\cdot\|_{L^{\infty}({\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{\bar{{\mathbb{H}}}})}
.
We define the spaces
\mathcal{B}_{0}({\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{\bar{{\mathbb{H}}}})
and
\mathcal{B}({\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{\bar{{\mathbb{H}}}})
in the obvious way, similarly as the definition of the spaces
\mathcal{B}_{0}
and
\mathcal{B}
.

Lemma 3.19
.

[
18
, Lemma 4.3]

The map
\Theta:\sigma\in\mathcal{A}_{0}({\mathbb{E}})\mapsto\mathchoice{{\sigma\,\smash{\vrule height=3.44444pt,depth=3.91948pt}}_{\,{\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{\bar{{\mathbb{H}}}}}}{{\sigma\,\smash{\vrule height=3.44444pt,depth=3.91948pt}}_{\,{\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{\bar{{\mathbb{H}}}}}}{{\sigma\,\smash{\vrule height=2.41112pt,depth=4.79782pt}}_{\,{\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{\bar{{\mathbb{H}}}}}}{{\sigma\,\smash{\vrule height=1.72221pt,depth=5.38339pt}}_{\,{\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{\bar{{\mathbb{H}}}}}}\in\mathcal{A}_{0}({\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{\bar{{\mathbb{H}}}})
is surjective and extends
to a
C^{*}
-algebra morphism from
\mathcal{A}({\mathbb{E}})
onto
\mathcal{A}({\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{\bar{{\mathbb{H}}}})
. The restriction of this map to
\mathcal{B}({\mathbb{E}})
is also surjective on

\mathcal{B}({\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{\bar{{\mathbb{H}}}})
.

Now, for
\sigma\in\mathcal{B}_{0}({\mathbb{E}})
, Equation (
3.12
) gives in the limit
\hbar
goes to 0,

-\ell_{\infty}(\theta^{\prime},\sigma)=-2i\ell_{\hbar}(\theta,(\pi(V)\cdot V)\sigma).

Using the family of symbols
(\sigma_{\epsilon})_{\epsilon\in(0,1)}
introduced in Lemma
3.18
, we obtain

-\ell_{\infty}(\theta^{\prime},{\bm{1}}_{\widehat{\bar{{\mathbb{H}}}}}\sigma)=-2i\ell_{\hbar}(\theta,{\bm{1}}_{\widehat{\bar{{\mathbb{H}}}}}(\pi(V)\cdot V)\sigma),

or more explicitly

-\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}\theta^{\prime}(t)\int_{{\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{\bar{{\mathbb{H}}}}}{\rm Tr}\left(\sigma(x,\pi)\Gamma_{n,t}(x,\pi)\right)d\gamma_{t}(x,\pi)dt=\\
\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}\theta(t)\int_{{\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{\bar{{\mathbb{H}}}}}{\rm Tr}\left((\pi(V)\cdot V)\sigma(x,\pi)\Gamma_{n,t}(x,\pi)\right)d\gamma_{t}(x,\pi)dt.

We have reduced the study of the propagation of the restriction of the semiclassical measure to the non-generic representation to the case of the Heisenberg group
\bar{{\mathbb{H}}}
. Point (iii) of Theorem
3.13
then
follows from
[
17
, Theorem 2.10]
.

3.4.5.
Proof of Proposition
1.12

As the scheme of the proof is similar as the one presented above, we investigate here the properties of the time-averaged semiclassical measures
of a family of solutions, still denoted by
(\psi^{\hbar}(t))_{\hbar>0}
, for the Schrödinger equation with time-scale
\tau=2
:

\forall t\in{\mathbb{R}},\ \psi^{\hbar}(t)=e^{it\Delta_{\mathbb{E}}}\psi_{0}^{\hbar},

for
(\psi_{0}^{\hbar})_{\hbar>0}
a bounded family in
L^{2}({\mathbb{E}})
. We still denote by
\ell_{\hbar}
,
\hbar>0
, the following quantities

\forall\theta\in C_{c}({\mathbb{R}}_{t}),\forall\sigma\in\mathcal{A}_{0}({\mathbb{E}}),\ \ell_{\hbar}(\theta,\sigma)=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}\theta(t)\left({\rm Op}_{\hbar}(\sigma)\psi^{\hbar}(t),\psi^{\hbar}(t)\right)_{L^{2}({\mathbb{E}})}dt.

Let
t\mapsto\Gamma_{t}d\gamma_{t}
a time-averaged semiclassical measure for
(\psi^{\hbar}(t))_{\hbar>0}
and we once again denote by
\ell_{\infty}
denote the associated linear form.

The fact that
\psi^{\hbar}(t)
is solution to (
1.2
) for
\tau=2
and the symbolic calculus now give us that for
\theta\in C_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}_{t})
and
\sigma\in\mathcal{A}_{0}
,

-i\hbar^{2}\ell_{\hbar}(\theta^{\prime},\sigma)=\ell_{\hbar}(\theta,[\sigma,H])+2\hbar\ell_{\hbar}(\theta,(\pi(V)\cdot V)\sigma)+\hbar^{2}\ell_{\hbar}(\theta,\Delta_{\mathbb{E}}\sigma).

Thus for a general symbol
\sigma\in\mathcal{A}_{0}
, we obtain once again at the limit
\hbar\rightarrow 0
, that

\ell_{\infty}(\theta,[\sigma,H])=0.

From this we deduce that once again, for almost all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, the operator
\Gamma_{t}
admits the following spectral decomposition

\Gamma_{t}(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta})=\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\Gamma_{n,t}(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta})\;\;{with}\;\;\Gamma_{n,t}(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta}):=\Pi_{n}(\pi^{\delta,\beta})\Gamma_{t}(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta})\Pi_{n}(\pi^{\delta,\beta}).

Now, using a symbol
\sigma\in\mathcal{B}_{0}({\mathbb{E}})
, i.e commuting with
H
, and uniformly 0 in a neighborhood of
\{\delta=0\}
, we obtain

\ell_{\hbar}(\theta,(\pi(V)\cdot V)\sigma)=\mathcal{O}(\hbar).

Using the decomposition in
H
-diagonal and
H
-off-diagonal parts of
(\pi(V)\cdot V)\sigma
and assuming we have chosen
\sigma
such that we have
\Pi_{n}\sigma\Pi_{n}=\sigma

for some
n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{>0}
, we can write

\ell_{\hbar}(\theta,\partial_{\beta}\mu_{n}(\delta,\beta)X_{2}\sigma)=\mathcal{O}(\hbar).

Taking the limit
\hbar\rightarrow 0
, we obtain that for almost all time
t\in{\mathbb{R}}_{t}
, we have (in a weak sense)

\partial_{\beta}\mu_{n}(\delta,\beta)X_{2}\gamma_{n,t}=0,

where
\gamma_{n,t}=\Gamma_{n,t}d\gamma_{t}
.

As we are in a non-compact setting and the flow
X_{2}
sends us to infinity, and as for
n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{>0}
, the operator-valued measure
\gamma_{n,t}
is of finite mass,
we deduce that these measures are necessarily zero where the flows
\partial_{\beta}\mu_{n}(\delta,\beta)X_{2}
,
n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{>0}
, are non-zero. We have thus proved the result of Proposition
1.12
.

3.5.
Proof of Theorem
1.10

This section is dedicated to the study of the semiclassical Schrödinger equation for the special case of the initial data being
a wave packet:

\psi_{0}^{\hbar}=WP_{x_{0},\pi^{\delta_{0},\beta_{0}}}^{\hbar}(a,\Phi_{1},\Phi_{2}),

for
a\in\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{R}}^{2}_{e^{c}})
,
(x_{0},\pi^{\delta_{0},\beta_{0}})\in{\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}_{\rm gen}
and
\Phi_{1}
,
\Phi_{2}\in\mathcal{H}_{\pi^{\delta_{0},\beta_{0}}}^{\infty}
satisfying

(3.18)

H(\pi^{\delta_{0},\beta_{0}})\Phi_{1}=\mu_{n}(\delta_{0},\beta_{0})\Phi_{1},

i.e
\Phi_{1}
is an eigenvector for the
n
-th eigenvalue,
n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{>0}
. In what follows, we will simply write
\pi^{0}
for the representation
\pi^{\delta_{0},\beta_{0}}
.
Moreover, as the subLaplacian is invariant by translation for the Engel group law, we can assume that
x_{0}=0
.

The idea of the proof is to consider the ansatz

\varphi^{\hbar}(t,x)=\hbar^{-Q/4}e^{i\frac{S(t)}{\hbar}}\left(a^{\hbar}(t,\hbar^{-1/2}\cdot(x(t)^{-1}x))\Phi_{1},\pi^{0}(\hbar^{-1}\cdot x)^{*}\Phi_{2}\right),

where
a^{\hbar}(t,x)\sim a(t,x){\rm Id}+\hbar^{1/2}\sigma_{1}+\hbar\sigma_{2}+\dots
is valued in the space of operators on
\mathcal{H}_{\pi^{0}}
,
S
is real-valued and
x(t)
is given by

x(0)=0\;\;\mbox{and}\;\;\dot{x}(t)=\partial_{\beta}\mu_{n}(\delta_{0},\beta_{0})X_{2}(x(t)),\ t\in{\mathbb{R}}.

Plugging this ansatz into Equation (
3.7
), we are led to solve a cascade of equations appearing at each power of
\hbar^{1/2}
. More precisely, as we want to approximate the real solution in
L^{2}({\mathbb{E}})
up to an error of order
\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{1/2})
, it is enough to
construct an approximate solution to the following equation

(3.19)

i\hbar\partial_{t}\varphi^{\hbar}+\hbar^{2}\Delta_{{\mathbb{E}}}\varphi^{\hbar}=O_{L^{2}({\mathbb{E}})}(\hbar^{3/2}).

Then an energy estimate allows us to conclude the approximation is of order
O(\hbar^{1/2})
, as desired.

First, observe that for
f\in\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{E}})
, we have

\frac{d}{dt}f(x(t)^{-1}x)=\frac{d}{dt}f\left({\rm Exp}_{{\mathbb{E}}}(-t\partial_{\beta}\mu_{n}(\delta_{0},\beta_{0})X_{2})x\right)=-\partial_{\beta}\mu_{n}(\delta_{0},\beta_{0})\frac{d}{ds}f\left({\rm Exp}_{{\mathbb{E}}}(sX_{2})x\right),

and we write
\tilde{X}_{2}
the vector field defined by the last term:

\frac{d}{ds}f\left({\rm Exp}_{{\mathbb{E}}}(sX_{2})x\right)=\tilde{X}_{2}f(x).

The vector field
\tilde{X}_{2}
corresponds to the right-invariant extension of the Lie alegbra element
X_{2}\in\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{E}}
that we have identified up to now with its left-invariant extension, see Equation (
2.3
).
It is possible to compute this vector field in semidirect coordinates: we obtain

\tilde{X}_{2}=\partial_{2}-x_{1}\partial_{3}.

Our analysis mainly rely on the following two following formulae:

\displaystyle i\hbar\partial_{t}\varphi^{\hbar}(t,x)=

\displaystyle-\dot{S}(t)\varphi^{\hbar}(t,x)-i\hbar^{1/2}\partial_{\beta}\mu_{n}(\delta_{0},\beta_{0})\left(\tilde{X}_{2}a^{\hbar}(t,\hbar^{-1/2}\cdot(x(t)^{-1}x))\Phi_{1},\pi^{0}(\hbar^{-1}\cdot x)^{*}\Phi_{2}\right)\hbar^{-Q/4}e^{i\frac{S(t)}{\hbar}}

\displaystyle+i\hbar\left(\partial_{t}a^{\hbar}(t,\hbar^{-1/2}\cdot(x(t)^{-1}x))\Phi_{1},\pi^{0}(\hbar^{-1}\cdot x)^{*}\Phi_{2}\right)\hbar^{-Q/4}e^{i\frac{S(t)}{\hbar}},

and

\displaystyle\hbar^{2}\Delta_{{\mathbb{E}}}\varphi^{\hbar}(t,x)

\displaystyle=-\left(a^{\hbar}(t,\hbar^{-1/2}\cdot(x(t)^{-1}x))\Phi_{1},H(\pi^{0})\pi^{0}(\hbar^{-1}\cdot x)^{*}\Phi_{2}\right)\hbar^{-Q/4}e^{i\frac{S(t)}{\hbar}}

\displaystyle+2\hbar^{1/2}\left((\pi^{0}(V)\cdot V)a^{\hbar}(t,\hbar^{-1/2}\cdot(x(t)^{-1}x))\Phi_{1},\pi^{0}(\hbar^{-1}\cdot x)^{*}\Phi_{2}\right)\hbar^{-Q/4}e^{i\frac{S(t)}{\hbar}}

\displaystyle+\hbar\left(\Delta_{{\mathbb{E}}}a^{\hbar}(t,\hbar^{-1/2}\cdot(x(t)^{-1}x))\Phi_{1},\pi^{0}(\hbar^{-1}\cdot x)^{*}\Phi_{2}\right)\hbar^{-Q/4}e^{i\frac{S(t)}{\hbar}}.

We will also make the assumption that for all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
a(t,\cdot)
is in
\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{R}}^{2}_{e^{c}})
, i.e in semidirect coordinates
a(t,\cdot)
depends only of the variables
x_{2}
and
x_{4}
.
Naturally we will need to check at the end of our analysis that this assumption is indeed realized.

3.5.1.
Error term of order
O(1)

The first terms that one observes in Equation (
3.19
) must satisfy

-\dot{S}(t)a(t,\hbar^{-1/2}\cdot(x(t)^{-1}x))\Phi_{1}-a(t,\hbar^{-1/2}\cdot(x(t)^{-1}x))H(\pi^{0})\Phi_{1}=0.

By the condition on
\Phi_{1}
, this leads to taking the phase
S
as follows:

S(t)=-\mu_{n}(\delta_{0},\beta_{0})t.

3.5.2.
Error term of order
O(\hbar^{1/2})

The second equation coming from Equation (
3.19
) and that one must solve is

\mu_{n}(\delta_{0},\beta_{0})\sigma_{1}(t,\hbar^{-1/2}\cdot(x(t)^{-1}x))\Phi_{1}-i\partial_{\beta}\mu_{n}(\delta_{0},\beta_{0})\tilde{X}_{2}a(t,\hbar^{-1/2}\cdot(x(t)^{-1}x))\Phi_{1}=\\
H(\pi^{0})\sigma_{1}(t,\hbar^{-1/2}\cdot(x(t)^{-1}x))\Phi_{1}-2(\pi^{0}(V)\cdot V)a(t,\hbar^{-1/2}\cdot(x(t)^{-1}x))\Phi_{1}.

This equation can be rewritten as follows:

[\sigma_{1}(t,y),H(\pi^{0})]\Pi_{n}(\pi^{0})=\left(i\partial_{\beta}\mu_{n}\tilde{X}_{2}a(t,y){\rm Id}-2(\pi^{0}(V)\cdot V)a(t,y)\right)\Pi_{n}(\pi^{0}).

Now by assumption on
a(t,y)
, as it does not depend on the variable
x_{3}
, we have for all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}

\tilde{X}_{2}a(t,\cdot)=X_{2}a(t,\cdot).

By Lemma
3.17
, it is thus enough to choose
\sigma_{1}
to be

(3.20)

\sigma_{1}(t,x)=\frac{i}{\delta_{0}}X_{1}a(t,x)\pi^{\delta_{0},\beta_{0}}(X_{3})\Pi_{n}(\pi^{0})-iX_{2}a(t,x)\partial_{\beta}\Pi_{n}(\pi^{0})\Pi_{n}(\pi^{0}).

3.5.3.
Error term of order
O(\hbar)

To obtain a solution to the approximate equation (
3.19
), we need to compute one last corrector term satifisfying

\mu_{n}(\delta_{0},\beta_{0})\sigma_{2}(t,y)\Phi_{1}-i\partial_{\beta}\mu_{n}(\delta_{0},\beta_{0})\tilde{X}_{2}\sigma_{1}(t,y))\Phi_{1}+i\partial_{t}a(t,y)\Phi_{1}\\
=H(\pi^{0})\sigma_{2}(t,y)\Phi_{1}-2(\pi^{0}(V)\cdot V)\sigma_{1}(t,y)\Phi_{1}-\Delta_{{\mathbb{E}}}a(t,y)\Phi_{1}.

As previously, this equation can be rewritten simply as

(3.21)

[\sigma_{2}(t,y),H(\pi^{0})]\Pi_{n}(\pi^{0})=(i\partial_{\beta}\mu_{n}\tilde{X}_{2}\sigma_{1}(t,y)-2(\pi^{0}(V)\cdot V)\sigma_{1}(t,y))\Pi_{n}(\pi^{0})\\
-(i\partial_{t}a+\Delta_{{\mathbb{E}}}a)(t,y)\Pi_{n}(\pi^{0}).

Denoting
R
the operator defined by the right-hand side term, we are to compute its diagonal part with respect to
\Pi_{n}(\pi^{0})
, i.e the operator
\Pi_{n}(\pi^{0})R\Pi_{n}(\pi^{0})
.
This will give the partial differential equation that the time-dependent
a
need to satisfy as this operator needs to be zero.
Determining
\sigma_{2}
will then follow easily as one would write

[\sigma_{2},H(\pi^{0})]\Pi_{n}(\pi^{0})=(\mu_{n}(\delta_{0},\beta_{0}){\rm Id}-H(\pi^{0}))\Pi_{n}^{\perp}(\pi^{0})\sigma_{2}=\Pi_{n}^{\perp}(\pi^{0})R\Pi_{n}(\pi^{0}),

where
\Pi_{n}^{\perp}(\pi^{0})={\rm Id}-\Pi_{n}(\pi^{0})
. As
\mu_{n}(\delta_{0},\beta_{0}){\rm Id}-H(\pi^{0})
is by definition invertible on
{\rm Ran}(\Pi_{n}^{\perp}(\pi^{0}))
,
we can rewrite the last equation simply as

\sigma_{2}=\left(\mu_{n}(\delta_{0},\beta_{0}){\rm Id}-H(\pi^{0})\right)^{-1}\Pi_{n}^{\perp}(\pi^{0})R\Pi_{n}(\pi^{0}).

We now proceed to determine the diagonal part term by term.

Term
i\partial_{\beta}\mu_{n}(\delta_{0},\beta_{0})\tilde{X}_{2}\sigma_{1}
:
By Equation (
3.20
) we have

\tilde{X}_{2}\sigma_{1}(t,y)=\frac{i}{\delta_{0}}\tilde{X}_{2}X_{1}a(t,y)\pi^{0}(X_{3})-i\tilde{X}_{2}X_{2}a(t,y)\partial_{\beta}\Pi_{n}(\pi^{0})\Pi_{n}(\pi^{0}).

We have already observed that the operator
\partial_{\beta}\Pi_{n}
has no diagonal part.
However, it is not the case for
\pi^{0}(X_{3})
. We obtain

i\partial_{\beta}\mu_{n}(\delta_{0},\beta_{0})\Pi_{n}(\pi^{0})\tilde{X}_{2}\sigma_{1}(t,y)\Pi_{n}(\pi^{0})=-\frac{1}{\delta_{0}}\partial_{\beta}\mu_{n}(\delta_{0},\beta)\tilde{X}_{2}X_{1}a(t,y)\Pi_{n}(\pi^{0})\pi^{0}(X_{3})\Pi_{n}(\pi^{0}).

Term
2\pi^{0}(X_{2})X_{2}\sigma_{1}
:
By Equation
3.20
, we have

2X_{2}\pi^{0}(X_{2})\sigma_{1}(t,y)=\frac{2i}{\delta_{0}}X_{2}X_{1}a(t,y)\pi^{0}(X_{2}X_{3})-2iX_{2}^{2}a(t,y)\pi^{0}(X_{2})\partial_{\beta}\Pi_{n}(\pi^{0})\Pi_{n}(\pi^{0}).

The following lemma allows us to compute the diagonal part of this operator.

Lemma 3.20
.

We have

(3.22)

\pi^{0}(X_{2}X_{3})=\left[-\frac{1}{2}\pi^{0}(X_{1}),H(\pi^{0})\right],

and

(3.23)

\Pi_{n}(\pi^{0})\pi^{0}(X_{2})\partial_{\beta}\Pi_{n}(\pi^{0})\Pi_{n}(\pi^{0})=\frac{i}{2}\left(\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\beta}^{2}\mu_{n}(\delta_{0},\beta_{0})-1\right)\Pi_{n}(\pi^{0}).

Proof.

The first equation results directly from the following computation in the enveloping algebra of
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{E}}
:

X_{2}X_{3}=\left[-\frac{1}{2}X_{1},-X_{1}^{2}-X_{2}^{2}\right].

For the second equation, recall that by Lemma
3.15
we have the following Feynmann-Hellmann formula:

H(\pi^{\delta,\beta})\partial_{\beta}\Pi_{n}(\pi^{\delta,\beta})-\partial_{\beta}\mu_{n}(\delta,\beta)\Pi_{n}(\pi^{\delta,\beta})=\mu_{n}(\delta,\beta)\partial_{\beta}\Pi_{n}(\pi^{\delta,\beta})+2i\pi^{\delta,\beta}(X_{2})\Pi_{n}(\pi^{\delta,\beta}),

holding for all
(\delta,\beta)\in\mathfrak{g}_{3}^{*}\setminus\{0\}\times\mathfrak{r}_{2}^{*}
. Deriving with respect to
\beta
, we obtain

H\partial_{\beta}^{2}\Pi_{n}-4i\pi^{\delta,\beta}(X_{2})\partial_{\beta}\Pi_{n}+2\Pi_{n}=\partial_{\beta}^{2}\mu_{n}\Pi_{n}+2\partial_{\beta}\mu_{n}\partial_{\beta}\Pi_{n}+\mu_{n}\partial_{\beta}^{2}\Pi_{n}.

By evalutating at
\pi^{0}
and multiplying on each side by
\Pi_{n}(\pi^{0})
, we have

\Pi_{n}(\pi^{0})\pi^{0}(X_{2})\partial_{\beta}\Pi_{n}(\pi^{0})\Pi_{n}(\pi^{0})=\frac{i}{2}\left(\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\beta}^{2}\mu_{n}(\delta_{0},\beta_{0})-1\right)\Pi_{n}(\pi^{0}).

∎

The diagonal part of
2X_{2}\pi^{0}(X_{2})\sigma_{1}(t,y)
is then given by

\left(\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\beta}^{2}\mu_{n}(\delta_{0},\beta_{0})-1\right)X_{2}^{2}a(t,y)\Pi_{n}(\pi^{0}).

Term
2\pi^{0}(X_{1})X_{1}\sigma_{1}
:
By definition we have

2\pi^{0}(X_{1})X_{1}\sigma_{1}(t,y)=\frac{2i}{\delta_{0}}X_{1}^{2}a(t,y)\pi^{0}(X_{1}X_{3})-2iX_{1}X_{2}a(t,y)\pi^{0}(X_{1})\partial_{\beta}\Pi_{n}(\pi^{0})\Pi_{n}(\pi^{0}).

Lemma 3.21
.

We have

\pi^{0}(X_{1}X_{3})=\frac{i\delta_{0}}{2}{\rm Id}+\left[\frac{1}{4i\delta_{0}}\pi^{0}(X_{3}^{2}),H(\pi^{0})\right],

and

\Pi_{n}(\pi^{0})\pi^{0}(X_{1})\partial_{\beta}\Pi_{n}(\pi^{0})\Pi_{n}(\pi^{0})=\frac{1}{2i\delta_{0}}\partial_{\beta}\mu_{n}(\delta_{0},\beta_{0})\Pi_{n}(\pi^{0})\pi^{0}(X_{3})\Pi_{n}(\pi^{0}).

Proof.

The first equation follows the next computation in the enveloping algebra of
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{E}}
:

\left[X_{3}^{2},-X_{1}^{2}-X_{2}^{2}\right]=4X_{1}X_{3}X_{4}-2X_{4}^{2}.

The second equation is less obvious and need to use the expression of
\pi^{0}(X_{1})
found in Lemma
3.17
:

\displaystyle\Pi_{n}\pi^{0}(X_{1})\partial_{\beta}\Pi_{n}\Pi_{n}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2i\delta_{0}}\left(\Pi_{n}\pi^{0}(X_{3})H(\pi^{0})\partial_{\beta}\Pi_{n}\Pi_{n}-\mu_{n}(\delta_{0},\beta_{0})\Pi_{n}\pi^{0}(X_{3})\partial_{\beta}\Pi_{n}\Pi_{n}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2i\delta_{0}}\Pi_{n}\pi^{0}(X_{3})(H(\pi^{0})-\mu_{n}(\delta_{0},\beta_{0}){\rm Id})\partial_{\beta}\Pi_{n}\Pi_{n}.

By Feynman-Hellman formula, this gives

\Pi_{n}\pi^{0}(X_{1})\partial_{\beta}\Pi_{n}\Pi_{n}=\frac{1}{2i\delta_{0}}\Pi_{n}\pi^{0}(X_{3})(2i\pi^{0}(X_{2})+\partial_{\beta}\mu_{n}(\delta_{0},\beta_{0}){\rm Id})\Pi_{n}.

By Lemma
3.20
we can write
\pi^{\delta_{0},\beta_{0}}(X_{3}X_{2})
as a commutator with
H(\delta_{0},\beta_{0})
and thus it has no diagonal part. The remaining term
gives the second equation of the lemma.
∎

The diagonal part of
2X_{1}\pi^{0}(X_{1})\sigma_{1}(t,y)
is then given by

-X_{1}^{2}a(t,y)\Pi_{n}(\pi^{0})-\frac{1}{\delta_{0}}\partial_{\beta}\mu_{n}(\delta_{0},\beta_{0})X_{1}X_{2}a(t,y)\Pi_{n}(\pi^{0})\pi^{0}(X_{3})\Pi_{n}(\pi^{0}).

Summing all terms:
The sum of the diagonal parts of each term in the righ-hand side of Equation (
3.21
) is

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{\delta_{0}}\partial_{\beta}\mu_{n}\tilde{X}_{2}X_{1}a(t,y)\Pi_{n}\pi^{\delta_{0},\beta_{0}}(X_{3})\Pi_{n}

\displaystyle+X_{1}^{2}a(t,y)\Pi_{n}+\frac{1}{\delta_{0}}\partial_{\beta}\mu_{n}X_{1}X_{2}a(t,y)\Pi_{n}\pi^{0}(X_{3})\Pi_{n}-\left(\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\beta}^{2}\mu_{n}-1\right)X_{2}^{2}a(t,y)\Pi_{n}

\displaystyle-(i\partial_{t}a+\Delta_{{\mathbb{E}}}a)(t,y)\Pi_{n}.

As this sums need to be equal to zero, after regrouping some terms together we obtain

\left(i\partial_{t}a+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\beta}^{2}\mu_{n}(\delta_{0},\beta_{0})X_{2}^{2}a\right)\Pi_{n}=\frac{1}{\delta_{0}}\partial_{\beta}\mu_{n}\left(X_{1}X_{2}-\tilde{X}_{2}X_{1}\right)a\Pi_{n}\pi^{0}(X_{3})\Pi_{n}.

As we have
\tilde{X}_{2}=X_{2}-x_{1}\partial_{3}
, we compute in semidirect coordinates that

\displaystyle X_{1}X_{2}-\tilde{X}_{2}X_{1}

\displaystyle=[X_{1},X_{2}]-x_{1}\partial_{3}X_{1}

\displaystyle=X_{3}-x_{1}X_{1}\partial_{3}+x_{1}[X_{1},\partial_{3}]

\displaystyle=X_{3}-x_{1}X_{1}\partial_{3}-\frac{1}{2}x_{1}\partial_{4}

\displaystyle=\partial_{3}-x_{1}X_{1}\partial_{3}.

Since we made the assumption that at any time
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, we have
\partial_{3}a(t,y)=0
, we deduce that the time-dependent profile
a
must satisfy the following partial differential equation:

(3.24)

i\partial_{t}a+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\beta}^{2}\mu_{n}(\delta_{0},\beta_{0})X_{2}^{2}a=0.

We easily check that, since
a_{0}
is in
\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{R}}^{2}_{e^{c}})
, so does the solution of Equation (
3.24
) at any time
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
.
The analysis is thus complete and we have constructed a solution to the approximate semiclassical Schrödinger equation (
3.19
).

4.
Second microlocalization on conic subsets

This section is devoted to developping the necessary tools to investigate in more detail the properties of the time-averaged semiclassical measures associated
with a solution to the Schrödinger equation (
1.2
) for time-scale
\tau=2
. As Proposition
1.12
has shown, these measures
split into the measures
(\gamma_{n,t})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{>0}}
along the spectral projectors of the symbol
H
, and we have the following support condition

(4.1)

{\rm supp}(\gamma_{n,t})\subset{\mathbb{E}}\times\{\pi^{\delta,\beta}\,:\,\partial_{\beta}\mu_{n}(\delta,\beta)=0,(\delta,\beta)\in\mathfrak{g}_{3}^{*}\setminus\{0\}\times\mathfrak{r}_{2}^{*}\}.

Using the relation between the eigenvalues of the symbol
H
and the Montgomery operators, we can write

\partial_{\beta}\mu_{n}(\delta,\beta)=\delta^{1/3}\partial_{\nu}\tilde{\mu}_{n}(\beta\delta^{-1/3}).

Thus any point in
{\rm supp}(\gamma_{n,t})
is contained in a set of the form

C_{\nu_{0}}={\mathbb{E}}\times\{\pi^{\delta,\beta}\in\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}_{\rm gen}\,:\,\beta=\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}\},

for any critical point
\nu_{0}
of
\tilde{\mu}_{n}
. Such sets can be seen to be
cones
for the dilation we introduced
on
\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}
.

In what follows, we fix
\nu_{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}
and develop a second-microlocalization on the cone
C_{\nu_{0}}
.

4.1.
Extended phase space and second-microlocal semiclassical calculus

4.1.1.
Second-microlocal observables

To achieve such an analysis, we need to extend our phase space
{\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}
by introducing a new variable
\eta\in{\mathbb{R}}
.
The test symbols associated to this extended phase space are defined as follows:

Definition 4.1
.

The space of second-microlocal symbols
\mathcal{A}_{0}^{2}({\mathbb{E}})
is defined as the set of field of operators
\sigma=\{\sigma(x,\pi,\eta)\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\pi})\,:\,(x,\pi,\eta)\in{\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}\times{\mathbb{R}}\}

for which there exists a application
\kappa:(x,y;\eta)\in{\mathbb{E}}\times{\mathbb{E}}\times{\mathbb{R}}\mapsto\kappa_{x}(y;\eta)
in
C^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}_{\eta},C_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{E}}_{x},\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{E}}_{y})))
, called also the
associated convolution kernel
, such that

•

For all
(x,\pi,\eta)\in{\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}\times{\mathbb{R}}
we have,
\sigma(x,\pi,\eta)=\mathcal{F}_{{\mathbb{E}}}(\kappa_{x}(\cdot\,;\eta))(\pi)
.

•

There exists a compact
K\subset{\mathbb{E}}
such that for all
\eta\in{\mathbb{R}}
, the map
x\in{\mathbb{E}}\mapsto\kappa_{x}(\cdot\,;\eta)\in\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{E}})
is
a smooth function compactly supported in
K
.

•

There exist two symbols
\sigma_{\pm 1}\in\mathcal{A}_{0}({\mathbb{E}})
and
R_{0}>0
such that, if
|\eta|>R_{0}
, then

\sigma(x,\pi,\eta)=\sigma_{\eta/|\eta|}(x,\pi)
, for
(x,\pi,\eta)\in{\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}\times{\mathbb{R}}
.

•

We ask that
\sigma
vanishes uniformly on a neighborhood of
\{\delta=0\}
, meaning there exists
\delta_{0}>0
such that

(4.2)

\forall(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta},\eta)\in{\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}_{\rm gen}\times{\mathbb{R}},\ |\delta|<\delta_{0}\implies\sigma(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta},\eta)=0.

Remark 4.2
.

The third point of the previous definition can be reformulated this way: the second-microlocal symbol
\sigma
is asked to be homogeneous of order
0
for
\eta
large enough uniformly. The two observables

\sigma_{\pm 1}
can be seen as a unique symbol
\sigma_{\infty}
defined on
{\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}\times\mathbb{S}^{0}
such that, for all
(x,\pi)\in{\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}
, we have

\forall\eta\in{\mathbb{R}},\ |\eta|\geq R_{0}\implies\sigma(x,\pi,\eta)=\sigma_{\infty}\left(x,\pi,\frac{\eta}{|\eta|}\right).

We will denote by
\mathcal{A}_{0}^{2,\infty}({\mathbb{E}})
the space of these symbols
\sigma_{\infty}
.

Remark that for
\eta\in{\mathbb{R}}
fixed, the symbol given by
\sigma(\cdot,\cdot,\eta)
is in the space of smoothing symbols
\mathcal{A}_{0}({\mathbb{E}})
.

4.1.2.
Quantization procedure

The addition of the extra variable
\eta
becomes clear once we define their quantization: for
\hbar>0
and a second-microlocal symbol
\sigma\in\mathcal{A}_{0}^{2}({\mathbb{E}})
, we associate the symbol
\sigma_{\hbar}^{C_{\nu_{0}}}
defined by

(4.3)

\sigma_{\hbar}^{C_{\nu_{0}}}(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta})=\sigma\left(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta},\frac{\beta-\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}}{\hbar}\right),

and we define the quantization of
\sigma
by

(4.4)

{\rm Op}_{\hbar}^{C_{\nu_{0}}}(\sigma):={\rm Op}_{\hbar}(\sigma_{\hbar}^{C_{\nu_{0}}}).

For this definition to make sense, we must make sure that, for
\hbar>0
fixed,
\sigma_{\hbar}^{C_{\nu_{0}}}
is a symbol in a certain class
S^{m}_{\rho,\delta}({\mathbb{E}})
(see Section
3.1.2
).

Note first that if (
4.4
) makes sense, we could rewrite the quantization of a second-microlocal symbol
\sigma\in\mathcal{A}_{0}^{2}({\mathbb{E}})
as follows:

{\rm Op}_{\hbar}^{C_{\nu_{0}}}(\sigma)={\rm Op}_{1}\left(\sigma\left(x,\hbar\cdot\pi^{\delta,\beta},\beta-\nu\delta^{1/3}\right)\right),

so, up to a dilation, it is equivalent to work with the symbol in the right-hand side.

The next proposition allows us to extend the formulae of the difference operators found in Proposition
2.11
for symbols in
\mathcal{S}(\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}})

and to use them to directly test if a given measurable field of operators on
\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}
is in one of our classes of regular symbols. For the sake of clarity, in the following proposition
and its proof we will denote by
\hat{\Delta}_{i}
,
i\in\{1,2,3,4\}
, the operators given by the right-hand side formulae of the corresponding difference operators
presented in Proposition
2.11
.

Proposition 4.3
.

Let
\sigma
be a smooth application in
C^{\infty}\left({\mathbb{R}}\setminus\{0\}\times{\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{R}}_{\xi}),\mathcal{S}^{\prime}({\mathbb{R}}_{\xi}))\right)
for which there exists
\delta_{0}>0
sich that

\forall(\delta,\beta)\in{\mathbb{R}}\subset\{0\}\times{\mathbb{R}},\ |\delta|<\delta_{0}\implies\sigma(\delta,\beta)=0,

and satisfying the following estimates: for
\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}}^{4}
,

(4.5)

\sup_{(\delta,\beta)\in{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\{0\}\times{\mathbb{R}}}\|\hat{\Delta}^{\alpha}\sigma(\delta,\beta)\|_{\mathcal{L}(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}_{\xi}))}<\infty.

Then there exists a symbol
\tau
in the class
S^{0}_{0}({\mathbb{E}})
such that

\forall\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}}^{4},\,\forall\pi^{\delta,\beta}\in\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}_{\rm gen},\ \Delta^{\alpha}\tau(\pi^{\delta,\beta})=\hat{\Delta}^{\alpha}\sigma(\delta,\beta).

Remark 4.4
.

Recall that a symbol
\tau=\left\{\tau(\pi)\,:\,\pi\in\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}\right\}
is in
S_{0}^{0}({\mathbb{E}})
if and only if,
for all
\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}}^{4}
, the field of operators

\left\{\Delta^{\alpha}\tau(\pi)\,:\,\pi\in\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}\right\},

makes sense and is in
L^{\infty}(\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}})
, i.e

\|\Delta^{\alpha}\tau\|_{L^{\infty}(\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}})}<\infty.

Proof.

By the estimate (
4.5
) for
\alpha=0
, the measurable symbol
\tau
defined by

\forall\pi^{\delta,\beta}\in\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}_{\rm gen},\ \tau(\pi^{\delta,\beta})=\sigma(\delta,\beta),

is in
L^{\infty}(\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}})
. By the discussion in the Section
2.2.4
, there exists a distribution
\kappa\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}({\mathbb{E}})

such that

\tau=\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{E}}\kappa.

We wish to prove that
\kappa
is in the domain of the difference operators
\Delta^{\alpha}
in the sense of Definition
2.7
and
that their action corresponds to the one of the operators
\hat{\Delta}^{\alpha}
on
\sigma
.
We introduce
\phi\in\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{E}})
a Schwartz function satisfying
\phi(0)=1
, and for
\epsilon>0
, the following approximation of identity

(4.6)

\forall x\in{\mathbb{E}},\ \phi_{\epsilon}(x)=\epsilon^{-Q}\phi(\epsilon^{-1}\cdot x).

As
\kappa
is a tempered distribution, we can consider the following sequence of smooth functions

\kappa_{\epsilon}=\kappa*\phi_{\epsilon}\in C_{b}^{\infty}({\mathbb{E}}),

satisfying
\kappa_{\epsilon}\rightarrow\kappa
in
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}({\mathbb{E}})
as
\epsilon
goes to 0. Moreover, the Fourier transform of
\kappa_{\epsilon}
, that we
denote by
\tau_{\epsilon}
, makes sense and satisfies

\tau_{\epsilon}=\hat{\phi}_{\epsilon}\tau.

We can actually prove that
\kappa_{\epsilon}
is a Schwartz function. First, since
\phi_{\epsilon}
is a Schwartz function, the inverse Fourier formula makes sense and we can write for
x\in{\mathbb{E}}
,

\kappa_{\epsilon}(x)=\int_{\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}_{\rm gen}}{\rm Tr}\left(\pi^{\delta,\beta}(x)\tau_{\epsilon}(\pi^{\delta,\beta})\right)d\mu_{\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}}(\pi^{\delta,\beta}).

For
i\in\{1,2,3,4\}
, observe by direct computation that we have for
x\in{\mathbb{E}}

\hat{\Delta}_{i}\pi^{\delta,\beta}(x)=x_{i}\pi^{\delta,\beta}(x),

where the operator
\hat{\Delta}_{i}
is applied to the field
\{\pi^{\delta,\beta}(x)\,:\,(\delta,\beta)\in\mathfrak{g}_{3}^{*}\setminus\{0\}\times\mathfrak{r}_{2}^{*}\}
and
(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4})
are the semidirect
coordinates of
x
.
This allows us to write

\displaystyle x_{i}\kappa_{\epsilon}(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}_{\rm gen}}{\rm Tr}\left(x_{i}\pi^{\delta,\beta}(x)\tau_{\epsilon}(\pi^{\delta,\beta})\right)d\mu_{\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}}(\pi^{\delta,\beta})

\displaystyle=\int_{\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}_{\rm gen}}{\rm Tr}\left(\hat{\Delta}_{i}\pi^{\delta,\beta}(x)\hat{\phi}_{\epsilon}(\pi^{\delta,\beta})\sigma(\delta,\beta)\right)d\mu_{\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}}(\pi^{\delta,\beta})

\displaystyle=-\int_{\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}_{\rm gen}}{\rm Tr}\left(\pi^{\delta,\beta}(x)\hat{\Delta}_{i}(\hat{\phi}_{\epsilon}(\pi^{\delta,\beta})\sigma(\delta,\beta))\right)d\mu_{\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}}(\pi^{\delta,\beta}),

the last equation following from the explicit formula given by Proposition
2.11
and by integration by parts, the remainder terms being zero by assumption on
the support of
\sigma
and smoothness of
\kappa_{\epsilon}
. Now we can write

\hat{\Delta}_{i}(\hat{\phi}_{\epsilon}\sigma)=\hat{\Delta}_{i}\hat{\phi}_{\epsilon}\circ\sigma+\hat{\phi}_{\epsilon}\circ\hat{\Delta}_{i}\sigma,

and by estimates (
4.5
), since
\phi_{\epsilon}
is in
\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{E}})
, we conclude that
x_{i}\kappa_{\epsilon}
is uniformly bounded. By recurrence, our claim is proved.

Now, as
\kappa_{\epsilon}
is a Schwartz function, we can write for
i\in\{1,2,3,4\}

(4.7)

\Delta_{i}\tau_{\epsilon}=\hat{\Delta}_{i}\tau_{\epsilon}=\hat{\Delta}_{i}\hat{\phi}_{\epsilon}\circ\tau+\hat{\phi}_{\epsilon}\circ\hat{\Delta}_{i}\sigma.

As
\hat{\Delta}_{i}\hat{\phi}_{\epsilon}=\Delta_{i}\hat{\phi}_{\epsilon}
, we check that for
\pi\in\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}
:

\Delta_{i}\hat{\phi}_{\epsilon}(\pi)=\int_{\mathbb{E}}-x_{i}\phi_{\epsilon}(x)\pi(x)^{*}dx=\epsilon^{\upsilon_{i}}\int_{\mathbb{E}}\psi^{i}_{\epsilon}(x)\pi(x)^{*}dx=\epsilon^{\upsilon_{i}}\hat{\psi}^{i}_{\epsilon}(\pi),

where we introduced
\psi^{i}=-x_{i}\phi
and its associated dilated family
(\psi^{i}_{\epsilon})_{\epsilon>0}
as in (
4.6
).
Similarly as in the discussion of estimate (
4.5
) for
\alpha=0
, the measurable symbol
\tau_{i}
defined by

\forall\pi^{\delta,\beta}\in\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}_{\rm gen},\ \tau_{i}(\pi^{\delta,\beta})=\hat{\Delta}_{i}\sigma(\delta,\beta),

is in
L^{\infty}(\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}})
. Equation (
4.7
) becomes

\Delta_{i}\tau_{\epsilon}=\hat{\phi}_{\epsilon}\circ\tau_{i}+\mathcal{O}_{L^{\infty}(\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}})}(\epsilon^{\upsilon_{i}}).

We deduce that since
(\phi_{\epsilon})_{\epsilon>0}
is an approximation of the identity,
\Delta_{i}\tau_{\epsilon}
converges towards
\tau_{i}
in
L^{\infty}(\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}})
for the strong operator topology as
\epsilon
goes to 0,
meaning seen as operators on
L^{2}({\mathbb{E}})
.
By the various realization of the group von Neumann algebra discussed in Section
2.2.4
, it implies that
-x_{i}\kappa_{\epsilon}
converges in the space of tempered distributions towards

\mathcal{F}_{{\mathbb{E}}}^{-1}\tau_{i}\in\mathcal{K}({\mathbb{E}})
. Observing that
-x_{i}\kappa_{\epsilon}
also converges towards
-x_{i}\kappa
in
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}({\mathbb{E}})
, by unicity of the limit
we have
-x_{i}\kappa\in\mathcal{K}({\mathbb{E}})
. It follows that
\Delta_{i}\tau
makes sense and satisfies

\forall\pi^{\delta,\beta}\in\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}_{\rm gen},\ \Delta_{i}\tau(\pi^{\delta,\beta})=\hat{\Delta}_{i}\sigma(\delta,\beta).

For difference operators of higher order, we conclude by recurrence.
∎

The following proposition makes sense of our quantization procedure for second-microlocal symbols as the measurable symbols
\sigma_{\hbar}^{C_{\nu_{0}}}
,
\hbar>0
, for
\sigma\in\mathcal{A}_{0}^{2}({\mathbb{E}})
introduced by
Equation (
4.3
) are shown to be proper regular symbols.

Proposition 4.5
.

For
\sigma\in\mathcal{A}_{0}^{2}
, the family

\left(\left\{\tilde{\sigma}_{\hbar}(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta})=\sigma(x,\hbar\cdot\pi^{\delta,\beta},\beta-\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3})\,:\,(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta})\in{\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}_{\rm gen}\right\}\right)_{\hbar>0},

is a bounded family of symbols in
S_{0,0}^{0}({\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}})
.
As a consequence, by Calderòn-Vaillancourt’s theorem
3.2
, the family
\left({\rm Op}_{\hbar}^{C_{\nu_{0}}}\left(\sigma\right)\right)_{\hbar>0}
is a bounded family of operators on
L^{2}({\mathbb{E}})
satisfying

\forall\hbar\in(0,1),\ \left\|{\rm Op}_{\hbar}^{C_{\nu_{0}}}\left(\sigma\right)\right\|_{L^{2}\rightarrow L^{2}}\leq C\sum_{0\leq i\leq d}\sup_{\eta\in{\mathbb{R}}}N(\partial_{\eta}^{i}\sigma(\cdot,\cdot,\eta)),

for some fixed
d\in{\mathbb{N}}_{>0}
. We will denote by
N_{2}(\sigma)
the seminorm defined by the right-hand side of the last inequality.

Proof.

By Proposition
4.3
, to prove that we have defined symbols in the class
S^{0}_{0,0}
, it is enough to bound uniformly in
\hbar
the seminorms
\|\sigma\|_{S_{0,0}^{0},\alpha,\beta}

using the explicit formulae of the difference operators (see Proposition
2.11
).
We remark that all difference operators acts on an element of
\mathcal{S}(\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}})
in part by commutator and in part by differentiating along the variables
\delta
and
\beta
.

For the commutator parts, we treat the case of
\Delta_{1}
: we have

\Delta_{1}\tilde{\sigma}(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta})=\frac{i}{\delta}\left[\pi^{\delta,\beta}(X_{3}),\sigma(x,\hbar\cdot\pi^{\delta,\beta},\beta-\nu\delta^{1/3})\right],

thus, we have the following estimate:

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in{\mathbb{E}}}\|\Delta_{1}\tilde{\sigma}_{\hbar}(x,\cdot)\|_{L^{\infty}(\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}})}

\displaystyle\leq\hbar\,\sup_{\eta\in{\mathbb{R}}}\|\Delta_{1}\sigma(\cdot,\cdot,\eta)\|_{L^{\infty}({\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}})}.

For the difference operators acting by differentiation, we treat the case of
\Delta_{2}
. We observe that

\Delta_{2}\tilde{\sigma}_{\hbar}(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta})=\hbar\Delta_{2}\sigma(x,\hbar\cdot\pi^{\delta,\beta},\beta-\nu\delta^{1/3})+\partial_{\eta}\sigma(x,\hbar\cdot\pi^{\delta,\beta},\beta-\nu\delta^{1/3}),

where in the right-hand side, the difference operator
\Delta_{2}
is seen acting on the symbol
\sigma(\cdot,\cdot,\beta-\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3})
. We deduce

\sup_{x\in{\mathbb{E}}}\|\Delta_{2}\tilde{\sigma}_{\hbar}(x,\cdot)\|_{L^{\infty}(\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}})}\leq\hbar\,\sup_{\eta\in{\mathbb{R}}}\|\Delta_{2}\sigma(\cdot,\cdot,\eta)\|_{L^{\infty}({\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}})}+\sup_{\eta\in{\mathbb{R}}}\|\partial_{\eta}\sigma(\cdot,\cdot,\eta)\|_{L^{\infty}({\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}})}.

We treat similarly the difference operators
\Delta_{3}
and
\Delta_{4}
, and more generally
\Delta^{\alpha}
to get a bound
of each seminorm by a uniform bound on
\eta
of a seminorm in
S_{0,0}^{0}
of
\partial_{\eta}^{i}\sigma(\cdot,\cdot,\eta)
,
i\leq|\alpha|
.

The Calderòn-Vaillancourt theorem gives that the norm of
{\rm Op}_{1,{\mathbb{E}}}\left(\tilde{\sigma}_{\hbar}\right)
as operators on
L^{2}({\mathbb{E}})
is bounded by the seminorm
N(\cdot)=\|\cdot\|_{S_{0,0,a,b}^{0}}
, and thus
we find there exists
d\in{\mathbb{N}}_{>0}
such that

\left\|{\rm Op}_{1,{\mathbb{E}}}\left(\tilde{\sigma}_{\hbar}\right)\right\|_{L^{2}\rightarrow L^{2}}\leq C\sum_{0\leq i\leq d}\sup_{\eta\in{\mathbb{R}}}N(\partial_{\eta}^{i}\sigma(\cdot,\cdot,\eta)).

∎

Thanks to Proposition
4.5
, it now makes sense to study the limiting objects of the linear forms

\sigma\in\mathcal{A}_{0}^{2}({\mathbb{E}})\mapsto\left({\rm Op}_{\hbar}^{C_{\nu_{0}}}(\sigma)\psi_{0}^{\hbar},\psi_{0}^{\hbar}\right)_{L^{2}({\mathbb{E}})},

for a bounded family
(\psi_{0}^{\hbar})_{\hbar>0}
in
L^{2}({\mathbb{E}})
, as
\hbar
goes to 0.

4.2.
Fourier integral operators

This section, as well as the following one, presents technical but necessary results for the identification of the limiting objects
given in Section
4.4
. It is also here that our analysis establishes the link with the pseudodifferential calculus
of the group
{\mathbb{H}}^{1,1}
.

4.2.1.
Quasi-contact manifolds and the group
{\mathbb{H}}^{1,1}

To continue the study of the operators obtained by the precedent quantization of the second-microlocal symbols, we further
discuss the relation between Engel manifolds and quasi-contact ones.

As it has been pointed out in the introduction, every Engel manifold
(M,\mathcal{D})
induces a quasi-contact distribution by considering

\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{D}+[\mathcal{D},\mathcal{D}]
: this is indeed a non-integrable distribution of dimension 3 on a 4-manifold. And as we have already seen,
the characteristic field
L
of an Engel manifold is actually a feature of the quasi-contact structure
(M,\mathcal{E})
and is defined by a
condition of stabilization of the distribution
\mathcal{E}
:

[L,\mathcal{E}]\equiv 0,\mod\mathcal{E}.

Similarly to Engel manifolds, quasi-contact ones also have a Darboux-like theorem and are all locally modelled on a particular nilotent Lie group,
that we call in this article the
quasi-Heisenberg group
and we denote by
{\mathbb{H}}^{1,1}
. Its Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}_{{\mathbb{H}}^{1,1}}
is spanned by four vector fields

Y_{1},Y_{2},Y_{3}
and
Y_{4}
satisfying the relations

[Y_{1},Y_{3}]=Y_{4}.

It is thus trivially isomorphic to
{\mathbb{H}}\times{\mathbb{R}}_{2}
where
{\mathbb{H}}={\rm Exp}_{{\mathbb{H}}^{1,1}}({\mathbb{R}}Y_{1}\oplus{\mathbb{R}}Y_{3}\oplus{\mathbb{R}}Y_{4})
and
{\mathbb{R}}_{2}={\rm Exp}_{{\mathbb{H}}^{1,1}}({\mathbb{R}}Y_{2})
.

These notations are voluntarily similar to the ones introduced in Section
2.1
for the semidirect decomposition of
{\mathbb{E}}

for the following reason. The Engel group
{\mathbb{E}}
is trivially an Engel manifold for the distribution
\mathcal{D}_{\mathbb{E}}
spanned by the vector fields
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
. The
quasi-contact distribution
\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{E}}
it induces is then given by the span of
X_{1},X_{2}
and
X_{3}
. It is remarkable that, when working in semidirect coordinates, this distribution, naturally invariant
by translations for the Engel group law, is also invariant by translations for the quasi-Heisenberg group law when
{\mathbb{H}}^{1,1}
is realized on the same manifold
{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
through exponential coordinates.
In fact, in these coordinates it is possible to relate the vector fields
Y_{1},Y_{2},Y_{3}
and
Y_{4}
to the ones on the Engel group:

Y_{1}=X_{1}+x_{2}X_{2},\ Y_{i}=X_{i},\ i\in\{2,3,4\},

and the characteristic direction is then unequivocally given by the vector field
X_{2}=Y_{2}
. Moreover, as submanifolds,
{\mathbb{H}}
and
{\mathbb{R}}_{2}

are well-defined, independently of the choice of exponential maps
{\rm Exp}_{\mathbb{E}}
or
{\rm Exp}_{{\mathbb{H}}^{1,1}}
used, and are subgroups for both group laws.

As the characteristic direction play a primary role in the quantum evolution, the precedent observations lead us to relate the semiclassical calculi of the groups
{\mathbb{E}}
and
{\mathbb{H}}^{1,1}
.

To this end, we introduce the relevant element of harmonic analysis on
{\mathbb{H}}^{1,1}
and fix some notations.
The following proposition describe the generic part of the dual of
{\mathbb{H}}^{1,1}
.

Proposition 4.6
.

The generic dual
\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}^{1,1}_{\rm gen}
of
{\mathbb{H}}^{1,1}
is homeomorphic to
\mathfrak{g}_{3}^{*}\setminus\{0\}\times\mathfrak{r}_{2}^{*}
and is made of the following irreducible unitary representations:
for
(\delta,\beta)\in\mathfrak{g}_{3}^{*}\setminus\{0\}\times\mathfrak{r}_{2}^{*}
, the representation
\check{\pi}^{\delta,\beta}
is defined on the Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{\check{\pi}^{\delta,\beta}}:=L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}_{\xi})
by

\forall\varphi\in\mathcal{H}_{\check{\pi}^{\delta,\beta}},\ \check{\pi}^{\delta,\beta}(x)\varphi(\xi)={\rm exp}\left[i\delta\left(x_{4}+\xi x_{3}+\frac{1}{2}x_{1}x_{3}\right)\right]e^{i\beta x_{2}}\varphi(\xi+x_{1}).

Moreover, the Plancherel measure is supported in
\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}^{1,1}_{\rm gen}
and is given by

d\mu_{\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}^{1,1}}(\check{\pi}^{\delta,\beta})=d\mu_{\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}}(\tilde{\pi}^{\delta})\frac{d\beta}{\sqrt{2\pi}}=c_{{\mathbb{H}}^{1,1}}|\delta|d\delta d\beta

with
c_{{\mathbb{H}}^{1,1}}>0
.

Remark 4.7
.

In what follows, we will denote by
\{\tilde{\pi}^{\delta}\,:\,\delta\in\mathfrak{g}_{3}^{*}\setminus\{0\}\}
the Schrödinger representations of the Heisenberg group

{\mathbb{H}}
obtained as restriction of the representations
\pi^{\delta,\beta}
,
\beta\in\mathfrak{r}_{2}^{*}
.
It is then straightforward to see that the previous proposition follows from the decomposition
{\mathbb{H}}^{1,1}={\mathbb{H}}\times{\mathbb{R}}_{2}
in a direct product and
the representation
\check{\pi}^{\delta,\beta}
is given by the product of the Schrödinger representation
\tilde{\pi}^{\delta}
and the character
e^{i\beta\cdot}
.
The same observation holds for the Plancherel measure.

We will also consider a particular family of dilations, that we call
partial dilations
, on
{\mathbb{H}}^{1,1}
: for
r>0
and
x=(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4})\in{\mathbb{H}}^{1,1}
in exponential coordinates, we set

r\cdot_{pt}x=(rx_{1},x_{2},r^{2}x_{3},r^{3}x_{4}).

Extending this family of dilations to
\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}^{1,1}
, we define the associated partial semiclassical Fourier transform
\mathcal{F}_{{\mathbb{H}}^{1,1}}^{\hbar}
on
{\mathbb{H}}^{1,1}
as follows: for
f\in\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{H}}^{1,1})
,

\forall\check{\pi}\in\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}^{1,1},\ \mathcal{F}_{{\mathbb{H}}^{1,1}_{pt}}^{\hbar}f(\check{\pi})=\int_{{\mathbb{H}}^{1,1}}f(x)\check{\pi}(\hbar^{-1}\cdot_{pt}x)^{*}\,dx.

This definition of the partial semiclassical Fourier transform on
{\mathbb{H}}^{1,1}
leads us to introduce the following associated quantization of a symbol
\tau\in\mathcal{A}_{0}({\mathbb{H}}^{1,1})
:

\forall f\in\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{H}}^{1,1}),\forall x\in{\mathbb{H}}^{1,1},\ {\rm Op}_{\hbar,{\mathbb{H}}_{pt}^{1,1}}(\tau)f(x)=\int_{\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}^{1,1}}{\rm Tr}\left(\check{\pi}(x)\tau(x,\hbar\cdot_{pt}\check{\pi})\mathcal{F}_{{\mathbb{H}}^{1,1}}f(\check{\pi})\right)d\mu_{\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}^{1,1}}(\check{\pi}),

where
(\hbar\cdot_{pt}\check{\pi})(y)=\check{\pi}(\hbar\cdot_{pt}y)
for
y\in{\mathbb{H}}^{1,1}
.

4.2.2.
Fourier integral operators

In the rest of this section, we fix
\nu_{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}
. We present here the construction of a particular unitary operator between the Hilbert spaces
L^{2}({\mathbb{H}}^{1,1})
and
L^{2}({\mathbb{E}})
.

Definition 4.8
.

We introduce the following map

\begin{array}[]{lrcl}T_{\nu_{0}}:&\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}^{1,1}_{\rm gen}&\longrightarrow&\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}_{\rm gen}\\
&[\check{\pi}^{\delta,\beta}]&\longmapsto&[\pi^{\delta,\beta+\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}}].\end{array}

For
\delta_{0}>0
, we also define the following sets

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{\delta_{0}}(\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}^{1,1})

\displaystyle=\left\{[\check{\pi}^{\delta,\beta}]\in\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}^{1,1}\,:\,|\delta|>\delta_{0},\,\beta\in{\mathbb{R}}\right\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{\delta_{0}}(\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}})

\displaystyle=\left\{[\pi^{\delta,\beta}]\in\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}\,:\,|\delta|>\delta_{0},\,\beta\in{\mathbb{R}}\right\},

as well as the map
T_{\nu_{0}}^{\delta_{0}}
between them as the restriction of
T
:

\begin{array}[]{lrcl}T_{\nu_{0}}^{\delta_{0}}:&\mathcal{O}_{\delta_{0}}(\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}^{1,1})&\longrightarrow&\mathcal{O}_{\delta_{0}}(\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}})\\
&[\check{\pi}^{\delta,\beta}]&\longmapsto&[\pi^{\delta,\beta+\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}}].\end{array}

Proposition 4.9
.

For
\delta_{0}>0
fixed, the sets
\mathcal{O}_{\delta_{0}}(\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}})
and
\mathcal{O}_{\delta_{0}}(\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}^{1,1})
are open in

\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}_{\rm gen}
and
\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}^{1,1}_{\rm gen}
respectively. Moreover
T_{\nu_{0}}
and
T_{\nu_{0}}^{\delta_{0}}
are homeomorphisms and, up to a certain constant
c>0
,
we have preservation of the Plancherel measure:

T_{\nu_{0}}^{*}\mu_{\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}^{1,1}}=c\mu_{\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}}.

Remark 4.10
.

In the rest of this paper, we will not keep track of the exact constant appearing in the Plancherel measures of the different groups,
and we will make the abuse of writing
c=1
in our computations.

As
T_{\nu_{0}}
preserves the Plancherel measures, for
\tau\in L^{2}(\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}^{1,1})
, we can consider its pushforward by
T_{\nu_{0}}
defined by

T_{\nu_{0}}^{*}\tau=\tau\circ T_{\nu_{0}}^{-1}.

Moreover, we have
\|\tau\|_{L^{2}(\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}^{1,1})}=\|T_{\nu_{0}}^{*}\tau\|_{L^{2}(\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}})}
.

We introduce now the operator that will play the role of a Fourier integral operator between the quasi-Heisenberg group
{\mathbb{H}}^{1,1}
and
the Engel group
{\mathbb{E}}
.

Definition 4.11
.

For
\hbar>0
, we introduce the unitary operator
\mathbb{U}_{\nu_{0}}^{\hbar}:L^{2}({\mathbb{H}}^{1,1})\rightarrow L^{2}({\mathbb{E}})
defined by

\mathbb{U}_{\nu_{0}}^{\hbar}=\left(\mathcal{F}_{{\mathbb{E}}}^{\hbar}\right)^{-1}\circ D^{\hbar}\circ T_{\nu_{0}}^{*}\circ\mathcal{F}_{{\mathbb{H}}_{pt}^{1,1}}^{\hbar},

where the operator
\tau\in L^{2}(\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}})\rightarrow D^{\hbar}\tau\in L^{2}(\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}})
is a dilation along the
\beta
variable, i.e is defined by

D^{\hbar}\tau(\pi^{\delta,\beta}):=\tau(\pi^{\delta,\frac{\beta}{\hbar}}).

We present now a more practical formula for this unitary operator.

Definition 4.12
.

For
\hbar>0
and
x_{2}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{2}
, we denote by
U_{\nu_{0}}^{\hbar}(x_{2},\cdot)
the following field of operators on
\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}
:

(4.8)

U_{\nu_{0}}^{\hbar}(x_{2},\cdot)=\left\{\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}}\left(\frac{x_{2}}{\hbar}\right)\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\tilde{\pi}^{\delta}})\,:\,\tilde{\pi}^{\delta}\in\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}_{\rm gen}\right\}.

We can rewrite informally the unitary operator
\mathbb{U}_{\nu_{0}}^{\hbar}
as follows: for
f\in\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{H}}^{1,1})
, we have for
x=(h_{x},x_{2})\in{\mathbb{E}}={\mathbb{H}}\rtimes{\mathbb{R}}_{2}
:

\mathbb{U}_{\nu_{0}}^{\hbar}f(h_{x},x_{2})=\hbar^{-6}\int_{\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}\times{\mathbb{R}}}\int_{{\mathbb{H}}\times{\mathbb{R}}}{\rm Tr}_{\mathcal{H}_{\tilde{\pi}^{\delta}}}\left(\tilde{\pi}^{\delta}\left(\hbar^{-1}\cdot(h_{y}^{-1}h_{x})\right)e^{i\beta(x_{2}-y_{2})}U_{\nu_{0}}^{\hbar}(x_{2},\tilde{\pi}^{\delta})\right)\\
\times f(h_{y},y_{2})\,d\mu_{\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}}(\tilde{\pi}^{\delta})d\beta dh_{y}dy_{2}.

Note that the dilation used on the Heisenberg group
{\mathbb{H}}
is the restriction of the one on the Engel group, and as such will be denoted the same.

We can give a meaning to this informal expression interpreting it as an oscillatory integral. To this end, we introduce
\left(\theta_{\epsilon}\right)_{\epsilon>0}
, an approximation of the identity in
\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{H}})
.

Proposition 4.13
.

The operator
\mathbb{U}_{\nu_{0}}^{\hbar}
considered as an operator
\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{H}}^{1,1})\rightarrow C_{b}({\mathbb{E}})
from the Schwartz space to the space of bounded continuous maps, makes sense and is continuous.
Moreover, it admits the following integral representation: for all
(h_{x},x_{2})\in{\mathbb{E}}
, we have:

\mathbb{U}_{\nu_{0}}^{\hbar}f(h_{x},x_{2})=\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\hbar^{-6}\int_{\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}}\int_{{\mathbb{H}}}{\rm Tr}\left(\tilde{\pi}^{\delta}\left(\hbar^{-1}\cdot(h_{y}^{-1}h_{x})\right)U_{\nu_{0}}^{\hbar}(x_{2},\tilde{\pi}^{\delta})\mathcal{F}_{{\mathbb{H}}}^{\hbar}\theta_{\epsilon}(\tilde{\pi}^{\delta})\right)f(h_{y},x_{2})\,d\mu_{\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}}(\tilde{\pi}^{\delta})dh_{y},

and the convergence is uniform as
\epsilon
goes to 0.

Proof.

First, continuity of
\mathbb{U}_{\nu_{0}}^{\hbar}f
follows from the following facts: since
f
is Schwartz,
\mathcal{F}_{{\mathbb{H}}^{1,1}_{pt}}f
is in
L^{1}(\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}^{1,1})
and as
D^{\hbar}
and
T_{\nu_{0}}
preserves the Plancherel measures up to constants,

\mathbb{U}_{\nu_{0}}^{\hbar}f
is the inverse Fourier transform of an element of
L^{1}(\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}})
. As such it is continuous on
