{\mathbb{E}}
by dominated convergence. Thus it makes sense to evaluate
\mathbb{U}_{\nu_{0}}^{\hbar}f
at any point.

Now, let
\chi\in C_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}})
a smooth positive compactly supported function satisfying
\chi(0)=1
.
We will write for
\epsilon>0
,
\chi_{\epsilon}(x_{2})=\epsilon^{-1}\chi\left(\frac{x_{2}}{\epsilon}\right)
and
\tilde{\theta}_{\epsilon}(h_{x},x_{2})=\theta_{\epsilon}(h_{x})\chi_{\epsilon}(x_{2})
for
(h_{x},x_{2})\in{\mathbb{H}}\times{\mathbb{R}}
.
Let
f\in\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{H}}^{1,1})
: we have
f*_{{\mathbb{H}}^{1,1}}\tilde{\theta}_{\epsilon}\in\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{H}}^{1,1})
and
for
(h_{x},x_{2})\in{\mathbb{H}}\times{\mathbb{R}}
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{U}_{\nu_{0}}^{\hbar}(f*\tilde{\theta}_{\epsilon})(h_{x},x_{2})=\hbar^{-7}\int_{\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}}{\rm Tr}\left(\tilde{\pi}^{\delta}(\hbar^{-1}\cdot h_{x})e^{i\beta\frac{x_{2}}{\hbar}}U_{\nu_{0}}^{\hbar}(x,\tilde{\pi}^{\delta})\mathcal{F}_{{\mathbb{H}}^{1,1}_{pt}}^{\hbar}(\tilde{\theta}_{\epsilon})(\check{\pi}^{\delta,\hbar^{-1}\beta})\mathcal{F}_{{\mathbb{H}}^{1,1}_{pt}}^{\hbar}f(\check{\pi}^{\delta,\hbar^{-1}\beta})\right)\,d\mu_{\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}}(\pi^{\delta,\beta})

\displaystyle=\hbar^{-6}\int_{\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}\times{\mathbb{R}}}\int_{{\mathbb{H}}\times{\mathbb{R}}}{\rm Tr}\left(\tilde{\pi}^{\delta}\left(\hbar^{-1}\cdot(h_{y}^{-1}h_{x})\right)e^{i\beta(x_{2}-y_{2})}U_{\nu_{0}}^{\hbar}(x,\tilde{\pi}^{\delta})\mathcal{F}_{{\mathbb{H}}^{1,1}_{pt}}^{\hbar}(\tilde{\theta}_{\epsilon})(\check{\pi}^{\delta,\beta})\right)f(h_{y},y_{2})\,d\mu_{\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}}(\tilde{\pi}^{\delta})d\beta dh_{y}dy_{2}.

We almost recognize an inverse Fourier formula in the variables
y_{2}
and
\beta
: for this, we write

\mathcal{F}_{{\mathbb{H}}^{1,1}_{pt}}^{\hbar}(\tilde{\theta}_{\epsilon})(\check{\pi}^{\delta,\beta})=\mathcal{F}_{{\mathbb{R}}}\chi_{\epsilon}(\beta)\mathcal{F}_{{\mathbb{H}}}^{\hbar}\theta_{\epsilon}(\tilde{\pi}^{\delta}).

And as we have the following asymptotic in
\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{R}}_{2})

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}e^{i\beta(x_{2}-y_{2})}\widehat{\chi_{\epsilon}}(\beta)f(h_{y},y_{2})\,d\beta dy_{2}\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}f(h_{y},x_{2}),

uniformly in
h_{y}\in{\mathbb{H}}
, we are left with

\mathbb{U}_{\nu_{0}}^{\hbar}(f\,*\,\tilde{\theta}_{\epsilon})(h_{x},x_{2})=\hbar^{-6}\int_{\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}}\int_{{\mathbb{H}}}{\rm Tr}\left(\tilde{\pi}^{\delta}\left(\hbar^{-1}\cdot(h_{y}^{-1}h_{x})\right)U_{\nu_{0}}^{\hbar}(x_{2},\tilde{\pi}^{\delta})\mathcal{F}_{{\mathbb{H}}}^{\hbar}\theta_{\epsilon}(\tilde{\pi}^{\delta})\right)f(h_{y},x_{2})\,d\mu_{\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}}(\tilde{\pi}^{\delta})dh_{y}+\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{E}})}(\epsilon).

Notice now that we have

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbb{U}_{\nu_{0}}^{\hbar}(f-f*\tilde{\theta}_{\epsilon})\right\|_{L^{\infty}({\mathbb{E}})}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|\mathcal{F}_{{\mathbb{H}}_{pt}^{1,1}}^{\hbar}(f-f*\tilde{\theta}_{\epsilon})\right\|_{L^{1}(\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}^{1,1})}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|\mathcal{F}_{{\mathbb{H}}_{pt}^{1,1}}^{\hbar}\left((1+\Delta_{{\mathbb{H}}^{1,1}})^{N}(f-f*\tilde{\theta}_{\epsilon})\right)\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}^{1,1})}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|(1+\Delta_{{\mathbb{H}}^{1,1}})^{N}(f-f*\tilde{\theta}_{\epsilon})\right\|_{L^{1}({\mathbb{H}}^{1,1})}.

where we have introduced the subLaplacian
\Delta_{{\mathbb{H}}^{1,1}}=\sum_{1\leq i\leq 3}Y_{i}^{2}
on the quasi-Heisenberg group and we have chosen
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
large enough and independently of
\epsilon
.
Since
f
is in
\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{H}}^{1,1})
, the last term goes to 0 when
\epsilon
tends to 0. We conclude that the integral formula holds true and the convergence is uniform on
{\mathbb{E}}
.
∎

As a technical tool, we define a similar operator as
\mathbb{U}_{\nu_{0}}^{\hbar}
where we introduce a cut-off function away from the neighborhood
\{\delta=0\}
.

Definition 4.14
.

Let
g\in C^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}})
a smooth function equal to 0 on
(-1/2,1/2)
and to 1 outside
(-1,1)
. For
\delta_{0}>0
, we introduce the operators
\mathbb{U}_{\nu_{0},\delta_{0}}^{\hbar}:L^{2}({\mathbb{H}}^{1,1})\rightarrow L^{2}({\mathbb{E}})
defined by

\mathbb{U}_{\nu_{0},\delta_{0}}^{\hbar}=\left(\mathcal{F}_{{\mathbb{E}}}^{\hbar}\right)^{-1}\circ D_{\delta_{0}}^{\hbar}\circ T_{\nu_{0}}^{*}\circ\mathcal{F}_{{\mathbb{H}}_{pt}^{1,1}}^{\hbar},

where the operator
D_{\delta_{0}}^{\hbar}:L^{2}(\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}})\rightarrow L^{2}(\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}})
is defined by

D_{\delta_{0}}^{\hbar}\tau(\pi^{\delta,\beta}):=g\left(\frac{\delta}{\delta_{0}}\right)\tau(\pi^{\delta,\frac{\beta}{\hbar}}).

The operators
\mathbb{U}_{\nu_{0},\delta_{0}}^{\hbar}
also admit an integral representation: for
f\in\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{H}}^{1,1})
and
(h_{x},x_{2})\in{\mathbb{E}}
,

\mathbb{U}_{\nu_{0},\delta_{0}}^{\hbar}f(h_{x},x_{2})=\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\hbar^{-6}\int_{\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}\times{\mathbb{H}}}{\rm Tr}\left(\tilde{\pi}^{\delta}\left(\hbar^{-1}\cdot(h_{y}^{-1}h_{x})\right)U_{\nu_{0}}^{\hbar}(x_{2},\tilde{\pi}^{\delta})g\left(\frac{\delta}{\delta_{0}}\right)\mathcal{F}_{{\mathbb{H}}}^{\hbar}\theta_{\epsilon}(\tilde{\pi}^{\delta})\right)\\
\times f(h_{y},x_{2})\,d\mu_{\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}}(\tilde{\pi}^{\delta})dh_{y},

with uniform convergence as
\epsilon
goes to 0.

4.2.3.
Intertwining of the two quantizations

The next Theorem is the fundamental result of this section as it relates the second-microlocal semiclassical analysis of the Engel group
{\mathbb{E}}
to the partial semiclassical analysis of
{\mathbb{H}}^{1,1}
(that will be studied in more detail later)
in a straightforward way.

Lemma 4.15
.

Let
\sigma\in\mathcal{A}_{0}^{2}({\mathbb{E}})
a second-microlocal symbol supported in
{\mathbb{E}}\times\mathcal{O}_{\delta_{0}}({\mathbb{E}})\times[-R,R]
for some
\delta_{0}>0
and
R>0
.
Then the symbol
\tau
defined on
{\mathbb{H}}^{1,1}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}^{1,1}_{\rm gen}
by

\forall x\in{\mathbb{E}}\sim{\mathbb{H}}^{1,1},\forall(\delta,\beta)\in\mathfrak{g}_{3}^{*}\setminus\{0\}\times\mathfrak{r}_{2}^{*},\ \tau(x,\check{\pi}^{\delta,\beta})=\sigma(x,\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}},\beta),

is a smoothing symbol, i.e
\tau
belongs to
\mathcal{A}_{0}({\mathbb{H}}^{1,1})
.

This lemma allow us to make sense of our main result of the section.

Theorem 4.16
.

Let
\sigma\in\mathcal{A}_{0}^{2}({\mathbb{E}})
with compact support on the last variable
\eta
.
Then if we define the symbol
\tau\in\mathcal{A}_{0}({\mathbb{H}}^{1,1})
as in Lemma
4.15
, we have

{\rm Op}_{\hbar,{\mathbb{E}}}^{C_{\nu_{0}}}(\sigma)=\mathbb{U}_{\nu_{0}}^{\hbar}\circ{\rm Op}_{\hbar,{\mathbb{H}}_{pt}^{1,1}}(\tau)\circ\mathbb{U}_{\nu_{0}}^{\hbar,*}+\mathcal{O}_{L^{2}\rightarrow L^{2}}(\hbar).

The rest of the Section is devoted to the proof of Lemma
4.15
and Theorem
4.16
.

Proof of Lemma
4.15
.

Let
\sigma\in\mathcal{A}_{0}^{2}({\mathbb{E}})
as above. By definition
4.1
and by compacity of
\sigma
in the variable
\eta
, the associated kernel
\kappa
is in

C_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}_{\eta},C_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{E}}_{x},\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{E}}_{y})))
. For
x\in{\mathbb{H}}^{1,1}
, the measurable symbol
\tau(x,\cdot)
is in
L^{\infty}(\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}^{1,1})
and thus it is the Fourier transform
of a distribution
\kappa^{\tau}_{x}(\cdot)\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}({\mathbb{H}}^{1,1})
:

\tau(x,\cdot)=\mathcal{F}_{{\mathbb{H}}^{1,1}}\kappa^{\tau}_{x}(\cdot).

As smoothness in the variable
x
is easily checked, we focus on proving smoothness and decay with respect to the variable
y
of the distribution
\kappa^{\tau}_{x}
.

First, we prove boundedness and continuity in
y
. To this end, we show that
\tau(x,\cdot)
is in
L^{1}(\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}^{1,1})
: indeed we can write, for
N\in{\mathbb{N}}_{>0}
,

\displaystyle\sigma(x,\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}},\beta)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}_{{\mathbb{E}}}(\kappa_{x}(\cdot,\beta))(\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}})

\displaystyle=H(\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}})^{-N}H(\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}})^{N}\mathcal{F}_{{\mathbb{E}}}(\kappa_{x}(\cdot,\beta))(\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}})

\displaystyle=H(\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}})^{-N}\mathcal{F}_{{\mathbb{E}}}(\Delta_{{\mathbb{E}}}^{N}\kappa_{x}(\cdot,\beta))(\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}}).

As
H(\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}})
is unitarily equivalent to the operator

\delta^{2/3}\left(-\partial_{\xi}^{2}+\left(\nu_{0}+\frac{1}{2}\xi^{2}\right)^{2}\right),

for
N
large enough the operator
\delta^{2N/3}H(\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}})^{-N}
is trace-class, with a uniformly bounded trace norm (see
[
2
]
). As the support
of
\tau(x,\cdot)
is included in
\mathcal{O}_{\delta_{0}}(\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}^{1,1})
, up to choosing
N
large enough,
{\mathbb{1}}_{\delta>\delta_{0}}\delta^{-2N/3}
is integrable for the
|\delta|d\delta
part of
the Plancherel measure on
\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}^{1,1}
and as
\kappa
is compactly supported with respect to the variable
\eta
, we obtain the integrability of
{\rm Tr}(|\tau(x,\cdot)|)
for
d\mu_{\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}^{1,1}}
as desired.

We are then allowed to use the Fourier inversion formula and write

(4.9)

\kappa^{\tau}_{x}(y)=\int_{\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}^{1,1}}{\rm Tr}\left(\check{\pi}(y)\tau(x,\check{\pi})\right)d\mu_{\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}^{1,1}}(\check{\pi}).

We see by this equation that
\kappa^{\tau}_{x}(\cdot)
is continuous and uniformly bounded.

Now, for a left-invariant vector field
Y
on
{\mathbb{H}}^{1,1}
, formula (
4.9
) would give

Y_{y}\kappa^{\tau}_{x}(y)=\int_{\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}^{1,1}}{\rm Tr}\left(\check{\pi}(y)\check{\pi}(Y)\tau(x,\check{\pi})\right)d\mu_{\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}^{1,1}}(\check{\pi}),

if we had
\check{\pi}\in\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}^{1,1}\mapsto\check{\pi}(Y)\tau(x,\check{\pi})
also in
L^{1}(\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}^{1,1})
. As we have

(4.10)

\check{\pi}^{\delta,\beta}(Y_{2})=i\beta,\quad\check{\pi}^{\delta,\beta}(Y_{i})=\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}}(X_{i}),\ i=1,3,4,

we can write for
i=1,3,4
,

\check{\pi}^{\delta,\beta}(Y_{i})\tau(x,\check{\pi}^{\delta,\beta})=\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}}(X_{i})\sigma(x,\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}},\beta)=\mathcal{F}_{{\mathbb{E}}}(X_{i}\kappa_{x}(\cdot,\beta))(\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}}),

and

\check{\pi}^{\delta,\beta}(Y_{2})\tau(x,\check{\pi}^{\delta,\beta})=\mathcal{F}_{{\mathbb{E}}}(i\beta\kappa_{x}(\cdot,\beta))(\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}}).

Recursively and applying the same previous reasoning, we obtain smoothness in
y
.

For decay, notice that for
j\in\{1,2,3,4\}
, Equation (
4.9
) gives

-y_{j}\kappa^{\tau}_{x}(y)=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\{0\}\times{\mathbb{R}}}{\rm Tr}\left(-\Delta_{j}^{{\mathbb{H}}^{1,1}}\check{\pi}^{\delta,\beta}(y)\tau(x,\check{\pi}^{\delta,\beta})\right)|\delta|d\delta d\beta,

where the difference operator
\Delta_{j}^{{\mathbb{H}}^{1,1}}
is applied to the field
\{\check{\pi}^{\delta,\beta}(y)\,:\,\check{\pi}^{\delta,\beta}\in\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}^{1,1}\}
. By Proposition
2.10
, we have

-y_{j}\kappa^{\tau}_{x}(y)=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\{0\}\times{\mathbb{R}}}{\rm Tr}\left(\check{\pi}^{\delta,\beta}(y)\Delta_{j}^{{\mathbb{H}}^{1,1}}\tau(x,\check{\pi}^{\delta,\beta})\right)|\delta|d\delta d\beta,

if
\check{\pi}\in\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}^{1,1}\mapsto\Delta_{j}^{{\mathbb{H}}^{1,1}}\tau(x,\check{\pi})
makes sense and is also in
L^{1}(\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}^{1,1})
.
As we can explicit the difference operators on
{\mathbb{H}}^{1,1}
as follows

\displaystyle\Delta_{1}^{{\mathbb{H}}^{1,1}}=\frac{i}{\delta}\left[\check{\pi}^{\delta,\beta}(Y_{3}),\cdot\right],\ \Delta_{2}^{{\mathbb{H}}^{1,1}}=\frac{1}{i}\partial_{\beta},\ \Delta_{3}^{{\mathbb{H}}^{1,1}}=-\frac{i}{\delta}\left[\check{\pi}^{\delta,\beta}(Y_{1}),\cdot\right],

\displaystyle\Delta_{4}^{{\mathbb{H}}^{1,1}}=\frac{1}{i}\partial_{\delta}+\frac{1}{2}\Delta_{1}^{{\mathbb{H}}^{1,1}}\Delta_{3}^{{\mathbb{H}}^{1,1}}+\frac{i}{\delta}\check{\pi}^{\delta,\beta}(Y_{3})\Delta_{3}^{{\mathbb{H}}^{1,1}},

using the relations (
4.10
), we see that the action of a difference operator for the quasi-Heisenberg group easily translates into
a difference operator on the Engel group acting on the symbol
\sigma
or into derivatives along the variable
\eta
. We conclude this way by recurrence that

\kappa_{x}^{\tau}
is in
\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{H}}^{1,1})
.
∎

The following lemma is a necessary tool in the proof of Theorem
4.16
as it allows us to microlocalize away from a neighborhood of
\{\delta=0\}
.

Lemma 4.17
.

Let
\tau\in\mathcal{A}_{0}({\mathbb{H}}^{1,1})
be a symbol with support in
{\mathbb{H}}^{1,1}\times\mathcal{O}_{\delta_{0}}(\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}^{1,1})
for a certain
\delta_{0}>0
. Then we have

\mathbb{U}_{\nu_{0}}^{\hbar}\circ{\rm Op}_{\hbar,{\mathbb{H}}_{pt}^{1,1}}(\tau)=\mathbb{U}_{\nu_{0},\delta_{0}}^{\hbar}\circ{\rm Op}_{\hbar,{\mathbb{H}}_{pt}^{1,1}}(\tau)+\mathcal{O}_{L^{2}\rightarrow L^{2}}(\hbar).

Proof.

Let
g\in C^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}})
as in
4.14
, i.e equal to 0 on
(-1/2,1/2)
and 1 outside
(-1,1)
.
Then the symbol
\sigma_{g_{\delta_{0}}}=\{g_{\delta_{0}}(\delta)=g\left(\frac{\delta}{\delta_{0}}\right){\rm Id}\,:\,\check{\pi}^{\delta,\beta}\in\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}^{1,1}\}
is in
S_{0,0}^{0}({\mathbb{H}}^{1,1})
and satisfies by definition

\mathbb{U}_{\nu_{0},\delta_{0}}^{\hbar}=\mathbb{U}_{\nu_{0}}^{\hbar}\circ{\rm Op}_{\hbar,{\mathbb{H}}_{pt}^{1,1}}(\sigma_{g_{\delta_{0}}}).

Thus it is enough to show that
{\rm Op}_{\hbar,{\mathbb{H}}_{pt}^{1,1}}(\sigma_{g_{\delta_{0}}})\circ{\rm Op}_{\hbar,{\mathbb{H}}_{pt}^{1,1}}(\tau)={\rm Op}_{\hbar,{\mathbb{H}}_{pt}^{1,1}}(\tau)+\mathcal{O}(\hbar)
.
Note that the operators
{\rm Op}_{\hbar,{\mathbb{H}}_{pt}^{1,1}}(\sigma_{g_{\delta_{0}}})
acts by convolution with the following kernel:

\kappa^{g_{\delta_{0}},\hbar}(y)=\hbar^{-3}\delta_{(y_{1},y_{2},y_{3})=0}\otimes\mathcal{F}_{{\mathbb{R}}}^{-1}g_{\delta_{0}}(\hbar^{-3}y_{4}),\quad y\in{\mathbb{H}}^{1,1},

while, if we denoteby
\kappa^{\tau}
the convolution kernel of
\tau
, the operator
{\rm Op}_{\hbar,{\mathbb{H}}_{pt}^{1,1}}(\tau)
acts by

{\rm Op}_{\hbar,{\mathbb{H}}_{pt}^{1,1}}(\tau)f(x)=f*\kappa_{x}^{\tau,\hbar}(x),\quad f\in\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{H}}^{1,1}),\ x\in{\mathbb{H}}^{1,1}.

with
\kappa_{x}^{\tau,\hbar}(y)=\hbar^{-6}\kappa_{x}^{\tau}(\hbar^{-1}\cdot_{pt}y)
.
Thus the composition of the two operators has a convolution kernel
\kappa_{x}^{\hbar}(z)=\hbar^{-6}\kappa_{x}(\hbar^{-1}\cdot_{pt}z)
given by

\displaystyle\kappa_{x}^{\hbar}(y^{-1}x)

\displaystyle=\hbar^{-9}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}\mathcal{F}_{{\mathbb{R}}}^{-1}g_{\delta_{0}}(\hbar^{-3}(x_{4}-v_{4}))\kappa_{(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},v_{4})}^{\tau}(\hbar^{-1}\cdot_{pt}(y^{-1}(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},v_{4})))\,dv_{4}

\displaystyle=\hbar^{-6}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}\mathcal{F}_{{\mathbb{R}}}^{-1}g_{\delta_{0}}(u_{4})\kappa_{x(\hbar\cdot_{pt}u_{4}^{-1})}^{\tau}((\hbar^{-1}\cdot_{pt}y^{-1}x)u_{4}^{-1})\,du_{4},

and with the change of variable
z=\hbar^{-1}\cdot_{pt}(y^{-1}x)
,

\kappa_{x}(z)=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}\mathcal{F}_{{\mathbb{R}}}^{-1}g_{\delta_{0}}(u_{4})\kappa_{x(\hbar\cdot_{pt}u_{4}^{-1})}^{\tau}(zu_{4}^{-1})\,du_{4}.

We can perform a Taylor expansion of the ma

u_{4}\in{\mathbb{R}}\mapsto\kappa_{x(\hbar\cdot_{pt}u_{4}^{-1})}^{\tau}(z)
:

\kappa_{x(\hbar\cdot_{pt}u_{4}^{-1})}^{\tau}(z)=\kappa_{x}^{\tau}(z)-\hbar^{3}u_{4}\int_{0}^{1}X_{4,x}\kappa_{x(\hbar\cdot_{pt}(tu_{4})^{-1})}(z)\,dt,

and we deduce

\kappa_{x}(z)=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}\mathcal{F}_{{\mathbb{R}}}^{-1}g_{\delta_{0}}(u_{4})\kappa_{x}^{\tau}(zu_{4}^{-1})\,du_{4}-\hbar^{3}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}u_{4}\mathcal{F}_{{\mathbb{R}}}^{-1}g_{\delta_{0}}(u_{4})X_{4,x}\kappa_{x(\hbar\cdot_{pt}u_{4}^{-1})}^{\tau}(zu_{4}^{-1})\,du_{4}.

The first term in the right-hand side is smooth and compactly supported in
x
and Schwartz in
z
, and defines, by taking its Fourier transform, a symbol
\sigma
in
\mathcal{A}_{0}({\mathbb{H}}^{1,1})
equal to

\sigma=\sigma_{g_{\delta_{0}}}\circ\tau=\tau,

by condition on the support of
\tau
. Finally, we see that the operator of convolution kernel equal to

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}u_{4}\mathcal{F}_{{\mathbb{R}}}^{-1}g_{\delta_{0}}(u_{4})X_{4,x}\kappa_{x(\hbar\cdot_{pt}u_{4}^{-1})}^{\tau}(zu_{4}^{-1})\,du_{4},

is bounded on
L^{2}({\mathbb{H}}^{1,1})
by Schur’s lemma, uniformly in
\hbar
, and we obtain the desired equality.
∎

Proof of Theorem
4.16
.

As
\sigma
is a symbol in
\mathcal{A}_{0}^{2}({\mathbb{E}})
, there exists
\delta_{0}>0
such that
\sigma
is supported in

{\mathbb{E}}\times\mathcal{O}_{\delta_{0}}(\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}})\times[-R,R]
for
R>0
large enough.
By Lemma
4.15
we know that the measurable symbol
\tau
defines indeed a smoothing symbol on
{\mathbb{H}}^{1,1}
.

First, we observe that, for
f\in\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{H}}^{1,1})
and
x=(h_{x},x_{2})\in{\mathbb{E}}
, we can write

\displaystyle\rm{\displaystyle Op}_{\hbar}^{C_{\nu_{0}}}(\sigma)(\mathbb{U}_{\nu_{0}}^{\hbar}f)(x)=\hbar^{-7}\int_{\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}}{\rm Tr}\left(\pi^{\delta,\beta}(\hbar^{-1}\cdot x)\sigma_{\hbar}^{C_{\nu_{0}}}(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta})\mathcal{F}_{{\mathbb{E}}}^{\hbar}(\mathbb{U}_{\nu_{0}}^{\hbar}f)(\pi^{\delta,\beta})\right)d\mu_{\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}}(\pi^{\delta,\beta})

\displaystyle=\hbar^{-7}\int_{\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}}{\rm Tr}\left(\pi^{\delta,\beta}(\hbar^{-1}\cdot x)\sigma_{\hbar}^{C_{\nu_{0}}}(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta})(D^{\hbar}\circ T_{\nu_{0}}^{*}\circ\mathcal{F}_{{\mathbb{H}}^{1,1}_{pt}}^{\hbar})f(\pi^{\delta,\beta})\right)d\mu_{\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}}(\pi^{\delta,\beta})

\displaystyle=\hbar^{-6}\int_{\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}}{\rm Tr}\left(\tilde{\pi}^{\delta}(\hbar^{-1}\cdot h_{x})e^{i\beta x_{2}}U_{\nu_{0}}^{\hbar}(x_{2},\tilde{\pi}^{\delta})\sigma_{\hbar}^{C_{\nu_{0}}}(x,\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}+\hbar\beta})\mathcal{F}_{{\mathbb{H}}^{1,1}_{pt}}^{\hbar}f(\check{\pi}^{\delta,\beta})\right)d\mu_{\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}}(\pi^{\delta,\beta})

\displaystyle=\hbar^{-6}\int_{\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}}\int_{{\mathbb{H}}\times{\mathbb{R}}}{\rm Tr}\left(\tilde{\pi}^{\delta}(\hbar^{-1}\cdot(h_{y}^{-1}h_{x}))e^{i\beta(x_{2}-y_{2})}U_{\nu_{0}}^{\hbar}(x_{2},\tilde{\pi}^{\delta})\sigma_{\hbar}^{C_{\nu_{0}}}(x,\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}+\hbar\beta})\right)f(h_{y},y_{2})\,dh_{y}dy_{2}d\mu_{\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}}(\pi^{\delta,\beta})

\displaystyle=\hbar^{-6}\int_{\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}}\int_{{\mathbb{H}}\times{\mathbb{R}}}{\rm Tr}\left(\check{\pi}^{\delta,\beta}(\hbar^{-1}\cdot_{pt}(y^{-1}x))U_{\nu_{0}}^{\hbar}(x_{2},\tilde{\pi}^{\delta})\sigma(x,\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}+\hbar\beta},\beta)\right)f(h_{y},y_{2})\,dh_{y}dy_{2}d\mu_{\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}}(\pi^{\delta,\beta}).

If we denote by
\kappa
the convolution kernel of
\sigma
(see Definition
4.1
), notice that we have

\displaystyle\sigma(x,\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}+\hbar\beta},\beta)-\sigma(x,\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}},\beta)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}_{{\mathbb{E}}}\kappa_{x}(\cdot,\beta)(\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}+\hbar\beta})-\mathcal{F}_{{\mathbb{E}}}\kappa_{x}(\cdot,\beta)(\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}})

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathbb{E}}}\kappa_{x}(y,\beta)\left(\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}+\hbar\beta}(y)^{*}-\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}}(y)^{*}\right)\,dy

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathbb{E}}}\kappa_{x}(y,\beta)\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}+\hbar\beta}(y)^{*}(1-e^{i\hbar\beta y_{2}})\,dy

\displaystyle=-i\hbar\int_{0}^{1}\int_{{\mathbb{E}}}\beta y_{2}\kappa_{x}(y,\beta)e^{it\hbar\beta y_{2}}\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}+\hbar\beta}(y)^{*}\,dydt.

For
t\in[0,1]
, thanks to the support condition on the variable
\eta
, we introduce the kernel
\tilde{\kappa}^{t,\hbar}\in C_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}_{\eta},C_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{E}}_{x},\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{E}}_{y})))
defined by

\tilde{\kappa}^{t,\hbar}_{x}(y,\eta)=\eta y_{2}e^{it\hbar\eta y_{2}}\kappa_{x}(y,\eta).

If we denote by
\sigma_{t,\hbar}
the associated second-microlocal symbol, the previous equation writes

\sigma(x,\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}+\hbar\beta},\beta)-\sigma(x,\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}},\beta)=-i\hbar\int_{0}^{1}\sigma_{t,\hbar}(x,\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}+\hbar\beta},\beta)\,dt.

Thus we have

{\rm Op}_{\hbar}^{C_{\nu_{0}}}(\sigma)(\mathbb{U}_{\nu_{0}}^{\hbar}f)(x)=\hbar^{-6}\int_{\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}}\int_{{\mathbb{H}}\times{\mathbb{R}}}{\rm Tr}\left(\check{\pi}^{\delta,\beta}(\hbar^{-1}\cdot_{pt}(y^{-1}x))U_{\nu_{0}}^{\hbar}(x_{2},\tilde{\pi}^{\delta})\sigma(x,\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}},\beta)\right)\\
f(h_{y},y_{2})\,dh_{y}dy_{2}d\mu_{\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}}(\pi^{\delta,\beta})-i\hbar\int_{0}^{1}{\rm Op}_{\hbar}^{C_{\nu_{0}}}(\sigma_{t,\hbar})(\mathbb{U}_{\nu_{0}}^{\hbar}f)(x)\,dt.

By looking at the definition of
\kappa^{t,\hbar}
, we see that the seminorm
N_{2}(\sigma_{t,\hbar})
is uniformly bounded in
t\in[0,1]
and
\hbar\in(0,1)
. We have thus proved by Proposition
4.5
that we have:

{\rm Op}_{\hbar}^{C_{\nu_{0}}}(\sigma)(\mathbb{U}_{\nu_{0}}^{\hbar}f)(x)=\hbar^{-6}\int_{{\mathbb{H}}^{1,1}}\int_{{\mathbb{H}}\times{\mathbb{R}}}{\rm Tr}\left(\check{\pi}^{\delta,\beta}(\hbar^{-1}\cdot_{pt}(y^{-1}x))U_{\nu_{0}}^{\hbar}(x_{2},\tilde{\pi}^{\delta})\tau(x,\check{\pi}^{\delta,\beta})\right)\\
\times f(y)\,dyd\mu_{\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}^{1,1}}(\check{\pi}^{\delta,\beta})+\mathcal{O}(\hbar)\|f\|_{L^{2}}.

We now compute the composition of
\mathbb{U}_{\nu_{0}}^{\hbar}
with
{\rm Op}_{{\mathbb{H}}^{1,1}_{pt}}^{\hbar}(\tau)
. Remark that
\tau
is a symbol in
\mathcal{A}_{0}({\mathbb{H}}^{1,1})
with support included in
{\mathbb{E}}\times\mathcal{O}_{\delta_{0}}(\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}^{1,1})
.
By Lemma
4.17
, we can instead look at
\mathbb{U}_{\nu_{0},\delta_{0}}^{\hbar}\circ{\rm Op}_{\hbar,{\mathbb{H}}^{1,1}}(\tau)
.
Using Proposition
4.13
with an approximation of identity defined by

\forall h\in{\mathbb{H}},\ \theta_{\epsilon}(h)=\epsilon^{-6}\theta(\epsilon^{-1}\cdot h),\ \epsilon>0,

with
\theta\in\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{H}})
satisfying
\theta(0)=1
, we can write

\mathbb{U}_{\nu_{0},\delta_{0}}^{\hbar}\left({\rm Op}_{{\mathbb{H}}^{1,1}_{pt}}^{\hbar}(\tau)f\right)(x)=\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\hbar^{-6}\int_{\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}}\int_{{\mathbb{H}}}{\rm Tr}\left(\tilde{\pi}^{\delta}(\hbar^{-1}\cdot(h_{w}^{-1}h_{x}))U_{\nu_{0}}^{\hbar}(x_{2},\tilde{\pi}^{\delta})g_{\delta_{0}}(\delta)\mathcal{F}_{{\mathbb{H}}}^{\hbar}\theta_{\epsilon}(\tilde{\pi}^{\delta})\right)\\
{\rm Op}_{{\mathbb{H}}^{1,1}_{pt}}^{\hbar}(\tau)f(h_{w},x_{2})\,d\mu_{\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}}(\tilde{\pi}^{\delta})dh_{w}\\
=\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\hbar^{-12}\int_{\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}}\int_{{\mathbb{H}}}\int_{\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}^{1,1}}\int_{{\mathbb{H}}\times{\mathbb{R}}}{\rm Tr}\left(\tilde{\pi}^{\delta}(\hbar^{-1}\cdot(h_{w}^{-1}h_{x}))U_{\nu_{0}}^{\hbar}(x_{2},\tilde{\pi}^{\delta})g_{\delta_{0}}(\delta)\mathcal{F}_{{\mathbb{H}}}^{\hbar}\theta_{\epsilon}(\tilde{\pi}^{\delta})\right)\\
{\rm Tr}\left(\tilde{\pi}^{\delta^{\prime}}(\hbar^{-1}\cdot(h_{y}^{-1}h_{w}))e^{i\beta(x_{2}-y_{2})}\tau(h_{w},x_{2},\check{\pi}^{\delta^{\prime},\beta})\right)f(h_{y},y_{2})\,d\mu_{\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}^{1,1}}(\check{\pi}^{\delta^{\prime},\beta})dh_{y}dy_{2}d\mu_{\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}}(\tilde{\pi}^{\delta})dh_{w}\\
=\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\hbar^{-12}\int_{\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}}\int_{{\mathbb{H}}}\int_{\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}^{1,1}}\int_{{\mathbb{H}}\times{\mathbb{R}}}{\rm Tr}\left(\tilde{\pi}^{\delta}(\hbar^{-1}\cdot h_{z})^{*}U_{\nu_{0}}^{\hbar}(x_{2},\tilde{\pi}^{\delta})g_{\delta_{0}}(\delta)\mathcal{F}_{{\mathbb{H}}}^{\hbar}\theta_{\epsilon}(\tilde{\pi}^{\delta})\right)\\
{\rm Tr}\left(\tilde{\pi}^{\delta^{\prime}}(\hbar^{-1}\cdot h_{z})e^{i\beta(x_{2}-y_{2})}\tau(h_{x}h_{z},x_{2},\check{\pi}^{\delta^{\prime},\beta})\tilde{\pi}^{\delta^{\prime}}(\hbar^{-1}\cdot(h_{y}^{-1}h_{x}))\right)\\
\times f(h_{y},y_{2})\,d\mu_{\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}^{1,1}}(\check{\pi}^{\delta^{\prime},\beta})dh_{y}dy_{2}d\mu_{\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}}(\tilde{\pi}^{\delta})dh_{z},

where we have performed the change of variable
h_{z}=h_{x}^{-1}h_{w}
.

We recognize in the last term the composition of a Fourier transform on
{\mathbb{H}}
(with the variable
h_{z}
) with an inverse Fourier transform with the variable
\tilde{\pi}^{\delta^{\prime}}
.
There is however an obstruction present in the dependence in the symbol
\tau
of the variable
h_{z}
.

Noting
h_{z}={\rm Exp}_{{\mathbb{H}}}(z_{1}X_{1}+z_{3}X_{3}+z_{4}X_{4})
, the mean value theorem on the Heisenberg group gives

\tau(h_{x}h_{z},x_{2},\check{\pi}^{\delta^{\prime},\beta})=\tau(h_{x},x_{2},\check{\pi}^{\delta^{\prime},\beta})+\int_{0}^{1}(z_{1}X_{1}+z_{3}X_{3}+z_{4}X_{4})\tau(h_{x}(th_{z}),x_{2},\check{\pi}^{\delta^{\prime},\beta})\,dt.

In the precedent computation of the composition of
\mathbb{U}_{\nu_{0}}^{\hbar}
with
{\rm Op}_{{\mathbb{H}}^{1,1}_{pt}}^{\hbar}(\tau)
, the first term in the mean value theorem gives

\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\hbar^{-6}\int_{\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}^{1,1}}\int_{{\mathbb{H}}\times{\mathbb{R}}}{\rm Tr}\left(e^{i\beta(x_{2}-y_{2})}\tau(h_{x},x_{2},\check{\pi}^{\delta^{\prime},\beta})\tilde{\pi}^{\delta^{\prime}}(\hbar^{-1}\cdot(h_{y}^{-1}h_{x}))U_{\nu_{0}}^{\hbar}(x_{2},\tilde{\pi}^{\delta^{\prime}})g_{\delta_{0}}(\delta^{\prime})\mathcal{F}_{{\mathbb{H}}}^{\hbar}\theta_{\epsilon}(\tilde{\pi}^{\delta^{\prime}})\right)\\
\times f(h_{y},y_{2})\,d\mu_{\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}^{1,1}}(\check{\pi}^{\delta^{\prime},\beta})dh_{y}dy_{2}\\
=\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\hbar^{-6}\int_{\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}^{1,1}}\int_{{\mathbb{H}}\times{\mathbb{R}}}{\rm Tr}\left(\tilde{\pi}^{\delta^{\prime}}(\hbar^{-1}\cdot(h_{y}^{-1}h_{x}))e^{i\beta(x_{2}-y_{2})}U_{\nu_{0}}^{\hbar}(x_{2},\tilde{\pi}^{\delta^{\prime}})g_{\delta_{0}}(\delta^{\prime})\mathcal{F}_{{\mathbb{H}}}^{\hbar}\theta_{\epsilon}(\tilde{\pi}^{\delta^{\prime}})\tau(h_{x},x_{2},\check{\pi}^{\delta^{\prime},\beta})\right)\\
\times f(h_{y},y_{2})\,d\mu_{\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}^{1,1}}(\check{\pi}^{\delta^{\prime},\beta})dh_{y}dy_{2},

which is equal, in the limit
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
, by the previous computations, to

{\rm Op}_{\hbar}^{C_{\nu_{0}}}(\sigma)(\mathbb{U}_{\nu_{0}}^{\hbar}f)(h_{x},x_{2})+\mathcal{O}(\hbar)\|f\|_{L^{2}}.

We are left with proving that the remainder of the mean value theorem gives rise to an operator of norm
\mathcal{O}_{L^{2}\rightarrow L^{2}}(\hbar)
.
To this end, we fix
t\in[0,1]
and remark that for any
i=1,3,4
, we have

\displaystyle\tilde{\pi}^{\delta}(\hbar^{-1}\cdot h_{z})^{*}(\tilde{\pi}^{\delta})\mathcal{F}^{\hbar}_{\mathbb{H}}\theta_{\epsilon}(\tilde{\pi}^{\delta}){\rm Tr}\left(\tilde{\pi}^{\delta^{\prime}}(\hbar^{-1}\cdot h_{z})e^{i\beta(x_{2}-y_{2})}(z_{i}X_{i}\tau)(h_{x}(th_{z}),x_{2},\check{\pi}^{\delta^{\prime},\beta})\tilde{\pi}^{\delta^{\prime}}(\hbar^{-1}\cdot(h_{y}^{-1}h_{x}))\right)

\displaystyle=z_{i}\tilde{\pi}^{\delta}(\hbar^{-1}\cdot h_{z})^{*}(\tilde{\pi}^{\delta})\mathcal{F}^{\hbar}_{\mathbb{H}}\theta_{\epsilon}(\tilde{\pi}^{\delta}){\rm Tr}\left(\tilde{\pi}^{\delta^{\prime}}(\hbar^{-1}\cdot h_{z})e^{i\beta(x_{2}-y_{2})}X_{i}\tau(h_{x}(th_{z}),x_{2},\check{\pi}^{\delta^{\prime},\beta})\tilde{\pi}^{\delta^{\prime}}(\hbar^{-1}\cdot(h_{y}^{-1}h_{x}))\right).

We observe that

z_{i}\tilde{\pi}^{\delta}(\hbar^{-1}\cdot h_{z})^{*}=\hbar^{\upsilon_{i}}\Delta_{i}^{{\mathbb{H}}}(\tilde{\pi}^{\delta}(\hbar^{-1}\cdot h_{z})^{*}),

where the right-hand side is the application of the difference operator
\Delta_{i}^{{\mathbb{H}}}
to the field
\{\tilde{\pi}^{\delta}(\hbar^{-1}\cdot h_{z})^{*}\,:\,\tilde{\pi}^{\delta}\in\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}\}
.
Applying an integration by part with respect to
\Delta_{i}^{{\mathbb{H}}}
(see Propostion
2.9
), we have

\int_{\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}}{\rm Tr}\left(z_{i}\tilde{\pi}^{\delta}(\hbar^{-1}\cdot h_{z})^{*}U_{\nu_{0}}^{\hbar}(x_{2},\tilde{\pi}^{\delta})g_{\delta_{0}}(\delta)\mathcal{F}_{{\mathbb{H}}}^{\hbar}\theta_{\epsilon}(\tilde{\pi}^{\delta})\right)d\mu_{\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}}(\tilde{\pi}^{\delta})\\
=-\hbar^{\upsilon_{i}}\int_{\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}}{\rm Tr}\left(\tilde{\pi}^{\delta}(\hbar^{-1}\cdot h_{z})^{*}\Delta_{i}^{{\mathbb{H}}}\left(U_{\nu_{0}}^{\hbar}(x_{2},\cdot)g_{\delta_{0}}\mathcal{F}_{{\mathbb{H}}}^{\hbar}\theta_{\epsilon}\right)(\tilde{\pi}^{\delta})\right)d\mu_{\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}}(\tilde{\pi}^{\delta}).

We need to compute the action of the difference operators on
U_{\nu_{0}}^{\hbar}(x_{2},\cdot)g_{\delta_{0}}\mathcal{F}_{{\mathbb{H}}}^{\hbar}\theta_{\epsilon}
.
First observe that we have

\Delta_{i}^{{\mathbb{H}}}\mathcal{F}_{{\mathbb{H}}}^{\hbar}\theta_{\epsilon}=\mathcal{F}_{{\mathbb{H}}}^{\hbar}(z_{i}\theta_{\epsilon})=\epsilon^{\upsilon_{i}}\mathcal{F}_{{\mathbb{H}}}^{\hbar}\theta_{\epsilon}^{i},

with
\theta_{\epsilon}^{i}(h_{z})=\epsilon^{-6}(z_{i}\theta)(\epsilon^{-1}\cdot h_{z})
, and thus, using the Leibniz formula
2.10
, these terms disappear in the limit
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
.
Moreover, the action of the difference operators on the field
g_{\delta_{0}}
gives 0 for all except
\Delta_{4}^{{\mathbb{H}}}
, and this will lead to a term of order
\mathcal{O}_{L^{2}\rightarrow L^{2}}(\hbar^{\upsilon_{4}})
.
We can then focus on the action of the difference operators on
U_{\nu_{0}}^{\hbar}(x_{2},\cdot)
.

The difference operators on the Heisenberg group for our particular choice of parametrisation of its dual
\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}
are given by the following formulae:

\Delta_{1}^{{\mathbb{H}}}=\frac{i}{\delta}\left[\tilde{\pi}^{\delta}(X_{3}),\cdot\right],\ \Delta_{3}^{{\mathbb{H}}}=-\frac{i}{\delta}\left[\tilde{\pi}^{\delta}(X_{1}),\cdot\right],\ \Delta_{4}^{{\mathbb{H}}}=\frac{1}{i}\partial_{\delta}+\frac{1}{2}\Delta_{1}^{{\mathbb{H}}}\Delta_{3}^{{\mathbb{H}}}+\frac{i}{\delta}\tilde{\pi}^{\delta}(X_{3})\Delta_{3}^{{\mathbb{H}}},

We then easily find

\displaystyle\Delta_{1}U_{\nu_{0}}^{\hbar}(x_{2},\cdot)=0,\ \Delta_{3}U_{\nu_{0}}^{\hbar}(x_{2},\cdot)=\hbar^{-1}x_{2}\frac{1}{i\delta}U_{\nu_{0}}^{\hbar}(x_{2},\cdot)\pi^{\delta,\beta}(X_{3}),

\displaystyle\Delta_{4}U_{\nu_{0}}^{\hbar}(x_{2},\cdot)=\hbar^{-1}x_{2}U_{\nu_{0}}^{\hbar}(x_{2},\cdot)\left(\frac{1}{3}\nu_{0}\delta^{-2/3}+\frac{1}{2}\delta^{-2}\pi^{\delta,\beta}(X_{3}^{2})\right).

The restriction enforced by the cutoff
g
gives that
\delta
is bounded by below and thus negative powers of
\delta
are dominated by powers of
\delta_{0}
.
Moreover, since
\upsilon_{3}=2
, the term obtained by integration by part with respect to
\Delta_{3}
is of order
\mathcal{O}_{L^{2}\rightarrow L^{2}}(\hbar)
, while the one
obtained via
\Delta_{4}
is of order
\mathcal{O}_{L^{2}\rightarrow L^{2}}(\hbar^{2})
.
∎

4.3.
Partial semiclassical analysis on the group
{\mathbb{H}}^{1,1}

As the Fourier integral operators introduced in the previous section allow us to work on the quasi-Heisenberg group,
we present here how the partial semiclassical analysis on
{\mathbb{H}}^{1,1}
can be reformulated as a particular instance of the semiclassical calculus introduced
in Appendix
A
.

The main observation to make is that we have the following isometric identification:

L^{2}({\mathbb{H}}^{1,1})=L^{2}({\mathbb{H}},L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}_{2})).

Letting
\mathcal{G}
denote the Hilbert space
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}_{2})
, we find ourselves in the setting of Appendix
A
.
Indeed, for
(h,\tilde{\pi}^{\delta})\in{\mathbb{H}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}
, we can associate to any symbol
\sigma
in
\mathcal{A}_{0}({\mathbb{H}}^{1,1})
an operator on
\mathcal{H}_{\tilde{\pi}^{\delta}}\otimes\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{H}_{\tilde{\pi}^{\delta}}\otimes L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}_{2})
in the following way:
for
\Phi_{1},\Phi_{2}\in\mathcal{H}_{\tilde{\pi}^{\delta}}
, we consider the scalar-valued smoothing symbol
\sigma_{\Phi_{1},\Phi_{2}}:(x_{2},\beta)\in{\mathbb{R}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{R}}\mapsto\langle\sigma(h,x_{2},\check{\pi}^{\delta,\beta})\Phi_{1},\Phi_{2}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{\tilde{\pi}^{\delta}}}
.
We will write
\sigma(h,x_{2},\tilde{\pi}^{\delta},D_{x_{2}})
the operator in
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\tilde{\pi}^{\delta}})\otimes\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{G})
defined by

\forall\Phi_{1},\Phi_{2}\in\mathcal{H}_{\tilde{\pi}^{\delta}},\ \langle\sigma(h,x_{2},\tilde{\pi}^{\delta},D_{x_{2}})\Phi_{1},\Phi_{2}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{\tilde{\pi}^{\delta}}}={\rm Op}_{1}(\sigma_{\Phi_{1},\Phi_{2}})\in\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{G}),

where
{\rm Op}_{1}
denotes here the usual Kohn-Nirenberg quantization on the real line.

Proposition 4.18
.

For
\sigma\in\mathcal{A}_{0}({\mathbb{H}}^{1,1})
,
\sigma(\cdot,x_{2},\cdot,D_{x_{2}})
is a symbol in
\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\mathcal{G}}(G)
.

Proof.

Let
(h,\tilde{\pi}^{\delta})\in{\mathbb{H}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}
. We fix
g_{1},g_{2}
two elements of
\mathcal{G}
as well as
\Phi_{1},\Phi_{2}
, two elements of
\mathcal{H}_{\tilde{\pi}^{\delta}}
.
Since
\sigma
is a smoothing operator on
{\mathbb{H}}^{1,1}
, there exists
\kappa\in C_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{H}}\times{\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{H}}\times{\mathbb{R}}))
such that
\sigma(x,\cdot)=\mathcal{F}_{{\mathbb{H}}^{1,1}}\kappa_{x}(\cdot)
, for
x\in{\mathbb{H}}^{1,1}
.
To show that
\sigma(\cdot,x_{2},\cdot,D_{x_{2}})
defined as above is in
\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\mathcal{G}}(G)
, we need to find the associated convolution kernel
\theta
and prove that it belongs to
C_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{H}},\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{H}},\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{G})))
.

Let consider for that the map defined as follows: for
h_{x},h_{z}\in{\mathbb{H}}
, we write
\mathcal{F}_{z_{2}}\kappa(h_{x},x_{2},h_{z},\beta)
for the Fourier transform of
(h_{x},x_{2},h_{z},z_{2})\mapsto\kappa(h_{x},x_{2},h_{z},z_{2})
in the last variable
z_{2}\in{\mathbb{R}}
, evaluated at
\beta\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
and we write
\theta(h_{x},h_{z})
for the operator in
\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{G})
obtained as the Kohn-Nirenberg quantization of
\mathcal{F}_{z_{2}}\kappa(h_{x},\cdot,h_{z},\cdot)
.

Following Equation (
A.1
), we compute the Fourier transform on
{\mathbb{H}}
of
\langle\theta(h_{x},h_{z})g_{1},g_{2}\rangle_{\mathcal{G}}
:

\displaystyle\left\langle\mathcal{F}_{{\mathbb{H}}}\left(\langle\theta(h_{x},\cdot)g_{1},g_{2}\rangle_{\mathcal{G}}\right)(\tilde{\pi}^{\delta})\,\Phi_{1},\Phi_{2}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{\tilde{\pi}^{\delta}}}

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathbb{H}}}\langle\theta(h_{x},h_{z})g_{1},g_{2}\rangle_{\mathcal{G}}\langle\tilde{\pi}^{\delta}(h_{z})^{*}\Phi_{1},\Phi_{2}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{\tilde{\pi}^{\delta}}}\,dh_{z}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\int_{{\mathbb{H}}}\theta(h_{x},h_{z})\langle\tilde{\pi}^{\delta}(h_{z})^{*}\Phi_{1},\Phi_{2}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{\tilde{\pi}^{\delta}}}\,dh_{z}\,g_{1},g_{2}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{G}}.

With the above notations, we are left to show that we have

{\rm Op}_{1}(\sigma_{\Phi_{1},\Phi_{2}})=\int_{{\mathbb{H}}}\theta(h_{x},h_{z})\langle\tilde{\pi}^{\delta}(h_{z})^{*}\Phi_{1},\Phi_{2}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{\tilde{\pi}^{\delta}}}\,dh_{z}.

Indeed, we can write

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{H}}}\theta(h_{x},h_{z})\langle\tilde{\pi}^{\delta}(h_{z})^{*}\Phi_{1},\Phi_{2}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{\tilde{\pi}^{\delta}}}\,dh_{z}

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathbb{H}}}{\rm Op}_{1}(\mathcal{F}_{z_{2}}\kappa(h_{x},\cdot,h_{z},\cdot))\langle\tilde{\pi}^{\delta}(h_{z})^{*}\Phi_{1},\Phi_{2}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{\tilde{\pi}^{\delta}}}\,dh_{z}

\displaystyle={\rm Op}_{1}\left(\int_{{\mathbb{H}}}\mathcal{F}_{z_{2}}\kappa(h_{x},\cdot,h_{z},\cdot)\langle\tilde{\pi}^{\delta}(h_{z})^{*}\Phi_{1},\Phi_{2}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{\tilde{\pi}^{\delta}}}\,dh_{z}\right),

as the Kohn-Nirenberg quantization
{\rm Op}_{1}:S^{-\infty}({\mathbb{R}})\rightarrow\mathcal{K}(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}))
is a continuous linear map. By the definition of
\kappa
, we obtain that

\int_{{\mathbb{H}}}\mathcal{F}_{z_{2}}\kappa(h_{x},x_{2},h_{z},\beta)\langle\tilde{\pi}^{\delta}(h_{z})^{*}\Phi_{1},\Phi_{2}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{\tilde{\pi}^{\delta}}}\,dh_{z}=\langle\sigma(h_{x},x_{2},\check{\pi}^{\delta,\beta})\Phi_{1},\Phi_{2}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{\tilde{\pi}^{\delta}}}=\sigma_{\Phi_{1},\Phi_{2}}(h_{x},x_{2},\check{\pi}^{\delta,\beta}).

This means that
\theta
is the associated kernel of
\sigma(\cdot,x_{2},\cdot,D_{x_{2}})
, and as we clearly have
\theta\in C_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{H}},\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{H}},\mathcal{B}))
, we obtain that
\sigma(\cdot,x_{2},\cdot,D_{x_{2}})
is in
\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\mathcal{G}}(G)
.
∎

We can now connect the semiclassical calculus with partial dilations on the quasi-Heisenberg group
{\mathbb{H}}^{1,1}
and the semiclassical calculus of Appendix
A

with nilpotent Lie group
G={\mathbb{H}}
and Hilbert space
\mathcal{G}=L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}_{2})
.

Proposition 4.19
.

For
\sigma\in\mathcal{A}_{0}({\mathbb{H}}^{1,1})
, identifying
L^{2}({\mathbb{H}}^{1,1})
with
L^{2}({\mathbb{H}},\mathcal{G})
, we have

(4.11)

{\rm Op}_{\hbar,{\mathbb{H}}_{pt}^{1,1}}(\sigma)={\rm Op}_{\hbar}^{\mathcal{G}}\left(\sigma(\cdot,x_{2},\cdot,D_{x_{2}})\right).

Proof.

Let
f\in\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{H}}^{1,1})
and
(h_{x},x_{2})\in{\mathbb{H}}^{1,1}
. Seeing
f
as an element of
\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{H}},\mathcal{G})
, we have

{\rm Op}_{\hbar}^{\mathcal{G}}\left(\sigma(\cdot,x_{2},\cdot,D_{x_{2}})\right)f(h_{x})(x_{2})=\int_{{\mathbb{H}}}\int_{\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}}{\rm Tr}_{\mathcal{H}_{\tilde{\pi}^{\delta}}}\left(\tilde{\pi}^{\delta}(\hbar^{-1}\cdot(h_{y}^{-1}h_{x}))\sigma(h_{x},x_{2},\tilde{\pi}^{\delta},D_{x_{2}})f(h_{y})(x_{2})\right)\,d\mu_{\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}}(\tilde{\pi}^{\delta})dh_{y}\\
=\int_{{\mathbb{H}}}\int_{\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}}{\rm Tr}_{\mathcal{H}_{\tilde{\pi}^{\delta}}}\left(\tilde{\pi}^{\delta}(\hbar^{-1}\cdot(h_{y}^{-1}h_{x}))\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}e^{i\beta(x_{2}-y_{2})}\sigma(h_{x},x_{2},\check{\pi}^{\delta,\beta})f(h_{y},y_{2})\,\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}d\beta dy_{2}\right)\,d\mu_{\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}}(\tilde{\pi}^{\delta})dh_{y}\\
=\int_{{\mathbb{H}}^{1,1}}\int_{\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}^{1,1}}{\rm Tr}_{\mathcal{H}_{\check{\pi}^{\delta,\beta}}}\left(\check{\pi}^{\delta,\beta}(\hbar^{-1}\cdot_{pt}(h_{y}^{-1}h_{x},x_{2}-y_{2}))\sigma(h_{x},x_{2},\check{\pi}^{\delta,\beta})f(h_{y},y_{2})\right)\,d\mu_{\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}^{1,1}}(\check{\pi}^{\delta,\beta})dh_{y}dy_{2},

which means we have

{\rm Op}_{\hbar}^{\mathcal{G}}\left(\sigma(\cdot,x_{2},\cdot,D_{x_{2}})\right)f(h_{x})(x_{2})={\rm Op}_{\hbar,{\mathbb{H}}^{1,1}}(\sigma)f(h_{x},x_{2}).

∎

4.4.
Second-microlocal semiclassical measures

We associate to a bounded family
(\psi_{0}^{\hbar})_{\hbar>0}
in
L^{2}({\mathbb{E}})
the quantities

\ell_{\hbar}^{2}(\sigma)=\left({\rm Op}_{\hbar}^{C_{\nu_{0}}}(\sigma)\psi_{0}^{\hbar},\psi_{0}^{\hbar}\right)_{L^{2}({\mathbb{E}})},\ \sigma\in\mathcal{A}_{0}^{2}({\mathbb{E}}),

the limits of which are characterized by the following theorem.

Theorem 4.20
.

Let
(\psi_{0}^{\hbar})_{\hbar>0}
be a bounded family in
L^{2}({\mathbb{E}})
, and let
(\hbar_{k})_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}
a sequence going to zero
for which
\Gamma d\gamma
is a semiclassical measure of
(\psi_{0}^{\hbar})_{\hbar>0}
. Then up to taking a subsequence of
(\hbar_{k})_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}
, there exist two
positive operator-valued measures
\Gamma^{\infty}d\gamma^{\infty}\in\mathcal{M}_{ov}^{+}\left(C_{\nu_{0}}\times\mathbb{S}^{0},(\mathcal{H}_{\pi})_{(x,\pi,\omega)\in C_{\nu_{0}}\times\mathbb{S}^{0}}\right)
and

\Gamma^{2}d\gamma^{2}\in\mathcal{M}_{ov}^{+}\left({\mathbb{H}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}},(\mathcal{H}_{\tilde{\pi}}\otimes L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}_{2}))_{(x,\tilde{\pi})\in{\mathbb{H}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}}\right)
,
such that, for every
\sigma\in\mathcal{A}_{0}^{2}({\mathbb{E}})
, we have

\left({\rm Op}_{\hbar_{n}}^{C_{\nu_{0}}}(\sigma)\psi_{0}^{\hbar_{k}},\psi_{0}^{\hbar_{k}}\right)\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits_{k\rightarrow+\infty}\int_{({\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}})\setminus C_{\nu_{0}}}{\rm Tr}_{\mathcal{H}_{\pi^{\delta,\beta}}}\left(\sigma_{\infty}\left(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta},\frac{\beta-\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}}{|\beta-\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}|}\right)\Gamma(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta})\right)\,d\gamma(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta})\\
+\int_{C_{\nu_{0}}\times\mathbb{S}^{0}}{\rm Tr}_{\mathcal{H}_{\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}}}}\left(\sigma_{\infty}(x,\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}},\omega)\Gamma^{\infty}(x,\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}},\omega)\right)\,d\gamma^{\infty}(x,\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}},\omega)\\
+\int_{{\mathbb{H}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}}{\rm Tr}_{\mathcal{H}_{\tilde{\pi}^{\delta}}\otimes L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}_{2})}\left(\sigma(h_{x},x_{2},\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}},D_{x_{2}})\Gamma^{2}(h_{x},\tilde{\pi}^{\delta})\right)d\gamma^{2}(h_{x},\tilde{\pi}^{\delta}),

where for
(h_{x},\tilde{\pi}^{\delta})\in{\mathbb{H}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}
,
\sigma(h_{x},x_{2},\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}},D_{x_{2}})
denotes the operator acting on
\mathcal{H}_{\tilde{\pi}^{\delta}}\otimes L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}_{2})
obtained by quantizing the symbol

(y,\eta)\mapsto\sigma(h_{x},y,\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}},\eta)
.

The two operator-valued measures
(\Gamma^{\infty}d\gamma^{\infty},\Gamma^{2}d\gamma^{2})
are called second-microlocal semiclassical measures above
C_{\nu_{0}}
.
Given the sequence
(\hbar_{k})_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}
, the second-microlocal semiclassical measures are the only operator-valued measures for which the above convergence holds.

Remark 4.21
.

As any symbol
\sigma
in
\mathcal{A}_{0}({\mathbb{E}})
can be seen as a symbol in
\mathcal{A}_{0}^{2}({\mathbb{E}})
independent of the extra variable
\eta
and as, in this case, its two quantizations coincide, i.e

{\rm Op}_{\hbar}(\sigma)={\rm Op}_{\hbar}^{C_{\nu_{0}}}(\sigma)
, we can see Theorem
4.20
as a refinement of Theorem
3.5
where the cone
C_{\nu_{0}}
has been blown-up.
In particular, any semiclassical measure of a family
(\psi_{0}^{\hbar})_{\hbar>0}
in
L^{2}({\mathbb{E}})
can be seen, up to a further extraction of the subsequence defining it, to be above
C_{\nu_{0}}
the marginal of
a second-microlocal semiclassical measure for second-microlocal symbols independent of
\eta
.

4.4.1.
Proof of Theorem
4.20

We now present the proof of the existence of second-microlocalized semiclassical measures.

Lemma 4.22
.

Let
\sigma\in\mathcal{A}_{0}^{2}({\mathbb{E}})
, let
\chi\in C^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}})
a smooth positive compactly supported function and
\epsilon>0
, we consider the symbol defined for

(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta},\eta)\in{\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}\times\bar{{\mathbb{R}}}
by
\tau(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta},\eta)=\sigma(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta},\eta)\left(1-\chi\left(\frac{\beta-\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}}{\epsilon}\right)\right)
:
there exists
\hbar_{0}>0
(depending on
\epsilon
) such that for
\hbar\in(0,\hbar_{0})
we have

{\rm Op}_{\hbar}^{C_{\nu_{0}}}(\tau)={\rm Op}_{\hbar}\left(\tau_{\infty}\left(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta},\frac{\beta-\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}}{|\beta-\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}|}\right)\right).

Proof.

Let
R_{0}>0
as defined in Definition
4.1
associated to
\sigma
. Recall that we have

{\rm Op}_{\hbar}^{C_{\nu_{0}}}(\tau)={\rm Op}_{\hbar}\left(\tau\left(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta},\frac{\beta-\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}}{\hbar}\right)\right),

and on the support of
\tau
, by definition we have
|\beta-\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}|>\epsilon
. Thus, for
\hbar
small enough we have

\frac{|\beta-\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}|}{\hbar}>\epsilon\hbar^{-1}>R_{0},

on the support of
\tau
, and we obtain

{\rm Op}_{\hbar}\left(\tau\left(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta},\frac{\beta-\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}}{\hbar}\right)\right)={\rm Op}_{\hbar}\left(\tau_{\infty}\left(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta},\frac{\beta-\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}}{|\beta-\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}|}\right)\right).

∎

Proof of Theorem
4.20
.

Let
\sigma\in\mathcal{A}_{0}^{2}({\mathbb{E}})
a second-microlocal symbol and let
R_{0}>0
such that

\forall(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta})\in{\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}_{gen},\forall\eta\in{\mathbb{R}},\,|\eta|>R_{0},\ \sigma(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta},\eta)=\sigma_{\infty}\left(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta},\frac{\eta}{|\eta|}\right).

Let
\delta_{0}>0
such that
\sigma(x,\cdot,\eta)
is uniformly supported in
\mathcal{O}_{\delta_{0}}(\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}})
. We fix
R>0
and
\chi\in\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},[0,1])
such that
\chi=1
in a neighborhood of
0
and we set

\displaystyle\sigma^{R}(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta},\eta):=\sigma(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta},\eta)\left(1-\chi\left(\frac{\eta}{R}\right)\right),

\displaystyle\sigma_{R}(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta},\eta):=\sigma(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta},\eta)\chi\left(\frac{\eta}{R}\right).

Of course we have

\sigma=\sigma_{R}+\sigma^{R}.

The idea is to consider the limits
\limsup_{R\rightarrow+\infty}\limsup_{\hbar\rightarrow 0}
of
\ell_{\hbar}^{2}(\sigma_{R})
and
\ell_{\hbar}^{2}(\sigma^{R})
respectively, taken in this order.

First we consider the symbol
\sigma_{R}
. Introducing the bounded family
(\tilde{\psi}_{0}^{\hbar})_{\hbar>0}
defined by
\tilde{\psi}_{0}^{\hbar}=\mathbb{U}_{\nu_{0}}^{\hbar}\psi_{0}^{\hbar}
, by Theorem
4.16
, we can write

\left({\rm Op}_{\hbar}^{C_{\nu_{0}}}(\sigma_{R})\psi_{0}^{\hbar},\psi_{0}^{\hbar}\right)=\left({\rm Op}_{\hbar,{\mathbb{H}}^{1,1}}(\sigma_{R}(x,\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}},\beta))\tilde{\psi}_{0}^{\hbar},\tilde{\psi}_{0}^{\hbar}\right)+\mathcal{O}(\hbar).

Thanks to Proposition
4.19
, viewing the family
(\tilde{\psi}_{0}^{\hbar})_{\hbar>0}
as a bounded family
\tilde{\Psi}_{0}^{\hbar}:h\in{\mathbb{H}}\mapsto\tilde{\psi}_{0}^{\hbar}(h,\cdot)
in
L^{2}({\mathbb{H}},\mathcal{G})
where
\mathcal{G}=L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}_{2})
, we have

\left({\rm Op}_{\hbar}^{C_{\nu_{0}}}(\sigma_{R})\psi_{0}^{\hbar},\psi_{0}^{\hbar}\right)=\left({\rm Op}_{\hbar}^{\mathcal{G}}(\sigma_{R}(h,x_{2},\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}},D_{x_{2}}))\tilde{\Psi}_{0}^{\hbar},\tilde{\Psi}_{0}^{\hbar}\right)_{L^{2}({\mathbb{H}},\mathcal{G})}+\mathcal{O}(\hbar).

By Theorem
A.9
in Appendix
A
, up to a further extraction
(\hbar_{k})_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}
, there exists a semiclassical measure
\Gamma^{2}d\gamma^{2}\in\mathcal{M}_{ov}^{+}\left({\mathbb{H}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}},(\mathcal{H}_{\tilde{\pi}}\otimes\mathcal{G})_{(x,\tilde{\pi})\in{\mathbb{H}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}}\right)

associated to the family
(\tilde{\Psi}_{0}^{\hbar_{k}})_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}
such that

\displaystyle\left({\rm Op}_{\hbar_{k}}^{\mathcal{G}}(\sigma_{R}(h,x_{2},\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}},D_{x_{2}}))\tilde{\Psi}_{0}^{\hbar_{k}},\tilde{\Psi}_{0}^{\hbar_{k}}\right)\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits_{k\rightarrow+\infty}

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{H}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}}{\rm Tr}_{\mathcal{H}_{\tilde{\pi}^{\delta}}\otimes\mathcal{G}}\left(\sigma_{R}(h,x_{2},\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}},D_{x_{2}})\Gamma^{2}(h,\tilde{\pi}^{\delta})\right)\,d\gamma^{2}(h,\tilde{\pi}^{\delta}).

To conclude with this part of the proof, just observe that when
R
goes to infinity, as the operator
\sigma_{R}(h,x_{2},\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}},D_{x_{2}})
converges in operator norm towards

\sigma(h,x_{2},\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}},D_{x_{2}})
, the right-hand side term then converges to

\int_{{\mathbb{H}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}}{\rm Tr}_{\mathcal{H}_{\tilde{\pi}^{\delta}}\otimes\mathcal{G}}\left(\sigma(h,x_{2},\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}},D_{x_{2}})\Gamma^{2}(h,\tilde{\pi}^{\delta})\right)\,d\gamma^{2}(h,\tilde{\pi}^{\delta}).

We consider now the symbol
\sigma^{R}
and we introduce the notation

I_{\hbar,R}(\sigma)=\left({\rm Op}_{\hbar}^{C_{\nu_{0}}}(\sigma^{R})\psi_{0}^{\hbar},\psi_{0}^{\hbar}\right).

We see that choosing
R>R_{0}
, we have
I_{\hbar,R}(\sigma)=I_{\hbar,R}(\sigma_{\infty})
: this way we consider
I_{\hbar,R}
as a linear form on the space

\mathcal{A}_{0}^{2,\infty}({\mathbb{E}})
. But by Proposition
4.5
,

|I_{\hbar,R}(\sigma_{\infty})|\leq N_{2}(\sigma^{R})\|f^{\hbar}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{E}})},

thus
(I_{\hbar,R}(\sigma_{\infty}))_{\hbar>0}
is a bounded sequence, and up to a furhter extraction
(\hbar_{k})_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}
, there exists a limit that we denote by
I_{R}(\sigma_{\infty})
.
Using a countable dense subset of
\mathcal{A}_{0}^{2,\infty}({\mathbb{E}})
and by a diagonal argument, there exists a common extracted subsequence
(\hbar_{k})_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}
for which we have the desired convergence.
From the previous inequality and from the computations done in the proof of Proposition
4.5
, we also obtain

\displaystyle|I_{R}(\sigma_{\infty})|

\displaystyle\leq C\left(1+\frac{1}{R}\right)\sup_{(x,\eta)\in{\mathbb{E}}\times{\mathbb{R}}}\left\|\sigma(x,\cdot,\eta)\left(1-\chi\left(\frac{\eta}{R}\right)\right)\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}})}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(1+\frac{1}{R}\right)\sup_{(x,\omega)\in{\mathbb{E}}\times\mathbb{S}^{0}}\|\sigma_{\infty}(x,\cdot,\omega)\|_{L^{\infty}(\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}})},

thus
I_{R}
is a continuous linear form on the completion of
\mathcal{A}_{0}^{2,\infty}({\mathbb{E}})
, denoted by
\mathcal{A}^{2,\infty}({\mathbb{E}})
, for the norm
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{A}^{2,\infty}}
defined as

\forall\sigma_{\infty}\in\mathcal{A}_{0}^{2,\infty}({\mathbb{E}}),\ \|\sigma_{\infty}\|_{\mathcal{A}^{2,\infty}}=\sup_{(x,\omega)\in{\mathbb{E}}\times\mathbb{S}^{0}}\|\sigma_{\infty}(x,\cdot,\omega)\|_{L^{\infty}(\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}})}.

The linear forms on such a space are known as they are described by operator-valued measures
\Gamma^{R,\infty}d\gamma^{{\mathbb{R}},\infty}\in\mathcal{M}_{ov}\left({\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}\times\mathbb{S}^{0},(\mathcal{H}_{\pi})_{(x,\pi,\omega)\in{\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}\times\mathbb{S}^{0}}\right)
:

\forall\sigma_{\infty}\in\mathcal{A}_{0}^{2,\infty}({\mathbb{E}}),\ I_{R}(\sigma_{\infty})=\int_{{\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}\times\mathbb{S}^{0}}{\rm Tr}_{\mathcal{H}_{\pi}}\left(\sigma_{\infty}(x,\pi,\omega)\Gamma^{R,\infty}(x,\pi,\omega)\right)d\gamma^{R,\infty}(x,\pi,\omega).

We are left with understanding the limit of the sequence
(I_{R})_{R\in{\mathbb{R}}^{+}}
as
R\rightarrow+\infty
. As a bounded sequence of linear forms on
\mathcal{A}^{2,\infty}({\mathbb{E}})
, there exists a sequence
(R_{m})_{m\in{\mathbb{N}}}
and a linear form
I=\Gamma^{\infty}d\gamma^{\infty}\in\mathcal{A}^{2,\infty}({\mathbb{E}})^{*}=\mathcal{M}_{ov}\left({\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}\times\mathbb{S}^{0},(\mathcal{H}_{\pi})_{(x,\pi,\omega)\in{\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}\times\mathbb{S}^{0}}\right)

such that

\forall\sigma_{\infty}\in\mathcal{A}_{0}^{2,\infty}({\mathbb{E}}),\ I_{R_{m}}(\sigma_{\infty})\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits_{m\rightarrow+\infty}I(\sigma_{\infty})=\int_{{\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}\times\mathbb{S}^{0}}{\rm Tr}_{\mathcal{H}_{\pi}}\left(\sigma_{\infty}(x,\pi,\omega)\Gamma^{\infty}(x,\pi,\omega)\right)d\gamma^{\infty}(x,\pi,\omega).

We are left to show that
\Gamma^{\infty}d\gamma^{\infty}
is positive, or equivalently defines a state on
\mathcal{A}^{2,\infty}({\mathbb{E}})
. This property follows from the symbolic calculus: as operator on
L^{2}({\mathbb{E}})
, we have

{\rm Op}_{\hbar}^{C_{\nu_{0}}}(\sigma_{\infty}^{R,*}\sigma_{\infty}^{R})={\rm Op}_{\hbar}^{C_{\nu_{0}}}(\sigma_{\infty}^{R})^{*}\circ{\rm Op}_{\hbar}^{C_{\nu_{0}}}(\sigma_{\infty}^{R})+\mathcal{O}\left(\hbar+\frac{1}{R}\right),

and as
I(\sigma_{\infty})=\lim_{m\rightarrow+\infty}\lim_{k\rightarrow+\infty}I_{\hbar_{k},R_{m}}(\sigma_{\infty})
, we obtain

\forall\sigma_{\infty}\in\mathcal{A}_{0}^{2,\infty}({\mathbb{E}}),\ I(\sigma_{\infty}^{*}\sigma_{\infty})\geq 0.

To conclude our proof, we have to further breakdown the operator-valued measure
\Gamma^{\infty}d\gamma^{\infty}
. We introduce a small parameter
\epsilon>0
and the folllowing decomposition of
\sigma^{R}
:

\displaystyle\sigma^{R}_{\epsilon}(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta},\eta):=\sigma^{R}(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta},\eta)\chi\left(\frac{\beta-\nu\delta^{1/3}}{\epsilon}\right),

\displaystyle\sigma^{R,\epsilon}(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta},\eta):=\sigma^{R}(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta},\eta)\left(1-\chi\left(\frac{\beta-\nu\delta^{1/3}}{\epsilon}\right)\right).

By the dominated convergence theorem, we have

I(\sigma_{\infty,\epsilon})\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\int_{C_{\nu_{0}}\times\mathbb{S}^{0}}{\rm Tr}_{\mathcal{H}_{\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}}}}\left(\sigma_{\infty}(x,\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}},\omega)\Gamma^{\infty}(x,\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}},\omega)\right)\,d\gamma^{\infty}(x,\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}},\omega),

and we obtain the second term in Theorem
4.20
.

For the symbol
\sigma_{\infty}^{\epsilon}
, observe that by Lemma
4.22
, we have for
\hbar
small enough,

\left({\rm Op}_{\hbar}^{C_{\nu_{0}}}(\sigma^{R,\epsilon})\psi_{0}^{\hbar},\psi_{0}^{\hbar}\right)_{L^{2}({\mathbb{E}})}=\left({\rm Op}_{\hbar}\left(\sigma^{\epsilon}_{\infty}\left(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta},\frac{\beta-\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}}{|\beta-\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}|}\right)\right)\psi_{0}^{\hbar},\psi_{0}^{\hbar}\right)_{L^{2}({\mathbb{E}})}.

By definition of the semiclassical measure
\Gamma d\gamma\in\mathcal{M}_{ov}^{+}({\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}})
associated to
(\psi_{0}^{\hbar})_{\hbar>0}
, we can write

\left({\rm Op}_{\hbar_{k}}^{C_{\nu_{0}}}(\sigma^{R,\epsilon})\psi_{0}^{\hbar_{k}},\psi_{0}^{\hbar_{k}}\right)\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits_{\hbar_{k}\rightarrow 0}\int_{{\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}}{\rm Tr}_{\mathcal{H}_{\pi^{\delta,\beta}}}\left(\sigma^{\epsilon}_{\infty}\left(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta},\frac{\beta-\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}}{|\beta-\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}|}\right)\Gamma(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta})\right)\,d\gamma(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta}).

Letting
\epsilon
go to
0
, we have decomposed our measure
\Gamma^{\infty}d\gamma^{\infty}
into two contributions, one supported on the subset
C_{\nu_{0}}\times\mathbb{S}^{0}
and the other as the semiclassical measure
\Gamma d\gamma

restricted to the subset
({\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}})\setminus C_{\nu_{0}}
.
∎

4.4.2.
Example: computation of a second-microlocal semiclassical measure

We propose here an example of computation of second-microlocal semiclassical measure. Let fix
\nu_{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
(h_{0},\delta_{0},\eta_{0})\in{\mathbb{H}}\times{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\{0\}\times{\mathbb{R}}
, then for
\hbar>0
we define the profile

\forall x=(h,x_{2})\in{\mathbb{E}},\ \alpha_{\hbar}(x)=\hbar^{-3/2}a(\hbar^{-1/2}\cdot(h_{0}^{-1}h))\varphi(x_{2}),

where we have fixed
a\in C_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{H}})
,
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{R}}_{2})
. Now, choosing a vector
\Phi
in the Hilbert space associated to the representation
\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}}
, we introduce
the following bounded family in
L^{2}({\mathbb{E}})
:

(4.12)

\forall x\in{\mathbb{E}},\ u^{\hbar}(x)=\alpha_{\hbar}(x)\left(\pi^{\delta_{0},\nu_{0}\delta_{0}^{1/3}+\hbar\eta_{0}}(\hbar^{-1}\cdot(x_{0}^{-1}x))\Phi,\Phi\right),

where
x_{0}=(h_{0},0)\in{\mathbb{E}}
.
This construction is inspired from the wave packets already introduced in Section
1.6
.

Proposition 4.23
.

The family
(u^{\hbar})_{\hbar>0}
is a bounded family of
L^{2}({\mathbb{E}})
and has unique semiclassical measure
\Gamma d\gamma
and second-microlocal semiclassical measures
(\Gamma^{\infty}d\gamma^{\infty},\Gamma^{2}d\gamma^{2})
satisfying:

\bm{1}_{({\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}})\setminus C_{\nu_{0}}}\Gamma d\gamma=0,\quad\Gamma^{\infty}d\gamma^{\infty}=0,

and

d\gamma^{2}=\delta_{\{h=h_{0}\}}\otimes\delta_{\{\pi^{\delta}=\pi^{\delta}_{0}\}},\quad\Gamma^{2}=\lvert\Phi\rangle\langle\Phi\rvert\otimes\lvert\varphi e^{i\eta_{0}x_{2}}\rangle\langle\varphi e^{i\eta_{0}x_{2}}\rvert\in\mathcal{L}^{1}\left(\mathcal{H}_{\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}}}\otimes L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}_{2})\right).

From this, we recover the full semiclassical measure
\Gamma d\gamma
:

d\gamma=\delta_{\{h=h_{0}\}}\otimes|\varphi(x_{2})|^{2}dx_{2}\otimes\delta_{\{\pi^{\delta,\beta}=\pi^{\delta_{0},\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}}\}},\quad\Gamma=\lvert\Phi\rangle\langle\Phi\rvert\in\mathcal{L}^{1}\left(\mathcal{H}_{\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}}}\right).

We compute the action of a pseudodifferential operator on the family
(u^{\hbar})_{\hbar>0}
. Such a comutation can already been found in
[
19
, Lemma 4.3]
.

Lemma 4.24
.

Let
\sigma\in S_{0,0}^{0}({\mathbb{E}})
, we have for all
x\in{\mathbb{E}}

{\rm Op}_{\hbar}(\sigma)u^{\hbar}(x)=\alpha_{\hbar}(x)\left(\pi^{\delta_{0},\nu_{0}\delta_{0}^{1/3}+\hbar\eta_{0}}(\hbar^{-1}\cdot(x_{0}^{-1}x))\sigma(x,\pi^{\delta_{0},\nu_{0}\delta_{0}^{1/3}+\hbar\eta_{0}})\Phi,\Phi\right)+\mathcal{O}_{L^{2}({\mathbb{E}})}(\sqrt{\hbar}).

Proof.

We denote by
\kappa
the convolution kernel associated to
\sigma
. We have

\displaystyle{\rm Op}_{\hbar}(\sigma)u^{\hbar}(x)

\displaystyle=u^{\hbar}*\kappa^{\hbar}_{x}(x)=\int_{{\mathbb{E}}}u^{\hbar}(xy^{-1})\kappa^{\hbar}_{x}(y)\,dy

\displaystyle=\hbar^{-7}\int_{{\mathbb{E}}}u^{\hbar}(xy^{-1})\kappa_{x}(\hbar^{-1}\cdot y)\,dy

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathbb{E}}}\alpha_{\hbar}(x(\hbar\cdot y^{-1}))\left(\pi^{\delta_{0},\nu_{0}\delta_{0}^{1/3}+\hbar\eta_{0}}(\hbar^{-1}\cdot(x_{0}^{-1}x))\pi^{\delta_{0},\nu_{0}\delta_{0}^{1/3}+\hbar\eta_{0}}(y^{-1})\Phi,\Phi\right)\kappa_{x}(y)\,dy

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathbb{E}}}\alpha_{\hbar}(x(\hbar\cdot y^{-1}))\left(\pi^{\delta_{0},\nu_{0}\delta_{0}^{1/3}+\hbar\eta_{0}}(\hbar^{-1}\cdot(x_{0}^{-1}x))\kappa_{x}(y)\pi^{\delta_{0},\nu_{0}\delta_{0}^{1/3}+\hbar\eta_{0}}(y)^{*}\Phi,\Phi\right)\,dy.

We would recognize in the last equation the Fourier transform of
\kappa_{x}
, and thus the symbol
\sigma
, evaluated on the representation
\pi^{\delta_{0},\nu_{0}\delta_{0}^{1/3}+\hbar\eta_{0}}
if the profile
\alpha_{\hbar}
had no dependency on the variable
y
.
To this end, we perform a Taylor expansion, and by the defintion of the profile
\alpha_{\hbar}
we obtain a remainder of order
\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{\hbar})
:

{\rm Op}_{\hbar}(\sigma)u^{\hbar}(x)=\alpha_{\hbar}(x)\left(\pi^{\delta_{0},\nu_{0}\delta_{0}^{1/3}+\hbar\eta_{0}}(\hbar^{-1}\cdot(x_{0}^{-1}x))\int_{{\mathbb{E}}}\kappa_{x}(y)\pi^{\delta_{0},\nu_{0}\delta_{0}^{1/3}+\hbar\eta_{0}}(y)^{*}\,dy\,\Phi,\Phi\right)+\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{\hbar}),

and we conclude.
∎

Remark 4.25
.

As for the wave packets of Section
1.6
, the previous proof can give rise to a full asymptotic expansion of the action of pseudodifferential operators on our bounded family. Moreover, such a result stays true
on other nilpotent Lie groups, with a similar proof.

Proof of Proposition
4.23
.

First we prove that the semiclassical measures of
(u^{\hbar})_{\hbar>0}
are necessarily concentrated on
C_{\nu_{0}}
. For
\sigma\in\mathcal{A}_{0}({\mathbb{E}})
,
consider, for
\epsilon>0
, we define the symbol
\sigma^{\epsilon}
as follows:

\forall(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta})\in{\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}},\ \sigma^{\epsilon}(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta})=\sigma(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta})\left(1-\chi\left(\frac{\beta-\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}}{\epsilon}\right)\right).

Then by Lemma
4.24
, we have for
x\in{\mathbb{E}}

{\rm Op}_{\hbar}(\sigma^{\epsilon})u^{\hbar}(x)=\alpha_{\hbar}(x)\left(\pi^{\delta_{0},\nu_{0}\delta_{0}^{1/3}+\hbar\eta_{0}}(\hbar^{-1}\cdot(x_{0}^{-1}x))\sigma^{\epsilon}(x,\pi^{\delta_{0},\nu_{0}\delta_{0}^{1/3}+\hbar\eta_{0}})\Phi,\Phi\right)+\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{\hbar}),

and easily observe that

\sigma^{\epsilon}(x,\pi^{\delta_{0},\nu_{0}\delta_{0}^{1/3}+\hbar\eta_{0}})=\mathcal{O}(\hbar),

for
\hbar
small engouh as
\sigma^{\epsilon}
vanishes on a neighborhood of
C_{\nu_{0}}
.
Thus, any semiclassical measure
\Gamma d\gamma
satisfies:

\int_{{\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}}{\rm Tr}\left(\sigma^{\epsilon}(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta})\Gamma(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta})\right)d\gamma(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta})=0,

and by dominating convergence, letting
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
, we get
\bm{1}_{({\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}})\setminus C_{\nu_{0}}}\Gamma d\gamma=0
.

For the part at infinity
\Gamma^{\infty}d\gamma^{\infty}
of any second-microlocal semiclassical measures, recall that for
\sigma\in\mathcal{A}_{0}^{2}({\mathbb{E}})
, we have

\int_{C_{\nu_{0}}\times\mathbb{S}^{0}}{\rm Tr}\left(\sigma(x,\pi,\omega)\Gamma(x,\pi,\omega)\right)d\gamma(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta},\omega)=\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\lim_{R\rightarrow+\infty}\lim_{\hbar\rightarrow 0}\left({\rm Op}_{\hbar}^{C_{\nu_{0}}}(\sigma_{\epsilon}^{R})u^{\hbar},u^{\hbar}\right),

where for all
(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta},\eta)\in{\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}\times{\mathbb{R}}
,

\sigma_{\epsilon}^{R}(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta},\eta)=\sigma(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta},\eta)\left(1-\chi\left(\frac{\eta}{R}\right)\right)\chi\left(\frac{\beta-\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}}{\epsilon}\right).

Using Lemma
4.24
with symbol
\left(\sigma_{\epsilon}^{R}\right)_{\hbar}^{C_{\nu_{0}}}
, we need to understand the action on the vector
\Phi
of the following operator

\left(\sigma_{\epsilon}^{R}\right)_{\hbar}^{C_{\nu_{0}}}(x,\pi^{\delta_{0},\nu_{0}\delta_{0}^{1/3}+\hbar\eta_{0}})=\sigma(x,\pi^{\delta_{0},\nu_{0}\delta_{0}^{1/3}+\hbar\eta_{0}},\eta_{0})\left(1-\chi\left(\frac{\eta_{0}}{R}\right)\right)\chi\left(\frac{\hbar\eta_{0}}{\epsilon}\right).

This way, when
|R|>|\eta_{0}|
, we get that

{\rm Op}_{\hbar}^{C_{\nu_{0}}}(\sigma_{\epsilon}^{R})u^{\hbar}=\mathcal{O}_{L^{2}({\mathbb{E}})}(\sqrt{\hbar}),

giving at the limit
\Gamma^{\infty}d\gamma^{\infty}=0
.

Finally for the remaining part
\Gamma^{2}d\gamma^{2}
, recall that this part of this measure can be computed as the operator-valued semiclassical measure of
\tilde{u}_{\hbar}=\mathbb{U}_{\nu_{0}}^{\hbar}u^{\hbar}
in
L^{2}({\mathbb{H}}^{1,1})
seen as
L^{2}({\mathbb{H}},L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}_{2}))
.
We claim that we have, for
(h,x_{2})\in{\mathbb{H}}^{1,1}
,

(4.13)

\tilde{u}^{\hbar}(h,x_{2})=\hbar^{-3/2}a(\hbar^{-1/2}\cdot(h_{0}^{-1}h))\varphi(x_{2})e^{i\eta_{0}x_{2}}\left(\tilde{\pi}^{\delta_{0}}(\hbar^{-1}\cdot(h_{0}^{-1}h))\Phi,\Phi\right)+\mathcal{O}_{L^{2}({\mathbb{H}}^{1,1})}(\sqrt{\hbar}).

We denote the right-hand side term (without the remainder
\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{\hbar})
) by
\check{u}^{\hbar}
. If Equation (
4.13
) holds true, then the computation of any operator-valued measure for
(\check{u}^{\hbar})_{\hbar>0}
is straightforward as we identify a wave packet on the Heisenberg variables,
while on the real variable
x_{2}
, we identify the profile
\varphi e^{i\eta_{0}x_{2}}
. Indeed, the semiclassical measures of wave packets on
{\mathbb{H}}
being simply the Dirac measures on the point of concentration
(h_{0},\tilde{\pi}^{\delta_{0}})
(for proofs see for example
[
16
]
), we obtain the announced result.

We are left with proving (
4.13
): observe that for
(h_{x},x_{2})\in{\mathbb{E}}
, we have

\mathbb{U}_{\nu_{0}}^{\hbar}\check{u}^{\hbar}(h_{x},x_{2})=\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\hbar^{-6}\int_{{\mathbb{H}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}}{\rm Tr}\left(\tilde{\pi}^{\delta}(\hbar^{-1}\cdot(h_{y}^{-1}h_{x}))U_{\nu_{0}}^{\hbar}(x_{2},\tilde{\pi}^{\delta})\mathcal{F}_{{\mathbb{H}}}^{\hbar}\theta_{\epsilon}(\tilde{\pi}^{\delta})\right)\check{u}^{\hbar}(h_{y},x_{2})\,d\mu_{\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}}(\tilde{\pi}^{\delta})dh_{y},

where
\theta_{\epsilon}
is an approximation of the identity in
\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{H}})
. From the structure of
\check{u}^{\hbar}
, the previous operator only acts on the Heisenberg variables: focusing on these variables, we have

\hbar^{-6}\int_{{\mathbb{H}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}}{\rm Tr}\left(\tilde{\pi}^{\delta}(\hbar^{-1}\cdot(h_{y}^{-1}h_{x}))U_{\nu_{0}}^{\hbar}(x_{2},\tilde{\pi}^{\delta})\mathcal{F}_{{\mathbb{H}}}^{\hbar}\theta_{\epsilon}(\tilde{\pi}^{\delta})\right)WP_{(h_{0},\tilde{\pi}^{\delta_{0}})}^{\hbar}(a,\Phi,\Phi)(h_{y})\,d\mu_{\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}}(\tilde{\pi}^{\delta})dh_{y}\\
={\rm Op}_{\hbar,{\mathbb{H}}}\left(U_{\nu_{0}}^{\hbar}(x_{2},\cdot)\mathcal{F}_{{\mathbb{H}}}^{\hbar}\theta_{\epsilon}(\cdot)\right)WP_{(h_{0},\tilde{\pi}^{\delta_{0}})}^{\hbar}(a,\Phi,\Phi)(h_{x}),

where we write
WP_{(h_{0},\tilde{\pi}^{\delta_{0}})}^{\hbar}(a,\Phi,\Phi)(h_{x})=\hbar^{-3/2}a(\hbar^{-1/2}\cdot(h_{0}^{-1}h_{x}))\left(\tilde{\pi}^{\delta_{0}}(\hbar^{-1}\cdot(h_{0}^{-1}h_{x}))\Phi,\Phi\right)
. By an entirely similar proof as in Lemma
4.24
, we have

{\rm Op}_{\hbar,{\mathbb{H}}}\left(U_{\nu_{0}}^{\hbar}(x_{2},\cdot)\mathcal{F}_{{\mathbb{H}}}^{\hbar}\theta_{\epsilon}(\cdot)\right)WP_{(h_{0},\tilde{\pi}^{\delta_{0}})}^{\hbar}(a,\Phi,\Phi)(h_{x})\\
=\hbar^{-3/2}a(\hbar^{-1/2}\cdot(h_{0}^{-1}h_{x}))\left(\tilde{\pi}^{\delta_{0}}(\hbar^{-1}\cdot(h_{0}^{-1}h_{x}))U_{\nu_{0}}^{\hbar}(x_{2},\tilde{\pi}^{\delta_{0}})\mathcal{F}_{{\mathbb{H}}}^{\hbar}\theta_{\epsilon}(\tilde{\pi}^{\delta_{0}})\Phi,\Phi\right)+\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{\hbar}),

and in the right-hand side we recognize
\pi^{\delta_{0},\nu_{0}\delta_{0}^{1/3}}(\hbar^{-1}\cdot(x_{0}^{-1}x))\mathcal{F}_{{\mathbb{H}}}^{\hbar}\theta_{\epsilon}(\tilde{\pi}^{\delta_{0}})
acting on the vector
\Phi
. Remark that the remainder does not depend on
\epsilon
and thus we can take the limit
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
and our claim is proved.
∎

5.
Schrödinger equation and second-microlocal semiclassical measures

This section is devoted to the semiclassical analysis of the following Schrödinger equation

\begin{cases}&i\partial_{t}\psi^{\hbar}=-\Delta_{{\mathbb{E}}}\psi^{\hbar},\\
&\psi_{|t=0}^{\hbar}=\psi_{0}^{\hbar}\in L^{2}({\mathbb{E}}),\end{cases}

where
(\psi_{0}^{\hbar})_{\hbar>0}
is a bounded family in
L^{2}({\mathbb{E}})
. More precisely we wish to apply the second-microlocal analysis developed in the previous section to analyze in further details than in
Proposition
1.12
the support of the time-averaged semiclassical measures associated to bounded families of solutions to the previous Schrödinger equation.

5.1.
Time-averaged second-microlocal semiclassical measures and their propagation

For a bounded family
(\psi^{\hbar}(t))_{\hbar>0}
in
L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}_{t},L^{2}({\mathbb{E}}))
, we associate the quantities

(5.1)

\ell_{\hbar}^{2}(\theta,\sigma)=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}\theta(t)\left({\rm Op}_{\hbar}^{C_{\nu_{0}}}(\sigma)\psi^{\hbar}(t),\psi^{\hbar}(t)\right)_{L^{2}({\mathbb{E}})}dt,\;\;\sigma\in\mathcal{A}_{0}^{2}({\mathbb{E}}),\;\;\theta\in L^{1}({\mathbb{R}}_{t}),

the limits of which are characterized in the following proposition, which generalizes Theorem
3.9
.

Proposition 5.1
.

Let
(\psi^{\hbar}(t))_{\hbar>0}
be a bounded family in
L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}_{t},L^{2}({\mathbb{E}}))
and a sequence
(\hbar_{k})_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}
in
(0,+\infty)
with
\hbar_{k}\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits_{k\rightarrow+\infty}0

for which we have a time-averaged semiclassical measure
t\mapsto\Gamma_{t}d\gamma_{t}
in

L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}_{t},{\mathcal{M}}_{ov}^{+}({\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}))
. Then up to an extraction of
(\hbar_{k})_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}
, there exist two maps, called the time-averaged second-microlocal semiclassical measures,

\displaystyle t\mapsto\Gamma_{t}^{\infty}d\gamma_{t}^{\infty}\in L^{\infty}\left({\mathbb{R}}_{t},\mathcal{M}_{ov}^{+}\left(C_{\nu_{0}}\times\mathbb{S}^{0},(\mathcal{H}_{\pi})_{(x,\pi,\omega)\in C_{\nu_{0}}\times\mathbb{S}^{0}}\right)\right),

\displaystyle t\mapsto\Gamma_{t}^{2}d\gamma_{t}^{2}\in L^{\infty}\left({\mathbb{R}}_{t},\mathcal{M}_{ov}^{+}\left({\mathbb{H}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}},(\mathcal{H}_{\tilde{\pi}}\otimes L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}_{2}))_{(x,\tilde{\pi})\in{\mathbb{H}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}}\right)\right),

such that for all
\theta\in L^{1}({\mathbb{R}}_{t})
and
\sigma\in\mathcal{A}_{0}^{2}({\mathbb{E}})
, we have

\displaystyle\ell_{\hbar}^{2}(\theta,\sigma)

\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits_{k\rightarrow+\infty}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}}\theta(t){\rm Tr}\left(\sigma(x,\pi)\Gamma_{t}(x,\pi)\right)d\gamma_{t}(x,\pi)dt

\displaystyle+\int_{{\mathbb{R}}\times C_{\nu_{0}}\times\mathbb{S}^{0}}\theta(t){\rm Tr}_{\mathcal{H}_{\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}}}}\left(\sigma_{\infty}(x,\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}},\omega)\Gamma_{t}^{\infty}(x,\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}},\omega)\right)\,d\gamma_{t}^{\infty}(x,\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}},\omega)dt

\displaystyle+\int_{{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{H}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}}\theta(t){\rm Tr}_{\mathcal{H}_{\tilde{\pi}^{\delta}}\otimes L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}_{2})}\left(\sigma(h_{x},x_{2},\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}},D_{x_{2}})\Gamma_{t}^{2}(h_{x},\tilde{\pi}^{\delta})\right)d\gamma_{t}^{2}(h_{x},\tilde{\pi}^{\delta})dt.

Given the sequence
(\hbar_{k})_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}
, these measures are the only operator-valued measures for which the above convergence holds.

This proposition can be proved with the same arguments as in the proof of Theorem
4.20
and in
[
17
]
[Section 3.2]. The following results generalize
Theorem
1.14
presented in Section
1.7
and show that our second-microlocal analysis allow to uncover the propagation laws underlying the Schrödinger equation.

Theorem 5.2
.

Let
(\psi_{0}^{\hbar})_{\hbar>0}
be a bounded family in
L^{2}({\mathbb{E}})

and
\psi^{\hbar}(t)={\rm e}^{it\Delta_{{\mathbb{E}}}}\psi^{\hbar}_{0}
be the solution to (
1.2
) for
\tau=2
. Let
\nu_{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
then any time-averaged second-microlocal semiclassical measures
t\mapsto(\Gamma_{t}^{\infty}d\gamma_{t}^{\infty},\Gamma_{t}^{2}d\gamma_{t}^{2})

as in Proposition
5.1
for the family
(\psi^{\hbar}(t))_{\hbar>0}
,
satisfies the following additional properties:

(i)

For
d\gamma_{t}^{2}dt
-almost all
(t,h,\tilde{\pi}^{\delta})\in{\mathbb{R}}_{t}\times{\mathbb{H}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}
, the operator
\Gamma_{t}^{2}(h,\tilde{\pi}^{\delta})
commutes with
H(\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}})

and admits the following decomposition

(5.2)

\Gamma_{t}^{2}(h,\tilde{\pi}^{\delta})=\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\Gamma_{n,t}^{2}(h,\tilde{\pi}^{\delta}),

where

\Gamma_{n,t}(h,\tilde{\pi}^{\delta}):=\left(\Pi_{n}(\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}})\otimes{\rm Id}_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}_{2})}\right)\Gamma_{t}^{2}(h,\tilde{\pi}^{\delta})\left(\Pi_{n}(\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}})\otimes{\rm Id}_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}_{2})}\right)\in\mathcal{L}^{1}(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}_{2})).

(ii)

Similarly, for
d\gamma_{t}^{\infty}dt
-almost all
(t,(x,\pi),\omega)\in{\mathbb{R}}_{t}\times C_{\nu_{0}}\times\mathbb{S}^{0}
,
\Gamma_{t}^{\infty}(x,\pi,\omega)
commutes with
H(\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}})
and decomposes as follow

(5.3)

\Gamma_{t}^{\infty}(x,\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}},\omega)=\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\Gamma_{n,t}^{\infty}(x,\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}},\omega),

with

\Gamma_{n,t}^{\infty}(x,\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}},\omega):=\Pi_{n}(\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}})\Gamma_{t}^{\infty}(x,\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}},\omega)\Pi_{n}(\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}}).

Let
m\in{\mathbb{N}}_{>0}
and suppose that
\nu_{0}
is a non-degenerate critical point of the
m
-th curve of dispersion
\nu\mapsto\tilde{\mu}(\nu)
of the Montgomery operators
(\tilde{H}(\nu))_{\nu\in{\mathbb{R}}}
.

(iii)

For almost all time
t\in{\mathbb{R}}_{t}
, the measure
d\gamma_{m,t}^{\infty}=\Gamma_{m,t}^{\infty}d\gamma_{t}^{\infty}
is zero:

d\gamma_{m,t}^{\infty}=0.

(iv)

The
m
-th mode
d\gamma_{m,t}^{2}=\Gamma_{m,t}^{2}d\gamma_{t}^{2}
satisfies the following Heisenberg equation in the weak sense:

(5.4)

i\partial_{t}\gamma_{m,t}^{2}=\left[-\frac{\tilde{\mu}_{m}^{\prime\prime}(\nu_{0})}{2}\partial_{x_{2}}^{2},\gamma_{m,t}^{2}\right],

where the operator
\partial_{x_{2}}
is seen as an operator on
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}_{2})
.

Remark 5.3
.

The equation satisfied by
d\gamma_{m,t}^{2}
must be understood in the following sense: for
\sigma\in\mathcal{A}_{0}^{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}_{2})}({\mathbb{H}})
, we have

i\frac{d}{dt}\int_{{\mathbb{H}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}}{\rm Tr}_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}_{2})}\left(\sigma(x,\tilde{\pi}^{\delta})\Gamma_{m,t}^{2}(x,\tilde{\pi}^{\delta})\right)d\gamma_{t}^{2}(x,\tilde{\pi}^{\delta})=\\
\int_{{\mathbb{H}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}}{\rm Tr}_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}_{2})}\left(\left[\sigma(x,\tilde{\pi}^{\delta}),\frac{\tilde{\mu}_{m}^{\prime\prime}(\nu_{0})}{2}\partial_{x_{2}}^{2}\right]\Gamma_{m,t}^{2}(x,\tilde{\pi}^{\delta})\right)d\gamma_{t}^{2}(x,\tilde{\pi}^{\delta}).

In order to be able to integrate these propagations laws from initial conditions corresponding to second-microlocal semiclassical measures of the family of initial datum
(\psi_{0}^{\hbar})_{\hbar>0}
, we need
a result concerning the regularity of these measures with respect to time.

Proposition 5.4
.

Consider, as in Theorem
5.2
, the
m
-th mode
t\mapsto d\gamma_{m,t}^{2}=\Gamma_{m,t}^{2}d\gamma_{t}^{2}
of a time-averaged second-microlocal
semiclassical measures corresponding to the family of solutions to the Schrödinger equation (
1.2
) for
\tau=2
. Then for any symbol
\sigma\in\mathcal{A}_{0}^{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}_{2})}({\mathbb{H}})
commuting
with the field of projectors
\tilde{\pi}^{\delta}\in\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}\mapsto\Pi_{m}(\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}})\otimes{\rm Id}_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}_{2})}
, the map

t\in{\mathbb{R}}\mapsto\int_{{\mathbb{H}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}}{\rm Tr}\left(\sigma(x,\tilde{\pi})\Gamma_{m,t}^{2}(x,\tilde{\pi})\right)d\gamma_{t}^{2}(x,\tilde{\pi}),

is locally Lipshitz on
{\mathbb{R}}
.

Remark 5.5
.

The proof of Proposition
5.4
implies that, under the assumptions of the proposition, if
(\hbar_{k})_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}
is the subsequence realizing
the time-averaged semiclassical measure
t\mapsto\Gamma_{t}^{2}d\gamma_{t}^{2}
, then we have for all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
that
d\gamma_{m,t}^{2}
is the
m
-th mode of the second-microlocal measure
of the family
(\psi^{\hbar_{k}}(t))_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}
, meaning we can pass to the limit
t
-by-
t
and not only when averaged in time.

5.2.
Proof of Theorem
5.2
and Proposition
5.4

Let
(\psi_{0}^{\hbar})_{\hbar>0}
be a bounded family in
L^{2}({\mathbb{E}})
and
\psi^{\hbar}(t)={\rm e}^{it\Delta_{{\mathbb{E}}}}\psi^{\hbar}_{0}
be the solution to (
1.2
) for
\tau=2
. Let
\nu_{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
and consider a time-averaged second-microlocal semiclassical measures
t\mapsto(\Gamma_{t}^{\infty}d\gamma_{t}^{\infty},\Gamma_{t}^{2}d\gamma_{t}^{2})
for the family
(\psi^{\hbar}(t))_{\hbar>0}
for the sequence
(\hbar_{k})_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}
.

To begin with, it follows from the commutator formula (
3.3
) that we have for all
\theta\in C_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}_{t})
and
\sigma\in\mathcal{A}_{0}^{2}({\mathbb{E}})
:

(5.5)

-i\hbar^{2}\ell_{\hbar}^{2}(\theta^{\prime},\sigma)=\ell_{\hbar}^{2}(\theta,\left[\sigma,H\right])+2\hbar\ell_{\hbar}^{2}(\theta,(\pi(V)\cdot V)\sigma)+\hbar^{2}\ell_{\hbar}^{2}(\theta,\Delta_{{\mathbb{E}}}\sigma).

5.2.1.
Propagation of
\Gamma_{t}^{2}d\gamma_{t}^{2}

We focus on the case where our symbol
\sigma\in\mathcal{A}_{0}^{2}({\mathbb{E}})
has compact support on its last variable
\eta\in{\mathbb{R}}
.
As we have seen in the proof of Theorem
4.20
, such a second-microlocal symbol only see in the limit the operator-valued measures
\Gamma_{t}^{2}d\gamma_{t}^{2}
, in the sense that:

\ell_{\hbar_{k}}^{2}(\theta,\sigma)\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits_{k\rightarrow+\infty}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{H}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}}\theta(t){\rm Tr}_{\mathcal{H}_{\tilde{\pi}^{\delta}}\otimes L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}_{2})}\left(\sigma(h,x_{2},\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}},D_{x_{2}})\Gamma^{2}_{t}(h,\tilde{\pi}^{\delta})\right)\,d\gamma^{2}_{t}(h,\tilde{\pi}^{\delta})dt.

We denote by
\ell_{\infty}^{2,c}(\theta,\sigma)
the right-hand side term. Using Equation (
5.5
), we readily obtain that

\forall\sigma\in\mathcal{A}_{0}^{2}({\mathbb{E}}),\ \ell_{\hbar}^{2}\left(\theta,\left[\sigma(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta},\eta),H(\pi^{\delta,\beta})\right]\right)=\mathcal{O}(\hbar),

thus we obtain that for a.e
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
and for all
\sigma\in\mathcal{A}_{0}^{2}({\mathbb{E}})
,

\int_{{\mathbb{H}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}}{\rm Tr}_{\mathcal{H}_{\tilde{\pi}^{\delta}}\otimes L^{2}({\mathbb{R}})}\left(\left[\sigma(h,x_{2},\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}},D_{x_{2}}),H(\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}})\right]\Gamma^{2}_{t}(h,\tilde{\pi}^{\delta})\right)\,d\gamma^{2}_{t}(h,\tilde{\pi}^{\delta})=0.

As in Section
3.4.1
, we deduce from the previous equation that the operator
\Gamma_{t}^{2}(h,\tilde{\pi}^{\delta})
is
d\gamma_{t}^{2}
-a.e diagonal with respect to the operator
H(\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}})\otimes{\rm Id}_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}_{2})}
. Being compact,
we can decompose
\Gamma_{t}^{2}
as the following sum

\Gamma_{t}^{2}=\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}^{*}}\Gamma_{n,t}^{2},

where

\forall(h,\tilde{\pi}^{\delta})\in{\mathbb{H}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}},\ \Gamma_{n,t}^{2}(h,\tilde{\pi}^{\delta})=(\Pi_{n}(\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}})\otimes{\rm Id})\Gamma_{t}^{2}(h,\tilde{\pi}^{\delta})(\Pi_{n}(\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}})\otimes{\rm Id}).

We focus now on the
m
-th mode
\Gamma_{m,t}^{2}d\gamma^{2}_{t}
, where we assume that
\nu_{0}
is a non-degenerate critical point of
\nu\mapsto\tilde{\mu}_{m}(\nu)
.

We take
\sigma
a second-microlocal symbol commuting with the symbol of the suplaplacian
H
, and in particular satisfying
\Pi_{m}\sigma\Pi_{m}=\sigma
.
Equation (
5.5
) simply becomes

-i\hbar\,\ell_{\hbar}^{2}(\theta^{\prime},\sigma)=2\ell_{\hbar}^{2}(\theta,(\pi(V)\cdot V)\sigma)+\hbar\ell_{\hbar}^{2}(\theta,\Delta_{{\mathbb{E}}}\sigma).

As previously, we write the decomposition of
(\pi(V)\cdot V)\sigma
as its
H
-diagonal part and its
H
-off-diagonal one: by Lemma
3.17
we have

(\pi(V)\cdot V)\sigma(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta},\eta)=\frac{i}{2}\partial_{\beta}\mu_{m}(\delta,\beta)X_{2}\sigma(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta},\eta)+\frac{1}{2}\left[\sigma_{1}(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta},\eta),H(\pi^{\delta,\beta})\right],

with

\sigma_{1}(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta},\eta)=-\frac{i}{\delta}\pi^{\delta,\beta}(X_{3})X_{1}\sigma(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta},\eta)+i\partial_{\beta}\Pi_{m}(\pi^{\delta,\beta})X_{2}\sigma(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta},\eta).

We perform now the following Taylor expansion:

\partial_{\beta}\mu_{m}(\delta,\beta)=\delta^{1/3}\tilde{\mu}_{m}^{\prime}(\beta\delta^{-1/3})=\delta^{1/3}\tilde{\mu}_{m}^{\prime}(\nu_{0})+(\beta-\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3})\tilde{\mu}_{m}^{\prime\prime}(\nu_{0})+(\beta-\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3})^{2}\delta^{-2/3}r(\delta,\beta),

where we have introduced the map
r
as remainder. As
\nu_{0}
is a critical point for
\tilde{\mu}_{m}
, we have

\partial_{\beta}\mu_{m}(\delta,\beta)X_{2}\sigma_{\hbar}^{C_{\nu_{0}}}(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta})=\hbar\,\tilde{\mu}_{m}^{\prime\prime}(\nu_{0})\frac{\beta-\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}}{\hbar}X_{2}\sigma_{\hbar}^{C_{\nu_{0}}}(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta})\\
+\hbar^{2}\delta^{-2/3}r(\delta,\beta)\left(\frac{\beta-\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}}{\hbar}\right)^{2}X_{2}\sigma_{\hbar}^{C_{\nu_{0}}}(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta})\\
=\hbar\,\tilde{\mu}_{m}^{\prime\prime}(\nu_{0})(\eta X_{2}\sigma)_{\hbar}^{C_{\nu_{0}}}(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta})+\hbar^{2}\delta^{-2/3}r(\delta,\beta)X_{2}(\eta^{2}\sigma)_{\hbar}^{C_{\nu_{0}}}(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta}).

Thus we obtain

-i\hbar\,\ell_{\hbar}^{2}(\theta^{\prime},\sigma)=i\hbar\,\ell_{\hbar}^{2}(\theta,\tilde{\mu}_{m}^{\prime\prime}(\nu_{0})\eta X_{2}\sigma)+\ell_{\hbar}^{2}(\theta,\left[\sigma_{1},H\right])+\hbar\,\ell_{\hbar}^{2}(\theta,\Delta_{{\mathbb{E}}}\sigma)+\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{2}).

Using once again Equation (
5.5
) with the symbol
\sigma_{1}
, we finally obtain

(5.6)

-i\ell_{\hbar}^{2}(\theta^{\prime},\sigma)=i\ell_{\hbar}^{2}(\theta,\tilde{\mu}_{m}^{\prime\prime}(\nu_{0})\eta X_{2}\sigma)-\ell_{\hbar}^{2}(\theta,2(\pi(V)\cdot V)\sigma_{1}-\Delta_{{\mathbb{E}}}\sigma)+\mathcal{O}(\hbar).

We are left with computing the
H
-diagonal part of the symbol
2(\pi(V)\cdot V)\sigma_{1}-\Delta_{{\mathbb{E}}}\sigma
.

Lemma 5.6
.

There exists
\sigma_{2}\in\mathcal{A}_{0}^{2}({\mathbb{E}})
such that

2(\pi(V)\cdot V)\sigma_{1}-\Delta_{{\mathbb{E}}}\sigma=\frac{1}{\delta}\partial_{\beta}\mu_{m}\Pi_{m}\pi(X_{3})\Pi_{m}X_{1}X_{2}\sigma-\frac{\partial_{\beta}^{2}\mu_{m}}{2}X_{2}^{2}\sigma+\frac{1}{2}[\sigma_{2},H].

Proof.

The diagonal part follows easily from Lemmata
3.20
and
3.21
, where one must pay attention to the definition of
\sigma_{1}
as there is a sign difference.
The existence of the symbol
\sigma_{2}
is proved using the same arguments as in Lemma
3.17
or as in Section
3.5.3
in the proof of Theorem
1.10
.
∎

Hence, as
\partial_{\beta}\mu_{m}(\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3})=0
and
\partial_{\beta}^{2}\mu_{m}(\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3})=\tilde{\mu}_{m}^{\prime\prime}(\nu_{0})
for all
