\delta\in\mathfrak{g}_{3}^{*}\setminus\{0\}
, taking the limit
\hbar_{k}\rightarrow 0
we obtain

-i\ell_{\infty}^{2,c}(\theta^{\prime},\sigma(h,x_{2},\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}},D_{x_{2}}))=\\
\ell_{\infty}^{2,c}\left(\theta,\frac{\mu^{\prime\prime}(\nu_{0})}{2}\left(2(i\eta X_{2}\sigma)(h,x_{2},\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}},D_{x_{2}})+(X_{2}^{2}\sigma)(h,x_{2},\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}},D_{x_{2}})\right)\right).

By simple Kohn-Nirenberg symbolic calculus on
{\mathbb{R}}
, as the vector field
X_{2}
simply acts as
\partial_{2}
, this equation is equivalent to

-i\ell_{\infty}^{2,c}(\theta^{\prime},\sigma(h,x_{2},\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}},D_{x_{2}}))=\ell_{\infty}^{2,c}\left(\theta,\left[\sigma(h,x_{2},\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}},D_{x_{2}}),\frac{\mu^{\prime\prime}(\nu_{0})}{2}D_{x_{2}}^{2}\right]\right),

from which we deduce the weak Heisenberg equation satisfied by
t\mapsto d\gamma_{m,t}^{2}
.

5.2.2.
Proof of Proposition
5.4

For any
\sigma\in\mathcal{A}_{0}^{2}({\mathbb{E}})
with compact support in
\eta
and satisfying
\Pi_{m}\sigma\Pi_{m}=\sigma
, we write

\ell_{\hbar,t}^{2,c}(\sigma)=\left({\rm Op}_{\hbar}^{C_{\nu_{0}}}(\sigma)\psi^{\hbar}(t),\psi^{\hbar}(t)\right)_{L^{2}({\mathbb{E}})}.

Observe that by Equation (
5.6
), we have

\frac{d}{dt}\ell_{\hbar,t}^{2,c}(\sigma)=\mathcal{O}_{\sigma}(1),

in the sense that the map
t\mapsto\frac{d}{dt}\ell_{\hbar,t}(\sigma)
is uniformly bounded in a given bounded interval of
{\mathbb{R}}
and this uniformly for
\hbar\in(0,1)
.
Thus the family of maps
(\ell_{\hbar,\cdot}^{2,c}(\sigma))_{\hbar>0}
is bounded and equicontinuous in
C_{b}({\mathbb{R}})
, so that by Arzelà-Ascoli theorem we can extract
a subsequence
(\hbar_{j_{k}})_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}
such that, as
k\rightarrow+\infty
,
\hbar_{j_{k}}\rightarrow 0
and
(\ell_{\hbar_{j_{k}},\cdot}^{2,c}(\sigma))_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}
converges uniformly
on any bounded interval to a locally Lipshitz function
t\mapsto\ell_{t}^{2,c}(\sigma)
for all
\sigma\in\mathcal{A}_{0}^{2}({\mathbb{E}})
with compact support in
\eta
and satisfying
\Pi_{m}\sigma\Pi_{m}=\sigma

(this last assertion follows from considering a dense subset of such symbols and a diagonal extraction procedure).

To conclude to the equality

\forall t\in{\mathbb{R}},\ \ell_{t}^{2,c}(\sigma)=\int_{{\mathbb{H}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}}{\rm Tr}\left(\sigma(h,x_{2},\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}},D_{x_{2}})\Gamma_{t}^{2}(x,\tilde{\pi}^{\delta})\right)d\gamma_{t}^{2}(x,\tilde{\pi}^{\delta}),

we can proceed as in the proof of Theorem
4.20
, observing that actually the linear form
\ell_{t}^{2,c}(\sigma)
depends of the symbol

\sigma(\cdot,x_{2},\cdot,D_{x_{2}})\in\mathcal{A}_{0}^{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}_{2})}({\mathbb{H}})
and thus define a state on
\mathcal{A}_{0}^{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}_{2})}({\mathbb{H}})
. As their characterization has been given
in Theorem
A.9
in Appendix
A
, it is given by an operator-valued measure in
\mathcal{M}_{ov}^{+}\left({\mathbb{H}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}},\left(\mathcal{H}_{\tilde{\pi}}\otimes L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}_{2})\right)_{(x,\tilde{\pi})\in{\mathbb{H}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}}\right)
.
Finally, by construction of
\ell_{\cdot}^{2,c}
, these measures necessarily coincide with the map
t\mapsto\Gamma_{m,t}^{2}d\gamma_{t}^{2}
, and
we obtain the desired result.

5.2.3.
Invariance of the spherical semiclassical measures

We recall that the spherical part of the semiclassical measure, noted
\Gamma_{t}^{\infty}d\gamma_{t}^{\infty}
, is obtained as follows: for
\sigma\in\mathcal{A}_{0}^{2}({\mathbb{E}})
,

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}\theta(t)\int_{C_{\nu_{0}}\times\mathbb{S}^{0}}{\rm Tr}\left(\sigma_{\infty}(x,\pi,\omega)\Gamma_{t}^{\infty}(x,\pi,\omega)\right)d\gamma_{t}^{\infty}(x,\pi,\omega)dt=\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\lim_{R\rightarrow+\infty}\lim_{\hbar_{k}\rightarrow 0}\ell_{\hbar}^{2}(\theta,\sigma_{\epsilon}^{R}),

where we have introduced the notation

\forall(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta},\eta)\in{\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}\times{\mathbb{R}},\ \sigma_{\epsilon}^{R}(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta},\eta)=\sigma(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta},\eta)\chi\left(\frac{\beta-\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}}{\epsilon}\right)\left(1-\chi\left(\frac{\eta}{R}\right)\right).

Similarly as previously, Equation (
5.5
) gives straightforwardly that

\ell_{\hbar}^{2}(\theta,\left[\sigma_{\epsilon}^{R},H\right])=\mathcal{O}(\hbar),

and thus, for almost every
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
,

\int_{C_{\nu_{0}}\times\mathbb{S}^{0}}{\rm Tr}\left(\left[\sigma_{\infty}(x,\pi,\omega),H(\pi)\right]\Gamma_{t}^{\infty}(x,\pi,\omega)\right)d\gamma_{t}^{\infty}(x,\pi,\omega)dt=0.

And we deduce that similarly as before, the operator
\Gamma_{t}^{\infty}
decomposes for almost every
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
as follows

\forall(x,\pi)\in C_{\nu_{0}},\ \Gamma_{t}^{\infty}(x,\pi,\omega)=\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}^{*}}\Gamma_{n,t}^{\infty}(x,\pi,\omega),

where for
n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{>0}
,

\Gamma_{n,t}^{\infty}(x,\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}},\omega)=\Pi_{n}(\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}})\Gamma_{t}^{\infty}(x,\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}},\omega)\Pi_{n}(\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}}).

We focus now on the
m
-th mode
\Gamma_{m,t}^{\infty}d\gamma_{m,t}^{\infty}
of the spherical semiclassical measure.
Let
\sigma\in\mathcal{A}_{0}^{2}({\mathbb{E}})
commuting with the symbol of the subLaplacian
H
.

We introduce the following family of symbols: for
\hbar>0
,

\tau_{\hbar}^{\epsilon,R}=\hbar^{-1}\left(\eta^{-1}\sigma_{\epsilon}^{R}\right)_{\hbar}^{C_{\nu_{0}}}.

By Proposition
4.5
, seeing that
\eta^{-1}\sigma_{\epsilon}^{R}
is a second-microlocal symbol thanks to the cut-off in the variable
\eta
, we have
that
\tau_{\hbar}^{\epsilon,R}
belongs to the class of symbol
S_{0,0}^{0}
and we have the following estimate on the norm of its quantization:

(5.7)

{\rm Op}_{\hbar}(\tau_{\hbar}^{\epsilon,R})=\mathcal{O}_{L^{2}({\mathbb{E}})\rightarrow L^{2}({\mathbb{E}})}(1/(\hbar R)).

By symbolic calculus, we have the following equality:

[{\rm Op}_{\hbar}(\tau_{\hbar}^{\epsilon,R}),-\hbar^{2}\Delta_{\mathbb{E}}]=2\hbar{\rm Op}_{\hbar}((\pi(V)\cdot V)\tau_{\hbar}^{\epsilon,R})+\hbar^{2}{\rm Op}_{\hbar}(\Delta_{\mathbb{E}}\tau_{\hbar}^{\epsilon,R}).

By Lemma
3.17
and Equation (
3.17
), we have the following decomposition

(\pi(V)\cdot V)\tau_{\hbar}^{\epsilon,R}(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta})=\frac{i}{2}\partial_{\beta}\mu_{m}(\delta,\beta)X_{2}\tau_{\hbar}^{\epsilon,R}(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta})+\frac{1}{2}\left[\tau_{1,\hbar}^{\epsilon,R}(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta}),H(\pi^{\delta,\beta})\right],

with

\tau_{1,\hbar}^{\epsilon,R}=\frac{1}{i\delta}\pi^{\delta,\beta}(X_{3})X_{1}\tau_{\hbar}^{\epsilon,R}+i\partial_{\beta}\Pi_{m}(\pi^{\delta,\beta})X_{2}\tau_{\hbar}^{\epsilon,R}\in S_{0,0}^{0}.

Once again, the
H
-diagonal part of
(\pi(V)\cdot V)\tau_{\hbar}^{\epsilon,R}
is the one we are interesting about. We perform a Taylor expansion on
\mu_{m}
:

\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\beta}\mu_{m}(\delta,\beta)X_{2}\tau_{\hbar}^{\epsilon,R}(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta})=\frac{1}{2}\tilde{\mu}_{m}^{\prime\prime}(\nu_{0})\frac{\beta-\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}}{|\beta-\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}|}X_{2}\sigma_{\epsilon}^{R}\left(x,\pi^{\delta,\beta},\frac{\beta-\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}}{\hbar}\right)+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon),

where the remainder is caused by the cut-off at scale
\epsilon
close to the cone
C_{\nu_{0}}
.

Observe that this equality implies

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\lim_{R\rightarrow+\infty}\lim_{\hbar_{k}\rightarrow 0}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}\theta(t)\left({\rm Op}_{\hbar}\left(\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\beta}\mu_{m}X_{2}\tau_{\hbar}^{\epsilon,R}\right)\psi^{\hbar}(t),\psi^{\hbar}(t)\right)_{L^{2}({\mathbb{E}})}dt=

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{R}}\times C_{\nu_{0}}\times\mathbb{S}^{0}}\theta(t){\rm Tr}\left(\frac{1}{2}\tilde{\mu}_{m}^{\prime\prime}(\nu_{0})X_{2}\sigma_{\infty}(x,\pi,\omega)\Gamma_{t}^{\infty}(x,\pi,\omega)\right)d\gamma_{t}^{\infty}(x,\pi,\omega)dt.

Thus, writing

\ell_{\hbar}(\theta,\tau_{\hbar}^{\epsilon,R})=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}\theta(t)\left({\rm Op}_{\hbar}(\tau_{\hbar}^{\epsilon,R})\psi^{\hbar}(t),\psi^{\hbar}(t)\right)_{L^{2}({\mathbb{E}})}dt,

in order to show the invariance of the measure
d\gamma_{m,t}^{\infty}=\Gamma_{m,t}^{\infty}d\gamma_{t}^{\infty}
for the flow

(h,x_{2},\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}},\omega)\mapsto\left(h,x_{2}+\frac{s}{2}\tilde{\mu}_{m}^{\prime\prime}(\nu_{0})\omega,\pi^{\delta,\nu_{0}\delta^{1/3}},\omega\right),

we are left to prove that

(5.8)

\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\lim_{R\rightarrow+\infty}\lim_{\hbar_{k}\rightarrow 0}\ell_{\hbar_{k}}\left(\theta,\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\beta}\mu_{m}X_{2}\tau_{\hbar_{k}}^{\epsilon,R}\right)=0.

To this end, observe that

\displaystyle{\rm Op}_{\hbar}(i\partial_{\beta}\mu_{m}X_{2}\tau_{\hbar}^{\epsilon,R})

\displaystyle=2{\rm Op}_{\hbar}((\pi(V)\cdot V)\tau_{\hbar}^{\epsilon,R})-{\rm Op}_{\hbar}\left(\left[\tau_{1,\hbar}^{\epsilon,R},H\right]\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\hbar}\left[{\rm Op}_{\hbar}(\tau_{\hbar}^{\epsilon,R}),-\hbar^{2}\Delta_{\mathbb{E}}\right]-{\rm Op}_{\hbar}\left(\left[\tau_{1,\hbar}^{\epsilon,R},H\right]\right)-\hbar{\rm Op}_{\hbar}(\Delta_{\mathbb{E}}\tau_{\hbar}^{\epsilon,R})

Now, doing the same analysis for the symbol
\tau_{1,\hbar}^{\epsilon,R}
, one can also write that

-\ell_{\hbar}\left(\theta,\left[\tau_{1,\hbar}^{\epsilon,R},H\right]\right)=\hbar^{2}\ell_{\hbar}(\theta^{\prime},\tau_{1,\hbar}^{\epsilon,R})+\hbar\ell_{\hbar}(\theta,2(\pi(V)\cdot V)\tau_{1,\hbar}^{\epsilon,R})+\hbar^{2}\ell_{\hbar}(\theta,\Delta_{\mathbb{E}}\tau_{1,\hbar}^{\epsilon,R}),

and thus using the operator norm estimate (
5.7
),

-\ell_{\hbar}\left(\theta,\left[\tau_{1,\hbar}^{\epsilon,R},H\right]\right)-\hbar\ell_{\hbar}(\theta,\Delta_{\mathbb{E}}\tau_{\hbar}^{\epsilon,R})=\hbar\ell_{\hbar}(\theta,2(\pi(V)\cdot V)\tau_{1,\hbar}^{\epsilon,R}-\Delta_{\mathbb{E}}\tau_{\hbar}^{\epsilon,R})+\mathcal{O}(\hbar)

Using Lemma
5.6
, we decompose the symbol
2(\pi(V)\cdot V)\tau_{1,\hbar}^{\epsilon,R}-\Delta_{\mathbb{E}}\tau_{\hbar}^{\epsilon,R}
as its
H
-diagonal and
H
-off-diagonal part:

2(\pi(V)\cdot V)\tau_{1,\hbar}^{\epsilon,R}-\Delta_{{\mathbb{E}}}\tau_{\hbar}^{\epsilon,R}=\frac{1}{\delta}\partial_{\beta}\mu_{m}\Pi_{m}\pi^{\delta,\beta}(X_{3})\Pi_{m}\tau_{\hbar}^{\epsilon,R}+\frac{\partial_{\beta}^{2}\mu_{m}}{2}X_{2}^{2}\tau_{\hbar}^{\epsilon,R}+\frac{1}{2}[\tau_{2,\hbar}^{\epsilon,R},H],

for some computable symbol
\tau_{2,\hbar}^{\epsilon,R}
in
S_{0,0}^{0}
.
Using once again the symbolic calculus, the off-diagonal part contributes to a
\mathcal{O}(\hbar)
, while for the diagonal part, we make use of similar estimation as (
5.7
)
and we obtain a contribution of order
\mathcal{O}(1/R)
.

At the end, we obtain

{\rm Op}_{\hbar}(i\partial_{\beta}\mu_{m}X_{2}\tau_{\hbar}^{\epsilon,R})=\frac{1}{\hbar}\left[{\rm Op}_{\hbar}(\tau_{\hbar}^{\epsilon,R}),-\hbar^{2}\Delta_{\mathbb{E}}\right]+\mathcal{O}(1/R)+\mathcal{O}(\hbar).

Therefore,

\displaystyle\ell_{\hbar}\left(\theta,\partial_{\beta}\mu_{m}X_{2}\tau_{\hbar}^{\epsilon,R}\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}\theta(t)\left({\rm Op}_{\hbar}(\partial_{\beta}\mu_{m}X_{2}\tau_{\hbar}^{\epsilon,R})\psi^{\hbar}(t),\psi^{\hbar}(t)\right)dt

\displaystyle=-\frac{i}{\hbar}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}\theta(t)\left(\left[{\rm Op}_{\hbar}(\tau_{\hbar}^{\epsilon,R}),-\hbar^{2}\Delta_{\mathbb{E}}\right]\psi^{\hbar}(t),\psi^{\hbar}(t)\right)dt+\mathcal{O}(1/R)+\mathcal{O}(\hbar)

\displaystyle=\hbar\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}\theta(t)\frac{d}{dt}\left({\rm Op}_{\hbar}(\tau_{\hbar}^{\epsilon,R})\psi^{\hbar}(t),\psi^{\hbar}(t)\right)dt+\mathcal{O}(1/R)+\mathcal{O}(\hbar)

\displaystyle=-\hbar\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}\theta^{\prime}(t)\left({\rm Op}_{\hbar}(\tau_{\hbar}^{\epsilon,R})\psi^{\hbar}(t),\psi^{\hbar}(t)\right)dt+\mathcal{O}(1/R)+\mathcal{O}(\hbar)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}(1/R)+\mathcal{O}(\hbar),

which gives (
5.8
). We conclude knowing that
d\gamma_{m,t}^{\infty}
has finite mass and the flow for which we have proved invariance is non-zero as
\nu_{0}
is a non-degenerate
critical point for
\tilde{\mu}_{m}
.

5.3.
Application: obstruction to Strichartz estimates and local smoothing effect

We apply here the results of the previous section to the problem of the dispersion of the subLaplcian
\Delta_{\mathbb{E}}
.

5.3.1.
Proof of Theorem
1.17

We argue by contradiction and suppose that the estimate (
1.8
) holds for some choice of
\epsilon>0
,
s>0
,
C>0
and for some bounded open set
\Omega
. Let consider the
initial datum
(u_{0}^{\hbar})_{\hbar>0}
given by Equation (
4.12
) in Section
4.4.2
where we choose
h_{0}=0
,
\eta_{0}=0
and
\nu_{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}
to be the unique critical point of
the first eigenvalue
\nu\mapsto\tilde{\mu}_{1}(\nu)
. We also choose
\Phi
to be in the eigenspace of
H(\pi^{\delta_{0},\nu_{0}\delta_{0}^{1/3}})
corresponding to the eigenvalue
\mu_{1}(\delta_{0},\nu_{0}\delta_{0}^{1/3})
.
As already seen, it is a bounded family in
L^{2}({\mathbb{E}})
converging weakly to zero as
\hbar
goes to zero. Indeed, we check that for
\psi\in C_{c}({\mathbb{E}})
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{E}}}u_{0}^{\hbar}(x)\psi(x)\,dx

\displaystyle=\hbar^{-3/2}\int_{{\mathbb{E}}}a(\hbar^{-1/2}\cdot h_{x})\varphi(x_{2})\left(\pi^{\delta_{0},\nu_{0}\delta_{0}^{1/3}}(\hbar^{-1}\cdot x)\Phi,\Phi\right)\psi(x)\,dx

\displaystyle=\hbar^{3/2}\int_{{\mathbb{E}}}a(h_{x})\varphi(x_{2})\left(\pi^{\delta_{0},\nu_{0}\delta_{0}^{1/3}}(\hbar^{-1/2}\cdot h_{x},\hbar^{-1}\cdot x_{2})\Phi,\Phi\right)\psi(\hbar^{1/2}\cdot h_{x},x_{2})\,dx,

and, as
\hbar\rightarrow 0
, the last term converges to zero.

Estimate (
1.8
) then implies that the solution to the Schrödinger equation
(u^{\hbar}(\cdot))_{\hbar>0}
is a bounded family in
L^{2}((0,\epsilon),\tilde{H}^{s}(\Omega))
, where the Hilbert spaces
\left(\tilde{H}^{s}(\Omega)\right)_{s\in{\mathbb{R}}}
are
the Sobolev spaces adapted to the Engel group
{\mathbb{E}}
, see
[
34
]
and
[
21
]
.
Moreover, since
u^{\hbar}
is a solution of (
1.2
) with
\tau=2
, i.e we have

i\partial_{t}u^{\hbar}=-\Delta_{{\mathbb{E}}}u^{\hbar},

thus we can write that
\partial_{t}u^{\hbar}\in L^{2}((0,\epsilon),\tilde{H}^{-2}(\Omega))
. Since
\Omega
is a bounded domain, by Rellich’s theorem, the injection of
\tilde{H}^{s}(\Omega)
in
L^{2}(\Omega)
is compact
and by the Aubin-Lions lemma, there exists a subsequence
(u^{\hbar_{k}})_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}
, with
\hbar_{k}\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits_{k\rightarrow+\infty}0
,
converging strongly in
L^{2}((0,\epsilon)\times\Omega)
: the limit is necessarily zero as it is already converging weakly to zero.
Indeed, for
\theta\in{\mathbb{C}}_{c}({\mathbb{R}})
and
\psi\in C_{c}({\mathbb{E}})
we have

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\epsilon}\theta(t)\left(u^{\hbar}(t),\psi\right)_{L^{2}(\Omega)}dt

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\epsilon}\theta(t)\left(u_{0}^{\hbar},e^{-it\Delta_{{\mathbb{E}}}}({\mathbb{1}}_{\Omega}\psi)\right)_{L^{2}({\mathbb{E}})}dt

\displaystyle=\left(u_{0}^{\hbar},\int_{0}^{\epsilon}\theta(t)e^{-it\Delta_{{\mathbb{E}}}}({\mathbb{1}}_{\Omega}\psi)\,dt\right)_{L^{2}({\mathbb{E}})},

and we conclude by weak convergence of
(u_{0}^{\hbar})_{\hbar>0}
to zero.

As a consequence, we obtain that any time-averaged semiclassical measure
t\mapsto\Gamma_{t}d\gamma_{t}
must necessarily be equal to zero for almost every
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
.
To compute
\Gamma_{t}d\gamma_{t}
for all time
t\in{\mathbb{R}}_{t}
, we use the existence, up to a further extraction, of a time-averaged second microlocal semiclassical measures

(t\mapsto\Gamma_{t}^{2}d\gamma_{t}^{2},t\mapsto\Gamma^{\infty}_{t}d\gamma^{\infty}_{t})
.
By Theorem
5.2
,
\Gamma^{\infty}_{t}d\gamma^{\infty}_{t}=0
for almost all time
t
and
\Gamma_{t}^{2}d\gamma_{t}^{2}
satisfies a weak Heisenberg equation.
Using Proposition
5.4
and Proposition
4.23
for the computation of the second-microlocal measures of
(u_{0}^{\hbar})_{\hbar>0}
, the Heisenberg equation can be integrated and we find that, for all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, we have

d\gamma_{t}^{2}=\delta_{\{h=0\}}\otimes\delta_{\{\tilde{\pi}^{\delta}=\tilde{\pi}^{\delta_{0}}\}},\quad\Gamma_{t}^{2}=\lvert\Phi\rangle\langle\Phi\rvert\otimes\lvert e^{it\frac{\tilde{\mu}_{1}^{\prime\prime}(\nu_{0})}{2}\partial_{x_{2}}^{2}}\varphi\rangle\langle e^{it\frac{\tilde{\mu}_{1}^{\prime\prime}(\nu_{0})}{2}\partial_{x_{2}}^{2}}\varphi\rvert.

Thus the full semiclassical measure
\Gamma_{t}d\gamma_{t}
is equal to

(5.9)

d\gamma_{t}=\delta_{\{h=0\}}\otimes|e^{it\frac{\tilde{\mu}_{1}^{\prime\prime}(\nu_{0})}{2}\partial_{x_{2}}^{2}}\varphi|^{2}dx_{2}\otimes\delta_{\{(\delta,\beta)=(\delta_{0},\nu_{0}\delta_{0}^{1/3})\}},\quad\Gamma_{t}=\lvert\Phi\rangle\langle\Phi\rvert,

which is not uniformly equal to zero and we obtain a contradiction.

5.3.2.
Proof ot Theorem
1.18

We argue once again by contradiction and suppose that the estimate (
1.8
) holds for some choice of
\epsilon>0
,
s>0
,
C>0
and for some bounded open set
\Omega
, which we suppose to contain 0. Let consider the
initial datum
(u_{0}^{\hbar})_{\hbar>0}
as in the proof of Theorem
1.17
and write
u^{\hbar}(t)
the associated solution of the Schrödinger equation. Choose
\chi_{\Omega}\in C_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{E}})
a smooth cutoff with support in
\Omega
and satisfying
\chi_{\Omega}(0)=1
, and
a smooth function
\theta\in C_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}})
uniformly equal to zero in a neighborhood of 0. We observe that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\epsilon}\left(\chi_{\Omega}\theta(i\hbar X_{2})u^{\hbar}(t),u^{\hbar}(t)\right)\,dt

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\epsilon}\left(\chi_{\Omega}\theta(i\hbar X_{2})|\hbar X_{2}|^{-s}|\hbar X_{2}|^{s}u^{\hbar}(t),u^{\hbar}(t)\right)\,dt

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\epsilon}\left({\rm Op}_{\hbar}\left(\chi_{\Omega}\tilde{\theta}(i\pi(X_{2}))\right)|\hbar X_{2}|^{s}u^{\hbar}(t),u^{\hbar}(t)\right)\,dt,

where
\tilde{\theta}(\beta)=|\beta|^{-s}\theta(\beta)
, and thus
\tilde{\theta}\in C_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}})
. Introducing the symbol
\sigma=\chi_{\Omega}\tilde{\theta}(i\pi(X_{2}))\in\mathcal{A}_{0}({\mathbb{E}})
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\int_{0}^{\epsilon}\left(\chi_{\Omega}\theta(i\hbar X_{2})u^{\hbar}(t),u^{\hbar}(t)\right)\,dt\right|

\displaystyle=\hbar^{s}\left|\int_{0}^{\epsilon}\left({\rm Op}_{\hbar}(\sigma)|X_{2}|^{s}u^{\hbar}(t),u^{\hbar}(t)\right)\,dt\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\hbar^{s}\int_{0}^{\epsilon}\||X_{2}|^{s}\left(e^{it\Delta_{\mathbb{E}}}u_{0}\right)\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}\,dt\leq C\hbar^{s}\|u_{0}^{\hbar}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{E}})},

for some constant
C>0
. As
\hbar\rightarrow 0
, if we denote again by
t\mapsto\Gamma_{t}d\gamma_{t}
the time-averaged semiclassical measures of
(u^{\hbar})_{\hbar>0}
, we obtain

\int_{0}^{\epsilon}\int_{{\mathbb{E}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{E}}}}{\rm Tr}\left(\chi_{\Omega}(x)\theta(i\pi(X_{2}))\Gamma_{t}(x,\pi)\right)d\gamma_{t}(x,\pi)dt=0.

As
t\mapsto\Gamma_{t}d\gamma_{t}
has been computed in the previous proof, we find

(5.10)

\langle\theta(i\pi^{\delta_{0},\nu_{0}\delta_{0}^{1/3}}(X_{2}))\Phi,\Phi\rangle\int_{0}^{\epsilon}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}\chi_{\Omega}(0,x_{2})\left|e^{it\frac{\tilde{\mu}_{1}^{\prime\prime}(\nu_{0})}{2}\partial_{x_{2}}^{2}}\varphi(x_{2})\right|^{2}\,dx_{2}dt=0.

Since we can write

\langle\theta(i\pi^{\delta_{0},\nu_{0}\delta_{0}^{1/3}}(X_{2}))\Phi,\Phi\rangle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\theta\left(-\left(\nu_{0}\delta_{0}^{1/3}+\frac{\delta_{0}}{2}\xi^{2}\right)\right)\left|\Phi(\xi)\right|^{2}\,d\xi,

it possible to choose
\theta
such that this quantity is not equal to zero. Equation (
5.10
) gives then a contradiction.

5.3.3.
Proof of Proposition
1.16

The proof follows once again by arguing by contradiction and by the use of the particular example
(u_{0}^{\hbar})_{\hbar>0}
and the computation of the time-averaged semiclassical measure of the associated solution
to the Schrödinger equation. Indeed, suppose that the estimate
1.6
holds for some couple of exponents
(q,p)
with
q>2
and
p>2
, then by a duality argument, any weak limit of time-averaged density
probabilities
(|u^{\hbar}(t,x)|^{2}dxdt)_{\hbar>0}
is in
L^{q/2}({\mathbb{R}}_{t},L^{p/2}({\mathbb{E}}))
and as such cannot be a measure concentrated on a lower dimensional manifold.

However, since the family
(u^{\hbar})_{\hbar>0}
satisfies at all time
t
the assumption (
1.3
) of
\hbar
-oscillation, we deduce by Proposition
3.6
that any weak limit
of
(|u^{\hbar}(t,x)|^{2}dxdt)_{\hbar>0}
is a marginal of a semiclassical measure. By Equation (
5.9
), we obtain a contradiction as we have exhibited
a concentration phenomenon on the subgroup
{\mathbb{R}}_{2}
.

Appendix A
Analysis of square-integrable families valued in a Hilbert space

Let
G
be a graded nilpotent Lie group of dimension
d
and homogeneous dimension
Q
. Let
\mathcal{G}
be a separable Hilbert space. In this appendix, we consider the semiclassical
pseudodifferential operators acting on
L^{2}(G,\mathcal{G})
, the set of square-integrable families valued in
\mathcal{G}
, i.e measurable functions from

G
into
\mathcal{G}
for which one has

\|f\|_{L^{2}(G,\mathcal{G})}:=\int_{G}\|f(x)\|_{\mathcal{G}}^{2}\,dx.

We shall also consider
\mathcal{S}(G,\mathcal{G})
the space of smooth functions
f
defined on
G
and valued in
\mathcal{G}
such that

\forall\alpha,\beta\in{\mathbb{N}}^{d},\ \exists C_{\alpha,\beta}>0,\ \sup_{x\in G}\|x^{\alpha}\partial_{x}^{\beta}f(x)\|_{\mathcal{G}}\leq C_{\alpha,\beta}.

In a first time, we extend the Fourier transform
\mathcal{F}_{G}
to function valued in the Banach algebra
\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{G})
of compact operators on
\mathcal{G}
. In a second time, we develop the notion of symbols as well as present their quantization,
and finally we discuss the associated notion of semiclassical measure.

A.1.
Fourier transform

A.1.1.
Tensor product

As to make the presentaion clear, we recall and define the two notions of tensor products we will encounter. In what follows we fix a representation
\pi\in\widehat{G}
and we
write
\mathcal{H}_{\pi}
for the (separable) Hilbert space associated to
\pi
.

First, the tensor product
\mathcal{H}_{\pi}\otimes\mathcal{G}
is the Hilbert space defined as the completion of the algebraic tensor product
\mathcal{H}_{\pi}\otimes\mathcal{G}

for the following hermitian product

\forall(\Phi_{1},g_{1}),(\Phi_{2},g_{2})\in\mathcal{H}_{\pi}\times\mathcal{G},\ \langle\Phi_{1}\otimes g_{1},\Phi_{2}\otimes g_{2}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{\pi}\otimes\mathcal{G}}=\langle\Phi_{1},\Phi_{2}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{\pi}}\langle g_{1},g_{2}\rangle_{\mathcal{G}}.

For a matter of notation, we keep the same one for either the algebraic one and its completion. This definition is straightforward as we are dealing here with Hilbert spaces. However, when one wish to take the tensor product of Banach spaces, choices need to be made.
As it will be our main concern, we only consider here the case of the Banach spaces
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\pi})
of bounded operators on
\mathcal{H}_{\pi}
and
\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{G})
the space of
compact operators on
\mathcal{G}
.

Every element of the algebraic tensor product
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\pi})\otimes\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{G})
can be identified with a bounded operator on the Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{\pi}\otimes\mathcal{G}
.
We denote by
\iota_{\pi}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\pi})\otimes\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{G})\rightarrow\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\pi}\otimes\mathcal{G})
this injection.

Definition A.1
.

We can define a norm on
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\pi})\otimes\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{G})
by the following formula:

\forall x\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\pi})\otimes\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{G}),\ \|x\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\pi})\otimes\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{G})}=\|\iota_{\pi}(x)\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\pi}\otimes\mathcal{G})}.

We still denote by
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\pi})\otimes\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{G})
the completion of the algebraic tensor product by this norm and by
\iota_{\pi}
the extended injection.

We easily check that
\iota_{\pi}(\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\pi})\otimes\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{G}))
is a closed subspace of
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\pi}\otimes\mathcal{B})
stable by composition.

Remark A.2
.

Our choice of norm on the algebraic tensor product
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\pi})\otimes\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{G})
coincide with the projective cross norm. Other cross norms might be possible, but they are all dominated
by the one introduced in the previous definition.

A.1.2.
Extension of
\mathcal{F}_{G}

Let
f\in\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{H}},\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{G}))
. For
g_{1},g_{2}\in\mathcal{G}
, the function
x\in G\mapsto f^{g_{1},g_{2}}(x)=\langle f(x)g_{1},g_{2}\rangle_{\mathcal{G}}
is in the space of Schwartz functions
\mathcal{S}(G)
.
We can consider its Fourier transform
\mathcal{F}(f^{g_{1},g_{2}})
and for
\pi\in\widehat{G}
, we have

\|\mathcal{F}_{G}(f^{g_{1},g_{2}})(\pi)\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\pi})}\leq\int_{G}|\langle f(x)g_{1},g_{2}\rangle_{\mathcal{G}}|\,dx.

We choose a Hermitian basis
(g_{l})_{l\in{\mathbb{N}}}
of
\mathcal{G}
. We define the Fourier tranform of
f
at
\pi\in\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}
by

(A.1)

\mathcal{F}_{G}f(\pi):=\sum_{i,j\in{\mathbb{N}}}\mathcal{F}(f^{g_{i},g_{j}})(\pi)\otimes\lvert g_{i}\rangle\langle g_{j}\rvert.

Proposition A.3
.

For
f\in\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{H}},\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{G}))
, Equation (
A.1
) defines the Fourier transform of
f
as
a bounded field of operators
\{\mathcal{F}_{G}f(\pi)\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\pi})\otimes\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{G})\,:\,\pi\in\widehat{G}\}
. Moreover, it does not depend on the choice
of the Hermitian basis
(g_{l})_{l\in{\mathbb{N}}}
.

Proof.

For
l\in{\mathbb{N}}
we write
\mathbb{P}_{l}
for the orthogonal projector on
(g_{1},\dots,g_{l})
and
f^{l}=\mathbb{P}_{l}f\mathbb{P}_{l}
the point-wise restriction of
f
to these spaces.
As
f
is valued in
\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{G})
we have the pointwise convergence
f^{l}(x)\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits_{l\rightarrow\infty}f(x)
for all
x\in G
: by dominated convergence we easily deduce

\int_{G}\|f-f^{l}\|_{\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{G})}\,dx\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits_{l\rightarrow\infty}0.

Observe that for
l,l^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}
,

\left\lVert\sum_{|i|,|j|\leq l}\mathcal{F}_{G}(f^{g_{i},g_{j}})(\pi)\otimes\lvert g_{i}\rangle\langle g_{j}\rvert-\sum_{|i|,|j|\leq l^{\prime}}\mathcal{F}_{G}(f^{g_{i},g_{j}})(\pi)\otimes\lvert g_{i}\rangle\langle g_{j}\rvert\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\pi})\otimes\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{G})}\leq\int_{{\mathbb{H}}}\|f^{l}-f^{l^{\prime}}\|_{\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{G})}\,dx,

hence, by Cauchy criterion we get the convergence of the serie and the definition of
\mathcal{F}_{G}f
makes sense. Moreover, we get the estimate

\sup_{\pi\in\widehat{G}}\lVert\mathcal{F}_{G}f(\pi)\rVert_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\pi})\otimes\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{G})}\leq\lVert f\rVert_{L^{1}(G,\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{G}))}.

Finally, let
(g^{\prime}_{k})_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}
a second Hermitian basis of
\mathcal{G}
. For
i\in{\mathbb{N}}
, write

g_{i}=\sum_{j\in{\mathbb{N}}}a_{i,j}g^{\prime}_{j},

with
(a_{i,j})_{j\in{\mathbb{N}}}
in
\ell^{2}({\mathbb{N}})
. We compute that

\displaystyle\sum_{k,l\in{\mathbb{N}}}\mathcal{F}_{G}(f^{g_{k},g_{l}})(\pi)\otimes\lvert g_{k}\rangle\langle g_{l}\rvert

\displaystyle=\sum_{k,l\in{\mathbb{N}}}\left(\sum_{i,j\in{\mathbb{N}}}a_{k,i}\bar{a}_{l,j}\mathcal{F}_{G}(f^{g^{\prime}_{i},g^{\prime}_{j}})(\pi)\right)\otimes\left(\sum_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}}\bar{a}_{k,i^{\prime}}a_{l,j^{\prime}}\lvert g^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}\rangle\langle g^{\prime}_{j^{\prime}}\rvert\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j\in{\mathbb{N}}}\sum_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}}\left(\sum_{k,l\in{\mathbb{N}}}a_{k,i}\bar{a}_{k,i^{\prime}}a_{l,j^{\prime}}\bar{a}_{l,j}\right)\mathcal{F}_{G}(f^{g^{\prime}_{i},g^{\prime}_{j}})(\pi)\otimes\lvert g^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}\rangle\langle g^{\prime}_{j^{\prime}}\rvert

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j\in{\mathbb{N}}}\mathcal{F}_{G}(f^{g^{\prime}_{i},g^{\prime}_{j}})(\pi)\otimes\lvert g^{\prime}_{i}\rangle\langle g^{\prime}_{j}\rvert.

∎

As we do not need a Fourier inversion formula, we will simply mention here that the Fourier transform defined on
\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{H}},\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{G}))
is injective.

A.1.3.
Convolution and Fourier transform

Definition A.4
.

Let
f_{1},f_{2}\in\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{H}},\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{G}))
. Define the convolution product of
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
by

(f_{1}*f_{2})(x)=\int_{{\mathbb{H}}}f_{1}(y)\circ f_{2}(y^{-1}x)\,dy.

Oberve that
f_{1}*f_{2}
is in
\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{H}},\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{G}))
.

Proposition A.5
.

For
f_{1},f_{2}\in\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{H}},\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{G}))
, we have for a representation
\pi\in\widehat{G}
:

\mathcal{F}_{G}(f_{1}*f_{2})(\pi)=\mathcal{F}_{G}f_{2}(\pi)\circ\mathcal{F}_{G}f_{1}(\pi).

Proof.

Let
(g_{l})_{l\in{\mathbb{N}}}
be a Hermitian basis of
\mathcal{G}
. For
i,j\in{\mathbb{N}}
, we have for
x\in G
,

\displaystyle(f_{1}*f_{2})^{g_{i},g_{j}}(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathbb{H}}}\langle f_{1}(y)f_{2}(y^{-1}x)g_{i},g_{j}\rangle_{\mathcal{G}}\,dy

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathbb{H}}}\sum_{l\in{\mathbb{N}}}\langle f_{2}(y^{-1}x)g_{i},g_{l}\rangle_{\mathcal{G}}\langle f_{1}(y)g_{l},g_{j}\rangle_{\mathcal{G}}\,dy

\displaystyle=\sum_{l\in{\mathbb{N}}}\left(f_{1}^{g_{l},g_{j}}*f_{2}^{g_{i},g_{l}}\right)(x).

Thus, taking the Fourier transform, we have

\mathcal{F}_{G}(f_{1}*f_{2})^{g_{i},g_{j}}(\pi)=\sum_{l\in{\mathbb{N}}}\mathcal{F}_{G}(f_{2}^{g_{i},g_{l}})(\pi)\circ\mathcal{F}_{G}(f_{1}^{g_{l},g_{j}})(\pi),

from which we deduce the result of the proposition.
∎

A.2.
Semiclassical pseudodifferential operators

A.2.1.
Algebra of smoothing symbols

We first define the space of symbols we want to quantize.

Definition A.6
.

We denote by
\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\mathcal{G}}(G)
the space of field of operators
\sigma
of the form

\sigma=\{\sigma(x,\pi)\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\pi})\otimes\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{G})\,:\,(x,\pi)\in G\times\widehat{G}\},

for which there exists a smooth and compactly supported map
\kappa:x\in G\mapsto\kappa_{x}\in\mathcal{S}(G,\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{G}))
such that

\sigma(x,\pi)=\mathcal{F}_{G}\kappa_{x}(\pi).

As the extended Fourier transform is still injective, it yields a one-to-one correspondence between the symbol
\sigma
and the map
\kappa
.
As before, we call
\kappa
the associated convolution kernel to
\sigma
.
Once again, by Proposition
A.5
the set
\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\mathcal{G}}(G)
is an algebra for the composition of symbols.

We can also define the following submultiplicative norm on
\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\mathcal{G}}(G)
: for a symbol
\sigma
, we define

\|\sigma\|_{\mathcal{A}^{\mathcal{G}}(G)}:=\sup_{(x,\pi)\in G\times\widehat{G}}\|\sigma(x,\pi)\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\pi})\otimes\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{G})}.

A.2.2.
Quantization

We now define the quantization of these symbols by expliciting their action on Schwartz functions.

Definition A.7
.

Let
\sigma\in\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\mathcal{G}}(G)
. For
f\in\mathcal{S}(G,\mathcal{G})
, we set, for
x\in G
,

{\rm Op}_{\hbar}^{\mathcal{G}}(\sigma)f(x)=\hbar^{-Q}\int_{G\times\widehat{G}}{\rm Tr}_{\mathcal{H}_{\pi}}(\pi(\hbar^{-1}\cdot(y^{-1}x))\sigma(x,\pi)f(y))\,d\mu_{\widehat{G}}(\pi)dy.

Then
{\rm Op}_{\hbar}^{\mathcal{G}}(\sigma)f
is again in
\mathcal{S}(G,\mathcal{G})
.

These operators are called
semiclassical pseudodifferential operators
on
\mathcal{S}(G,\mathcal{G})
.

Remark A.8
.

In the precedent quantization formula, the operator
\sigma(x,\pi)\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\pi})\otimes\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{G})
must be understood as
acting on on
0_{\mathcal{H}_{\pi}}\otimes f(y)\in\mathcal{G}
for
y\in G
. Meanwhile, the trace operator
{\rm Tr}_{\mathcal{H}_{\pi}}:\mathcal{L}^{1}(\mathcal{H}_{\pi})\rightarrow{\mathbb{C}}

is extended to the alegbraic tensor product of
\mathcal{L}^{1}(\mathcal{H}_{\pi})
and
\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{G})
and into
\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{G})
in the obvious way, and then extended to
the cross-norm completion of
\mathcal{L}^{1}(\mathcal{H}_{\pi})\otimes\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{G})
by continuity.

Following
[
16
]
,
[
17
]
, these operators extend their action on the space
L^{2}(G,\mathcal{G})
as bounded operators:

\exists C>0,\ \forall\sigma\in\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\mathcal{G}}(G),\ \forall\hbar>0,\ \|{\rm Op}_{\hbar}^{\mathcal{G}}(\sigma)\|_{L^{2}(G,\mathcal{G})}\leq C\int_{G}\sup_{x\in G}\|\kappa_{x}(z)\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{G})}\,dz.

We denote by
\|\sigma\|_{\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\mathcal{G}}(G)}
the right-hand side of this inequality.

A.3.
Semiclassical measures of a bounded square-integrable family

As presented in Section
3.2
, we describe the structure of the limiting objects of the quadratic quantities

\left({\rm Op}_{\hbar}^{\mathcal{G}}(\sigma)f^{\hbar},f^{\hbar}\right)_{L^{2}(G,\mathcal{G})},

where
\sigma\in\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\mathcal{G}}(G)
and
(f^{\hbar})_{\hbar>0}
is a bounded family of
L^{2}(G,\mathcal{G})
.

Theorem A.9
.

Let
(f^{\hbar})_{\hbar>0}
be a bounded family in
L^{2}(G,\mathcal{G})
. There exists a sequence
(\hbar_{k})_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}
in
(0,+\infty)
with
\hbar_{k}\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits_{k\rightarrow+\infty}0

and a positive operator-valued measure
\Gamma^{\mathcal{G}}d\gamma^{\mathcal{G}}
in the space

\mathcal{M}_{ov}^{+}\left(G\times\widehat{G},({\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}\otimes\mathcal{G})_{(x,\pi)\in G\times\widehat{G}}\right),

such that for all
\sigma\in\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\mathcal{G}}(G)
, we have

\left({\rm Op}_{\hbar_{k}}(\sigma)f^{\hbar_{k}},f^{\hbar_{k}}\right)\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits_{k\rightarrow+\infty}\int_{{\mathbb{H}}\times\widehat{{\mathbb{H}}}}{\rm Tr}_{\mathcal{H}_{\pi}\otimes\mathcal{G}}(\sigma(x,\pi)\Gamma^{\mathcal{G}}(x,\pi))\,d\gamma^{\mathcal{G}}(x,\pi).

Given the subsequence
(\hbar_{k})_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}
,
\Gamma^{\mathcal{G}}d\gamma^{\mathcal{G}}
is the only operator-valued measure for which the above convergence holds.

Proof.

Let
(g_{l})_{l\in{\mathbb{N}}}
be a Hermitian basis of
\mathcal{G}
. We write

f^{\hbar}(x)=\sum_{l\in{\mathbb{N}}}f^{\hbar}_{l}(x)g_{l},\ x\in{\mathbb{H}},

where
f^{\hbar}_{l}(x)=\langle f^{\hbar}(x),g_{l}\rangle_{\mathcal{G}}
. As we have

(A.2)

\sum_{l\in{\mathbb{N}}}\|f^{\hbar}_{l}\|_{L^{2}(G)}^{2}=\|f^{\hbar}\|_{L^{2}(G,\mathcal{G})}^{2}<+\infty,

each family
(f^{\hbar}_{l})_{\hbar>0}
is bounded in
L^{2}(G)
. By a diagonal extraction process, we can find a common sequence
(\hbar_{k})_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}
and a family
of operator-valued measures
(\Gamma_{k,l}d\gamma_{k,l})_{(k,l)\in{\mathbb{N}}^{2}}
in
{\mathcal{M}}_{ov}(G\times\widehat{G})

such that for all
\sigma\in\mathcal{A}_{0}(G)
and for all
(k,l)\in{\mathbb{N}}^{2}
, we have

\left({\rm Op}_{\hbar}(\sigma)f^{\hbar}_{k},f^{\hbar}_{l}\right)\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits_{k\rightarrow+\infty}\int_{G\times\widehat{G}}{\rm Tr}_{\mathcal{H}_{\pi}}(\sigma(x,\pi)\Gamma_{k,l}(x,\pi))\,d\gamma_{k,l}(x,\pi),

with
\Gamma_{l,l}d\gamma_{l,l}
being positive and satisfying
\|\Gamma_{l,l}d\gamma_{l,l}\|_{\mathcal{M}}\leq\limsup_{k\rightarrow+\infty}\|f^{\hbar_{n}}_{l}\|_{L^{2}(G)}
for
l\in{\mathbb{N}}
.
Moreover, by polarization identities one can show that for
k,l\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
\gamma_{k,l}
is absolutely continuous with respect to
\gamma_{k,k}+\gamma_{l,l}
.

We construct now our positive measure in
\mathcal{M}_{ov}^{+}\left(G\times\widehat{G},({\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}\otimes\mathcal{G})_{(x,\pi)\in G\times\widehat{G}}\right)
.
By Equation (
A.2
), we know that

\limsup_{k\rightarrow+\infty}\sum_{l\in{\mathbb{N}}}\|f^{\hbar_{k}}_{l}\|_{L^{2}(G)}^{2}<+\infty,

and we deduce that we can define a positive Radon measure of finite mass by the formula

(A.3)

\gamma^{\mathcal{G}}=\sum_{l\in{\mathbb{N}}}\gamma_{l,l}.

By the Radon-Nikodym theorem, there exists for every
(k,l)\in{\mathbb{N}}^{2}
a
\gamma^{\mathcal{G}}
-measurable density
a_{k,l}
such that

\gamma_{k,l}=a_{k,l}\gamma^{\mathcal{G}},

with
0\leq\|a_{l,l}\|\leq 1
,
d\gamma^{\mathcal{G}}
-almost everywhere.

We now define the field of trace class operators
\Gamma^{\mathcal{G}}=\{\Gamma^{\mathcal{G}}(x,\pi)\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{\pi})\otimes\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{G})\,:\,(x,\pi)\in G\times\widehat{G}\}
by

\Gamma^{\mathcal{G}}(x,\pi)=\sum_{k,l\in{\mathbb{N}}}\Gamma_{k,l}(x,\pi)\otimes a_{k,l}\lvert g_{k}\rangle\langle g_{l}\rvert.

We can check that, for all
(x,\pi)\in G\times\widehat{G}
,
\Gamma^{\mathcal{G}}(x,\pi)
is a positive self-adjoint operator
and satisfies

\int_{G\times\widehat{G}}{\rm Tr}_{\mathcal{H}_{\pi}\otimes\mathcal{G}}(\Gamma^{\mathcal{G}}(x,\pi))\,d\gamma^{\mathcal{G}}(x,\pi)=\sum_{l\in{\mathbb{N}}}\int_{G\times\widehat{G}}{\rm Tr}_{\mathcal{H}_{\pi}}(\Gamma_{l,l}(x,\pi))a_{l,l}\,d\gamma^{\mathcal{G}}=\sum_{l\in{\mathbb{N}}}\gamma_{l,l}(G\times\widehat{G})<+\infty.

We have thus constructed an element
\Gamma^{\mathcal{G}}d\gamma^{\mathcal{G}}
of
\mathcal{M}_{ov}^{+}\left(G\times\widehat{G},({\mathcal{H}}_{\pi}\otimes\mathcal{G})_{(x,\pi)\in G\times\widehat{G}}\right)
.
We need now to prove that this operator-valued has the desired property.

Let
\sigma\in\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\mathcal{G}}(G)
. For
l\in{\mathbb{N}}
, we denote by
\mathbb{P}_{l}
the projector
on the finite dimensional subspace of
\mathcal{G}
generated by
(g_{k})_{0\leq k\leq l}
. We define the symbol
\sigma_{l}\in\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\mathcal{G}}(G)
by

\forall(x,\pi)\in G\times\widehat{G},\ \sigma_{l}(x,\pi)=\mathbb{P}_{l}\sigma(x,\pi)\mathbb{P}_{l}.

Indeed, if we write
\sigma=\mathcal{F}_{G}\kappa_{x}
with
\kappa\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{c}(G,\mathcal{S}(G,\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{G})))
, we simply have
\sigma_{l}=\mathcal{F}_{G}\kappa_{x}^{l}
with

\forall(x,y)\in G^{2},\ \kappa_{x}^{l}(y)=\mathbb{P}_{l}\kappa_{x}(y)\mathbb{P}_{l}.

Since
\kappa_{x}(y)
is a compact operator for all
(x,y)\in G^{2}
and since
\kappa
is compact in its first variable and Schwartz in its second one, we easily get that

(A.4)

\|\sigma-\sigma_{l}\|_{\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\mathcal{G}}(G)}\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits_{l\rightarrow+\infty}0.

We observe that

\displaystyle\left({\rm Op}_{\hbar}(\sigma_{l})f^{\hbar},f^{\hbar}\right)_{L^{2}(G,\mathcal{G})}

\displaystyle=\int_{G\times G\times\widehat{G}}{\rm Tr}_{\mathcal{H}_{\pi}}\left(\pi(\hbar^{-1}\cdot(y^{-1}x))\langle\sigma_{l}(x,\pi)f^{\hbar}(y),f^{\hbar}(x)\rangle_{\mathcal{G}}\right)\,d\mu_{\widehat{G}}(\pi)dydx

\displaystyle=\sum_{k,k^{\prime}\leq l}\int_{G\times G\times\widehat{G}}{\rm Tr}_{\mathcal{H}_{\pi}}\left(\tilde{\pi}^{\lambda}(\hbar^{-1}\cdot(y^{-1}x))\langle\sigma_{l}(x,\pi)g_{k},g_{k}^{\prime}\rangle_{\mathcal{G}}\right)f^{\hbar}_{k}(y)f^{\hbar}_{k^{\prime}}(x)\,d\mu_{\widehat{G}}(\pi)dydx

\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits_{\hbar\rightarrow 0}\sum_{k,k^{\prime}\leq l}\int_{G\times\widehat{G}}{\rm Tr}_{\mathcal{H}_{\pi}}\left(\langle\sigma_{l}(x,\pi)g_{k},g_{k}^{\prime}\rangle_{\mathcal{G}}\Gamma_{k,k^{\prime}}(x,\pi)\right)d\gamma_{k,k^{\prime}}(x,\pi)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k,k^{\prime}\leq l}\int_{G\times\widehat{G}}{\rm Tr}_{\mathcal{H}_{\pi}}\left(a_{k,k^{\prime}}(x,\pi)\langle\sigma_{l}(x,\pi)g_{k},g_{k}^{\prime}\rangle_{\mathcal{G}}\Gamma_{k,k^{\prime}}(x,\pi)\right)d\gamma^{\mathcal{G}}(x,\pi)

\displaystyle=\int_{G\times\widehat{G}}{\rm Tr}_{\mathcal{H}_{\pi}\otimes\mathcal{G}}\left(\sigma_{l}(x,\pi)\Gamma^{\mathcal{G}}(x,\pi)\right)d\gamma^{\mathcal{G}}(x,\pi).

The conclusion follows by letting
l
go to
+\infty
: we observe that by the approximation (
A.4
) and Lebesgue dominated convergence theorem, using that
the inequality
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{A}^{\mathcal{G}}}\leq\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{A}^{\mathcal{G}}_{0}}
, we have

\int_{G\times\widehat{G}}{\rm Tr}_{\mathcal{H}_{\pi}\otimes\mathcal{G}}\left((\sigma(x,\pi)-\sigma_{l}(x,\pi))\Gamma^{\mathcal{G}}(x,\pi)\right)d\gamma^{\mathcal{G}}(x,\pi)\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits_{l\rightarrow+\infty}0,

and we deduce the desired convergence.
∎
